FASHIONING BUSINESS:  
UPDATING SUPPORT FOR LOCAL, SMALL-
SCALE INDEPENDENT FASHION DESIGNERS

updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
i 
Keywords 
Independent fashion design, micro-entrepreneurship, local small business,  
online business support, fashpreneur, e-mentoring. 


---

 
ii 
updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
Abstract 
The Australian fashion industry has undergone many changes in recent decades. 
As manufacture has moved almost entirely offshore on the wave of globalisation, small 
local business ownership has increased, unimpeded by current laissez faire 
government politics. Designer businesses are emerging in many locations, including 
cities not known for their fashion, such as Brisbane, thus prompting questions of 
viability and survival. Yet independent fashion design entrepreneurs are determined to 
build resilient businesses. Indeed, small business ownership may be the way forward 
for the majority of fashion designers in the future. Many factors contribute to business 
success or failure, not least of which is the support designers receive during the 
lifecycle of their business. If small-scale business ownership is to provide career 
longevity in the local economy, this study asks:  
How can small-scale independent designers be supported to ensure a 
sustainable income and build resilient businesses? 
Implementing a multi-method, qualitative approach to gathering empirical and 
textual data, this study also draws on my industry knowledge as a practitioner. The 
exegesis synthesises two distinct projects: the first project identified the characteristics 
and increasing diversity of small-scale fashion design entrepreneurship emerging on 
the fringes of the mainstream fashion system; the second project determined the 
support mechanisms available to these entrepreneurs. The findings conclude that 
variations in practice resulting from dissimilarities in motivation, philosophy and 
business orientation, combined with a shifting economy, lead to confusion about 
business practice. The study further reveals that a complex set of requirements is 
needed to support those practices.  


---

 
updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
iii 
Building on these findings, I propose that designers could benefit from a flexible 
online mentoring platform in tandem with community-building capacities, 
contextualised to the particularities of local conditions. Benefitting from the input of 
local designer entrepreneurs and fashion industry experts, the practical outcome of the 
project is a prototyped, initial proof-of-concept platform that provides easily findable, 
credible and relevant information for the various business models emerging in the 
current creative economy. So far unique to the fashion design sector, this platform has 
potential for commercial, policy and industry impact.  
This study makes two contributions to knowledge in fashion scholarship. First, 
it contributes a mapping of contemporary independent fashion design practice in a 
local Australian region, highlighting and identifying the variations and diversity of 
business motivations and business models. Second, it makes a practical contribution 
to fashion business mentoring and support, recognising that business models have 
distinct requirements that must be addressed individually and delivered in a variety of 
formats if ventures are to be effectively supported and maintain resilience.

xi 
Glossary 
Activist: Designers who use their fashion to make socio-political statements that 
question the status quo—for example, statements that reflect feminism, sustainability, 
diversity, gender issues, etc. This can manifest across production, marketing and/or 
distribution. Examples: Vivienne Westwood, Katherine Hamnet, sustainability 
designer Maison Briz Vegas, Muslim designer Anniesa Hasibuan (Elan 2017), Russian 
designers fetishising politics (Peleschuk 2014). 
Bespoke/custom: Individually hand-cut patterns and materials. Pieces are 
assembled through a combination of hand-stitching and machine-sewing. Custom 
clothing includes all production centred on the customer’s individualised fit. 
Business knowledge: Includes knowledge of systems regarding marketing, 
legislation, intellectual property, tax obligations, industrial relations, protection 
(insurances) and financial management. 
Business models: The way in which businesses conduct their activity to gain 
value (usually money). 
Conceptual designer: Centred on the value-add of the brand output and the 
designer’s drive to process trends or personal inspiration, thereby ‘conceptualising’ 
collections. Examples: Ellery, Dion Lee, Gail Sorronda. 
Conventional Business theory: Gain the most amount of profit with the least 
amount of cost in the shortest amount of time. 
Couture: Couture involves handmaking and making through sewing processes 
in constructing garments. It is labour intensive and requires high-quality materials and 
a significant amount of hand work; therefore, couture garments are at the highest price 


---

 
xii 
updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
point, often starting at several thousand dollars. For these reasons, couture is now 
generally only found in small enterprises specialising in evening, gala and bridal wear. 
Couture is distinct from the Haute Couture system, which is overseen by the Chambre 
Syndicale de la Haute Couture in Paris. Membership of the chamber is strictly limited 
and stringent rules apply.  
Creative economy: The system that represents the relationship between 
creativity and its exploitation for economic value (Howkins 2002, viii). 
Design practice: Researching, visualising, drawing, iterating and specifying 
ideas for reproduction. 
Dressmaker: The dressmaker’s process is similar to the couture designer’s 
process. However, the difference is that dressmakers are unlikely to design a sample 
range; their price point is also significantly lower. The client usually brings the 
materials to the dressmaker and makes decisions regarding the garment style, with the 
guidance of the dressmaker. 
Elite/ conceptual designer: Centred on the value-add of the brand output and 
the designer’s drive to process trends or personal inspiration, thereby ‘conceptualising’ 
collections. Examples: Ellery, Dion Lee, Gail Sorronda. 
Etsy: The largest e-commerce website for the sale of hand-crafted artefacts, 
vintage goods and craft supplies. 
Experimental designer: Experimental fashion or artwear presents a designer- 
or artist-centred approach motivated by the designer’s artistic expression. 
The Fashion Calendar: Set by the Council of Fashion Designers of America 
(CFDA 2014) and arranges the dates for fashion weeks consecutively in cities around 
the world, which allows buyers to place orders.  


---

 
updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
xiii 
Fashion designer: Lead aesthetic design, creating new styles for collections. 
These can be separate activities or conducted by a single designer. 
Fashion ecosystem: Includes, but is not limited to, the interaction of participants 
in incubators, industry lobbies, government departments, industry organisations, news 
sites, magazines, trend forecasters, global retailers, video services, private education 
providers and private citizens arranging meet-ups. 
Fashion system: Includes, but is not limited to, incubators, industry lobbies, 
government departments, industry organisations, news sites, magazines, trend 
forecasters, global retailers, video services, private education providers and private 
citizens. 
Fashpreneurs: Entrepreneurs transitioning from another career to start fashion 
businesses. Often professionals from another unrelated and/or non-creative area, 
fashpreneurs have decided on a change of career direction. This may be because they 
are affected by economic changes in the workforce or would like to pursue a more 
creatively satisfying, self-determined career. 
Fast fashion: Trend-driven fashion production prioritising speed-to-market and 
rapid stock turn. Fast-fashion designers rely on a combination of trend data, sales data, 
product analysis and travel for design development ideas, which aligns their role 
closely to that of merchandiser/buyer. 
Fordism: The first half of the twentieth century saw the growth of large 
companies establishing factories with all stages of production under one roof, using 
systems for the division of labour on assembly lines. This enabled the affordable 
production of clothing for mass consumption. 


---

 
xiv 
updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
Handmade: In the fashion context, this refers to the make-through process and 
requires cutting single layers of fabric with hand-held shears (rather than an electric 
cutter), one garment at a time. Some machining is used, but the finishing is done by 
hand using a needle and thread. 
Hipster: Delineates a lifestyle and consumer that prefers objects displaying 
irony, kitsch or nostalgia, demonstrating a taste for the obscure. 
Artisanal/designer/maker: Design is subcultural and centres on handmade or 
artisanal processes, which can include traditional crafting techniques such as 
embroidery, fabric manipulation or block printing and make-through processes. In the 
fashion context, the product is mostly produced locally (if not in-house). 
Indie: Short form of ‘independent’. In this study, the term ‘indie’ refers to a 
subcultural aesthetic in clothing originating from ‘indie’ musicians and is also 
associated today with hipster preferences (see above). 
Make-through: Make-through is a garment assembly technique. Once the 
single-layered pattern pieces are cut, each part is sewn together until the garment is 
complete in one make-through process. This differs from couture or tailoring 
techniques, in which the garment is assembled incrementally after successive fittings, 
or industrial techniques such as mass production. Each garment is likely to be cut 
individually with shears and made-through by a single maker at a time, unlike an 
automated process. The results are sometimes irregular, but this is the effect sought, 
and helps differentiate the garment from those with other finishes. 
Mass production: Uses systems for the division of labour on assembly lines to 
produce large quantities of garments. Garment parts are cut in layers with rotary cutters 
or automated saws and then bundled. Only single operations are carried out by the 


---

 
updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
xv 
sewing machinist, such as inserting zips. In Australia, the minimum quantity is 50 
garments (or units) per style per colour. Minimum order quantities in China average 
500 units  per style per colour.  
Merchandiser/buyer: Follows trends and analyses sales and spreadsheet data 
to determine the production and purchase of fashion ranges. 
Meta capabilities: Entrepreneurial, emotional and visionary attributes required 
to manage a successful career. 
Microenterprise: Microenterprises are distinguished by size: they have 0‒4 
employees and under $2M turnover (Australian Taxation Office [ATO] 2016a). 
According to the ATO’s interactive diagnostic tool, the main characteristic that 
distinguishes the hobby from a business or microenterprise (hence an entity with tax 
obligations) is the intent to make money (ATO 2016b). 
Mumpreneurs: These individuals combine child-rearing commitments and 
maker activities at home, selling creative output on aggregating websites such as Etsy 
and/or at local weekend markets. 
Neo-liberalism: Describes an underlying rationale for government policy which 
proposes that ‘human well-being can best be advanced by liberating individual 
entrepreneurial freedoms and skills within an institutional framework characterized by 
strong private property rights, free markets, and free trade’ (Harvey 2007, 2). 
Pasture to plate: A commonly understood term borrowed from the marketing 
of gourmet produce at farmers’ markets (The Border Mail 2016) referring to the direct 
connection between producer and consumer (cutting out the ‘middle man’).  
Post-Fordism: Large manufacturing plants were dismantled towards the end of 
the twentieth century, when globalisation offered opportunities for increased profits. 


---

 
xvi 
updating support for local, small-scale independent fashion designers 
Large brands engaged offshore plants, thus avoiding costly local infrastructure and 
benefit from higher profits. In Western economies, this meant that firms increasingly 
demanded flexibility of local labour, segmentation of contracts and outsourcing. It also 
meant that all phases of production were no longer overseen by the brand. Post-
Fordism also involves vertical disintegration, meaning that production, distribution 
and retail are independent. Country Road is still partially integrated, as it has its own 
retail outlets, as is Zara. 
Protean career: Investigated at length by Bridgstock (2011), a protean career 
means working in several jobs at the same time, usually to financially support a 
creative, sole-trader venture. 
Range: A seasonal collection of garments produced for a label.  
Run: The number of items produced of a particular garment style. 
Zine: Short form of magazine or fanzine. Mostly self-published original work, 
reproduced by photocopier, with a small circulation (under 1000 copies).

Chapter 1: Introduction 
1 
Chapter 1:  Introduction 
The purpose of this study is to investigate the opportunities and challenges faced 
by small-scale independent fashion designers in the local creative economy by 
studying their survival and success. The motivation for this work stems from my 
professional experience as a small-scale fashion business owner. Like many others 
aspiring to pursue a creative career, I always wanted to be a fashion designer and own 
my own label. I knew it would be challenging, but did not realise to what extent. 
Further, my business did not follow the ‘usual’ trajectory. I was excited to manage my 
own label, based first in Europe for 14 years, and then in the Australian city of Brisbane 
for 12 years, but continually felt that things could have run more smoothly. I had no 
formal education in either fashion design or business (instead, I had studied graphic 
design and education) and moved into my new venture applying instinct rather than 
formal planning. Although the creative aspect of fashion design posed little concern, I 
could not always solve all of my business problems, and I imagined that other 
designers experienced the same. Perhaps they also believed their businesses to be 
unusual and encountered difficulties maintaining resilience in the face of challenges. 
Like me, these designers may also have weathered obstacles presented by the local 
industry (Kellock 2010). Although I recently exited my business, my motivation to see 
small, creative, independent designer businesses survive has remained. Thus began my 
research into the nature of small fashion businesses.  
The current Australian fashion industry is undergoing various challenges in an 
era of ‘neo-liberal risk’ (Tonkinwise 2017, 29). Designers are finding less employment 
as a result of globalisation, and are thus moving progressively into self-employment. 
However, self-employment rapidly transforms the designer into a small business 


---

 
2 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
owner, with the attendant responsibilities this brings. It is difficult to build a successful 
enterprise without adequate business knowledge and the support of professional local 
infrastructure. As an industry participant for over 25 years, and now as an educator of 
undergraduates and a mentor of small fashion business start-ups, I have identified a 
gap in fundamental business knowledge among independent designers. Few accessible 
avenues of expert support meet that breach. Further, the perception that small centres 
such as Brisbane have little fashion influence adds to some designers’ and their 
observers’ negative perceptions of their chances of survival. Therefore, in this study, 
I investigate the opportunities and challenges faced by local small-scale independent 
designers, examining their ability to maintain a long-term livelihood in a self-
determined career. I also explore the effectiveness of the support mechanisms within 
their business environment, asking: 
In the light of challenges facing the local fashion industry, how can small-
scale independent Australian fashion design entrepreneurs be supported in their 
efforts to build resilient businesses? 
Through qualitative research gathering both textual and empirical data as well 
as design research practice, I illuminate how the changes taking place in the current 
creative economy are affecting local independent fashion design entrepreneurs.  
This thesis is the result of two discrete but connected projects. Project 1 
examines the diverse forms of independent fashion practice by designers located in 
Brisbane through case study methodology. Project 2 determines the various forms of 
support already available to designers in both digital and physical form, and identifies 
additional support needs arising from the diversity of designers’ practices. This 
exegesis does not spell out the individual projects, but rather synthesises the two 
studies. I also undertook design research by creating a prototype solution to the 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
3 
problem that emerged from the research. The various project elements are presented 
diagrammatically on page xxi of the front matter.  
I chose Brisbane as the site for this study because it offers a microcosm of local 
practice, within what I term a ‘non-fashion city’: it does not have the networks, 
resources or fashion credibility of the traditional fashion cities of Paris, London, New 
York, Milan and Tokyo (Skov 2011; Rantisi 2010). Fashion credibility is acquired 
through what Bovone (2005) defines as a complex interplay of people, production, 
marketplace, organisations, and, even images, that are called upon in the production 
of fashion, from concept to consumption. Nonetheless, the study finds that, enabled by 
the digital age, Brisbane-based fashion designers are creating their own ecosystem of 
fashion design entrepreneurship, which may serve as a model for small-scale initiatives 
in similar non-fashion centres globally.  
This chapter introduces the study’s research questions, aims, purposes, 
background, context and research design. It describes the significance and scope of the 
research, defines the terms used and explains my contribution to the study as both 
researcher and practitioner. It also outlines the remaining thesis chapters.  
Professional Background and Motivation for the Study 
The impetus for investigating the characteristics and challenges of small-scale 
practice comes from my own experience on the periphery of the mainstream fashion 
industry. My business story tracks the overlapping four-stage plot laid out by Mills 
(2011) in her study of independent fashion design. As Mills (2011, 188) classifies, I 
first began my business start-up in a do-it-yourself (DIY) manner, then engaged in 
collaborative action, then streamlined and diversified my business and finally 
downsized and exited.  


---

 
4 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
After moving from Sydney to Paris to work as a graphic designer at a fashion 
magazine, I established my avant-garde women’s wear label in Paris in 1990. I moved 
to Dresden in 1995, where I ran a boutique and studio, engaging several highly skilled 
employees. Here I furnished a growing elite with ‘designer’ formal wear in a town that 
was renewing after the fall of the Berlin Wall. The business expanded successfully, 
but my personal motivations slowly changed, and I returned to Australia in 2004. Over 
the ensuing 10 years, I established bridal boutiques in Brisbane, Sydney and 
Melbourne. My label thus evolved into a sustainable, medium/high price-point 
venture, supporting myself, my family and a number of qualified employees.  
In the early stages of my entrepreneurial career, issues arose because of my lack 
of knowledge, contacts and information. I then experienced stresses through periods 
of growth, interspersed with economic decline and, later, adjustment in consumer 
sentiment. Throughout, I was concerned that my business was not ‘normal’ and that 
this was part of my problem. My business seemed very erratic in both profitability and 
popularity. I did not understand why my garments and services sold one minute and 
not the next.  If I compared myself to competitors their success seemed regular and 
steadily increasing.  In retrospect this was a superficial understanding. These ups and 
downs I discovered later through research are a perfectly ‘normal’ trajectory for 
business. The problem was that I perceived my business to have problems that were 
unusual. My perception of the situation therefore was problematic. As the years passed, 
I modified my outlook on business ownership and experienced shifts in my personal 
motivations. All these phases presented new challenges and required new solutions, 
but I had no mentor and thus worked in an ad hoc manner, relying on family, friends 
and sometimes industry agents to guide me. Encountering several challenges in 
maintaining the business’ operations in the long term, particularly in Australia, I shared 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
5 
several of a larger, national set of concerns affecting other small businesses, including 
disadvantages of place, economic challenges and non-relevant government policy 
(Australian Bureau of Statistics [ABS] 2016; Johnson 2017). Some government policy 
including taxation and other law is based on calculations derived from large-scale and 
traditional business models and are not adjusted to the issues of small business. 
Fortunately this misalignment is now recognised by policy makers and changes are 
slowly taking place. 
I was well placed to observe significant differences between the conduct of 
small-scale businesses in world fashion centres and second-tier cities (Rantisi and 
Leslie 2006). Working at first in the European Community (EC), I was surrounded by 
fashion professionals, and accessed organised supply chains (see Appendix A: Studio 
experiences in Paris and Dresden for details). Enterprise in the EC is highly regulated 
(no matter the business size), requiring induction to business governance and reports 
to authorities. New business owners are expected to demonstrate understanding of 
fundamental business principles, even prior to commencing trading. Training is 
offered to intending start-ups to acquire the knowledge to operate both lawfully and 
safely. This includes but is not limited to: deciding the entity for the business; setting 
up business registration; tax obligations and tax reporting requirements; consumer 
rights; workplace health and safety; wage awards for employees and business plan 
development. On my return to Australia, I saw that regulation, professionalism, 
consideration of business procedure and industry specific infrastructures were lacking, 
which raised my concerns. 
This study draws on my experiences setting up business in Brisbane, where I 
replayed Mills’ (2011) plot. In the DIY stage of setting up the bridal studio, I 
endeavoured to do as much as I could myself, including patternmaking, keeping 


---

 
6 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
accounts and promoting the range. Support at this stage came from family and some 
colleagues (suppliers and fellow retailers). In the collaborative action stage, I hired 
staff and extended my business contacts to professional contractors such as an 
accountant, lawyer and marketing agent. Professional networks and support became 
increasingly important during this phase. In the streamlining stage, I moved to larger 
premises and opened additional retail outlets interstate, wholesaled to stockists, 
increased the price of my garments and actively collaborated with the press in return 
for discounted editorial placements. I became progressively dependent on professional 
support and networks and vital supply chains. In the final stage, I downsized to a small 
studio, removed myself from working within the operations and ultimately retired from 
the business. These decisions were more personal and required less qualified support 
although, in hindsight, would have benefitted from additional professional planning. 
Exiting a business ‘successfully’ or to the best advantage for all stakeholders requires 
careful planning, for example dissolving the business entity (subject to financial year 
reporting); closing reporting requirements to the ATO; liquidating stock and 
equipment; terminating employment agreements correctly; retrieving worth from the 
business in the form of a sale or succession, and so on. 
Operating my own business, particularly a fashion business, required a large 
amount of knowledge, both tacit and implicit. I sought information and support in 
many areas. In doing so, I was not able to discuss problems with my peers, as this 
presented a conflict of interest in a small and competitive market place. For example, 
colleagues are unlikely to disclose their sales methods, proprietary garment 
construction techniques or specialist suppliers that place them at a competitive 
advantage. There is also a degree of embarrassment among colleagues and peers 
associated with ‘not knowing’ industry-related information, which the study reveals is 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
7 
still a common occurrence among designers. Yet, in contrast to these experiences, 
early findings also evidence a shift away from this paradigm of trade secrecy among  
younger fashion designers and a greater willingness to collaborate. However, sharing 
relevant and useful knowledge still necessitates contacting the right person at the right 
time. This is problematic in a small and localised community of practice such as 
Brisbane, which is isolated from significant fashion stakeholders, likeminded 
practitioners and dedicated professionals. The problem is that independent fashion 
label owners are required to be apprised of and perform many tasks beyond those of 
an employed designer, including business, legal, financial and marketing management. 
I noticed fellow designers struggling through these organisational challenges. Without 
the necessary business training, it appears designers learn through mistakes in a 
sporadic manner, rather than managing their business through informed planning and 
foresight. They are therefore not taken seriously and considered business amateurs 
until they reach a significant financial threshold. Regrettably, this adverse stance from 
others both within and outside the industry towards their business abilities subverts the 
designers’ mindset and can undermine their potential resilience and success. 
Myths, beliefs and attitudes regarding designers and judgements on their ability 
or otherwise to successfully run small businesses are present in a number of sources. 
These include media stories that fuel the public desire for schadenfreude, revelling in 
the notion that creative jobs are not serious and are in fact a luxury or self-indulgence 
(Bridgstock 2014; Jones 2015). Designers realise that a creative business career is no 
luxury, but demanding and complex work—however, the cautionary tale of the 
starving artist persists and continues to permeate creative business psyche, thus 
destabilising any positive approach to sound business management. This study 
challenges the preconceived notion accepted by many designers and perpetuated by 


---

 
8 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
the media. It suggests that, rather than struggling as a solitary creative, with a 
combination of the right support from both peers and experts, mentors and specialist 
knowledge, the designers have a chance of making a viable long-term living. 
Fortunately, the small fashion business landscape is changing, and this study serves to 
uncover some of the new modes of practice, both creative and commercial, undertaken 
by designers as they adapt to the current creative economy, presenting their successes 
and thus intending to dispel the myth that designers are incapable business people. 
The myth tends also to permeate fashion students’ formative education. 
Although formal fashion education is not part of this study, fashion curricula and the 
value judgements of students and their teachers may be part of the problem facing 
independent designers once they embark on launching their own businesses. Design 
courses provide some business content, but the percentage of time dedicated to 
entrepreneurial studies is disproportionate to the amount of time and knowledge 
required of the future independent designer. Some would argue that a fashion business 
requires less than half design time, with a majority of time spent on business operations 
(Caves 2000; Etsy 2012), whereas formal syllabi inverse these ratios. Further, business 
studies are often not contextualised to fashion, which presents an even bigger problem. 
Students then find it difficult to understand the business theories presented in double-
degree courses hosted by business faculties, let alone how to apply those theories 
(Raffo et al. 2000). Again, things are slowly changing in Australia, with increased 
awareness and demand among students for business knowledge contextualised to 
fashion studies (Bill 2012). But teachers may not be confident in understanding or 
explaining the structures of fashion businesses, which simply exacerbates the 
damaging attitude slowly embedding in the mindset of young designers and would-be 
entrepreneurs. These attitudes include a reticence on the part of many fashion design 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
9 
educators to approach the subject of fashion business education with confidence (Bill 
2012; Raffo et al. 2000). External business support can supplement formal education 
once the student has graduated, and is also potentially more effective once the designer 
has embarked on their independent career (Rae 2004; Munro 2017). These 
observations, and my experiences as a fashion educator, have also led me to enquire 
how emerging designers could be supported in their transition from education to 
business ownership. 
Context 
Fashion businesses are exceptionally diverse: in size, target market, product 
offering and rationale. Independent fashion design is distinguished from larger fashion 
industry players in a number of ways. Large-scale fashion companies aim to attract the 
mass market with generic and affordable products, and measure their success through 
financial profit and return on investment (Granger and Sterling 2003). For example, 
Dior, owned by the conglomerate LVMH, is the largest of these firms and can turnover 
up to $40B per annum (Forbes 2017; LVMH 2017). By contrast, rather than measuring 
colossal sales, small-scale independent designers define themselves through different 
and more numerous markers of success. These include aesthetics and originality, 
values, philosophies and/or price point, as well as production limitations, which create 
exclusivity (Parrish 2004). When designers make the transition from education or 
employment to starting their own business, creating, producing and selling their own 
ranges, they are considered independent fashion designers (McRobbie 1998; Malem 
2008). This moniker is also synonymous with fashion business or fashion 
entrepreneurship (Mills 2011). Independent fashion design enterprises vary greatly, 
and many subcategories exist among small-scale independent designers. Most are 
understood as conceptual or elite designers (Craik 2009); some are designer/makers; 


---

 
10 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
and some, whom I prefer to call ‘fashpreneurs’, are transitioning from other careers. 
These subcategories are explained in detail later in the study. Importantly, this research 
is concerned with designers who are unknown (compared with ‘famous fashion 
designers’), do not necessarily work in large fashion capitals and yet maintain a viable 
livelihood. 
The terms ‘entrepreneurship’ and ‘business’ are often used interchangeably. For 
the purposes of this research, I rely on Stevenson and Carrillo’s (2008) view that 
entrepreneurship involves identifying new opportunities. It also requires managing and 
leveraging resources (Ireland, Hitt and Sirmon 2003) while undertaking inherent risk 
(Marks 2012), whereas business ownership is the less visionary process of managing 
projects and profits (Marks 2012). Entrepreneurship is a useful term that encompasses 
the polygonal thinking required by the small-scale business owner. Although this 
thesis focuses on small business ownership, it also encapsulates some of the broader 
skills of entrepreneurship. Further, entrepreneurship or business ownership is a 
predominantly male-dominated area (ABS 2016). Although this study is not concerned 
with gender issues, it is important to acknowledge that fashion, in both its production 
and consumption, is highly gendered. Female practitioners and consumers dominate 
the fashion industry. This study observes an overlap in traditionally gendered roles: 
while most independent fashion design businesses are founded by women, most 
businesses in general are founded and managed by men (ABS 2015). The study points 
to the difficulty some women in small business encounter in asserting themselves and 
assuming the traditionally male role of business proprietor alongside the more 
comfortable female role of designer within the one business (Luckman 2015; Mills 
and Pawson 2006). 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
11 
Fashion designers operate within the creative economy. As opposed to the ‘real’ 
economy (Hesmondhalgh 2002, 2), the creative economy is the system that represents 
the relationship between creativity and its exploitation for economic value (Howkins 
2002, viii). This system has undergone several disruptions since the final decades of 
the twentieth century. This study takes particular interest in four topics within the 
creative economy: the politico-economic trends of post-Fordism and neo-liberalism, 
globalisation, developments in technology and shifts in consumer behaviour. These 
factors have seen organisations systematically dismantle large-scale manufacture and 
employment in Western economies since the 1980s. A consequence of these trends is 
the decline of secure and ongoing jobs in the fashion manufacturing industry, which 
means that fashion designers turn increasingly to self-employment. This shift from 
stable employment to an uncertain and chequered career path may seem unacceptable 
to some, such as McRobbie (1998), one of the first researchers to study independent 
fashion designers in the mid-1990s. However, 20 years on, designers are now 
accustomed to the insecurity of independent fashion practice and barely expect 
government handouts or stable employment (Larner and Molloy 2013). Instead, they 
attempt to relish the advantages of free self-expression and the apparent self-
determination afforded by owning one’s own business (Luckman 2015). Further, 
changes in technology have given self-employed and small-scale entrepreneurs access 
to global communities and international markets that were not available 10 years ago, 
thus enabling their progress (Creative Enterprise Australia [CEA] 2016; Anderson 
2008). But as the field of independent fashion design increases, so too does the array 
of challenges designers face. 


---

 
12 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
Fashion Business Success, Failure and Resilience 
A resilient fashion business can be defined as one that is economically and 
creatively viable and able to meet its financial commitments while providing original 
and sought-after output every season (Stewart 2015). It means that a business can 
adjust to fluctuations in the broader marketplace such as financial crises, technological 
upheavals and shifts in consumer behaviour. It can maintain this status quo indefinitely 
while providing its owners a livelihood and flexibility of work commitments if 
necessary. According to Luckman (2015), a resilient business should be able to fund 
leave allowances for its owners, including time away for childcare (important in the 
predominantly female industry) or illness, and ideally also offer a retirement option. 
Upon the owner’s exit, the business should be traded for positive worth or set up for 
succession.1 According to the principal business consulting organisation Price 
Waterhouse Coopers (PWC 2015), resilience equals success. This conventional 
measure of success is declared in terms of financial gain and increased reputation. For 
the purposes of this study, resilience does not necessarily indicate the typical measure 
of business success (that is, large profits, high fashion credibility and notoriety for the 
owner/designer). Here, it simply means the flow of at least average residual income 
for the independent designer, and their ability to conduct the business at their 
discretion. Hence, resilience in the small-scale business landscape stands for survival 
and success measured using parameters other than those applicable to large-scale 
companies. 
Although ‘there is a high degree of uncertainty as to how to make a success in 
the fashion business’ (Aspers and Skov 2006, 809), businesses can remain resilient if 
                                               
 
1 Business inheritance or handover to family or staff member/s. 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
13 
specific circumstances are present. These include favourable economic conditions, a 
unique selling point, access to abundant and affordable resources and good 
management systems (Andersen et al. 2015). However, many businesses falter or fail, 
mainly through lack of funds, poor management or other contingent factors (Andersen 
et al. 2015). These dynamics are not exclusive to fashion, but are useful for 
investigation in the context of fashion enterprise. The entrepreneur’s motivations, 
degree of creativity, technical skills and readiness for small-scale business ownership 
are primary aspects to consider. Readiness for business ownership can also be viewed 
in terms of mindset and approach or business orientation. Some factors are beyond the 
business owner’s control, but others are well within reach—one of these more 
controllable elements is access to support. In this study, I propose that if independent 
fashion designers had better, more robust, professional and comprehensive support, 
they would stand an improved chance of survival, resilience and success. Support 
enabled by online access, currently referred to as e-mentoring, is a convenient way of 
reaching as many designers as possible. Therefore, online support via e-mentoring is 
a central theme of the study. 
Significance of the Study 
The survival and success of local small businesses is important as Australia 
emerges from the industrialised era and hurtles towards a knowledge-based economy 
that relies on creative output (Cunningham et al. 2016). A knowledge-based economy 
in the context of the fashion industry refers to the original output that can be created 
as well as the knowledge required to produce and reproduce that output in a way that 
can generate economic gain and/or other cultural capital. Not only are small businesses 
a significant part of the economy, employing 40% of the workforce (ABS 2016), but 
creative enterprise produces the cultural capital (Bourdieu 1986) that benefits the 


---

 
14 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
economy and society at large (Hesmondhalgh 2008). Nonetheless, according to many 
observers (Craik 2015; Weller 2008; Walsh 2009; Green 2008), the Australian fashion 
system is ailing. The fashion manufacturing industry that was present in the 1980s has 
dissolved, and the design sector, which was poised to fill the ensuing gap, has not 
reached its potential (Green 2008). Recognising Australia’s inability to compete with 
cheap labour markets offshore, the Australian Government has incrementally retracted 
grants and support since the transition to globalisation in the 1990s (Walsh 2009; ISSI 
2015). The federal government recently acknowledged this lack of support and signed 
a memorandum of understanding in 2016 with the Australian Fashion Chamber (AFC), 
demonstrating its willingness to step in and assist local talent. Moreover, the terms and 
functions of fashion design, fashion manufacture and fashion retail are often confused 
by government bodies. Government still sees the fashion industry in Australia as a 
manufacturing industry, and has done so since the last decades of the twentieth century 
(Weller 2008; Craik 2015). The distinction between industrial and cultural output has 
only recently been realised at a government policy level (Cunningham et al. 2016).  
Green (2008) reports that for Australian fashion to survive, it must consider not 
only commercial but cultural outputs. Indeed, Cunningham et al. (2016, 3), state that 
‘Australia needs an innovative, agile and creative workforce with the skills and 
capabilities to secure its future productivity’. The Culture and Related Industries, 
Industry Reference Committee Industry Skills Forecast and Proposed Schedule of 
Work 2017 identifies a number of key issues and trends facing the arts, culture, 
entertainment and design industry (Australian Industry Skills Committee [AISC] 
2018). PWC (2017) observes that this includes difficult working environments, with 
high levels of unstable work and/or low wage levels in this industry. To address this, 
workers need resilience and commercial skills (PWC 2017b). Meanwhile, various arts 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
15 
and design bodies, including the National Craft Initiative’s Agenda for Australian 
Craft and Design (National Craft Initiative 2016), the Training and Skills 
Commission’s 2016 Sector Profile: Arts and Recreation Services and the Innovation 
and Business Skills Australia Environment Scan 2015 (IBSA 2015), also note the 
importance of embracing various changes in skills needs for designers. Mentorships 
and apprenticeships are identified as requiring support and visibility to flourish and 
help protect artisanal skills, for example. According to these bodies, further skills are 
needed in marketing and branding, writing and publishing, business and negotiation, 
relationship management, collaborative business relationships within and outside of 
the industry and self and project management (AISC 2018). Customer co-creation and 
crowdfunding are increasingly used in creative industries, and practitioners need the 
skills to manage these new opportunities in order to take advantage of them (AISC 
2018). The expectation is that the creative industries determine a more successful 
future and, ultimately, a sounder economy. However, this places pressure on creative 
practitioners facing an increasingly turbulent market. Further, it is not clear how the 
designers’ mastery of commercialising creativity is to be achieved. Nonetheless, 
whether desired or not, as Western economies evolve, fostered by current laissez faire 
politico-economic ideologies, small-scale business and, more specifically, 
microenterprise, are a growing phenomenon.  
The growth in small business has led to big challenges, including more 
competition, micro-economies of scale and difficulties in market reach, despite the 
benefits of information communication technology (ICT). Intensified by an 
increasingly challenging consumer, the business owner is under extreme pressure to 
meet demand in shorter timeframes, with greater originality of output and for less 
return. Unsurprisingly, many businesses fail, some very rapidly, while others may fail 


---

 
16 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
mid-term (Ragtrader 2013). This study realises an important role in bridging the 
knowledge gaps that arise while maintaining a viable small-scale business, and 
proposes some methods that may underpin designer success. Additionally, the target 
audience for this project is not only the local independent designer, but a variety of 
potential secondary users. These include national industry bodies that aim to further 
the interests of their members and bolster the continued survival and success of their 
designer members (e.g., the AFC, The New Garde and the Queensland University of 
Technology [QUT] CEA Fashion Accelerator), fashion educators, mentors and others. 
These are roles that I have also undertaken or interacted with through my work in 
industry, and inform the study. 
Although scholarly research on large-scale manufacture abounds, the literature 
available on the small-scale designer in relation to globalisation is scarce (Larner and 
Molloy 2009). In response to calls for more research into situated and process-based 
approaches to the study of small-scale business, such as conducted by Mills (2011), 
this investigation seeks to understand the idiosyncrasies of the industry. Furthermore, 
the issues that Australian small fashion business face can be seen as universal when 
compared to other geographic locations and locational practices. Lessons can be learnt 
from the diverse and unconventional modes of practice undertaken by designers who 
survive relatively unnoticed, in local neighbourhoods, developing incrementally and 
maintaining long-term livelihoods.  
Research Design 
I chose a qualitative approach for the study informed by fashion theory, an 
interdisciplinary area of enquiry comprising cultural studies and sociology. I also rely 
on reflective practice, drawing upon my own professional practice as a designer, as 
this best captures the subjective, lived experiences of designers in general. To 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
17 
synthesise the two projects, I used a combined methodology of case study and design 
research because of its value in identifying categories, constructing and refining theory 
and suggesting complexities for further investigation (Stake 2005). This process of 
research involves collecting rich data, where possible in the participant’s setting, then 
analysing and interpreting the meaning of the data, inductively building general 
themes. 
Reflective Practice 
This study is informed by my lived experience and personal knowledge, both 
tacit and implicit, of fashion design practice in small-scale enterprise. Unlike social 
scientists that observe from the periphery, I view the designer/participants from within 
the group through the lens of my position as a designer/owner, bringing my own biases 
and assumptions to the research. I position myself alongside the designers, industry 
experts and fashion observers that have contributed to the study. In this way, I align 
my research approach with the pragmatist methodology of Wagner et al. (2012), which 
underscores the importance of values, causal relationships, internal and external 
validity and transferability when interpreting results. These values and predispositions 
include my experiences of struggling with uncertainty and constantly searching for a 
‘silver bullet’ solution to a range of business problems. According to Thomas (2011), 
if the researcher is personally knowledgeable about the relevant activities, spaces, 
relationships and contexts, case studies can be embraced intellectually. I can remain 
both subjective and objective, understand styles of entrepreneurship more readily and 
recognise nuances of practice more accurately; the study thereby benefits from a 
reflexive stance.  
The study is concerned with niche small business activities and categories. Each 
term has a specific definition that is important to reading this study. The term ‘small-


---

 
18 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
scale’ is used throughout. Small-scale fashion design business encompasses those 
ventures with a turnover of under AU$2M (ATO 2016b). Specifically, the designers I 
asked to participate in this study all have micro-sized enterprises (MSEs)—that is, 
businesses with 0‒4 employees. Most (although not all) have an annual turnover of 
under $200K. MSEs were chosen for two reasons. First, they are embedded within the 
local geography (Brisbane region); second, MSEs represent the majority of small-scale 
businesses in Australia (ABS, 2012). Because of their size, MSEs lack resources, 
which also means that their designers are more reliant on outside help, thus providing 
fertile ground for investigation. The study continues to use the term ‘small-scale’ 
rather than ‘MSE’ because a business’s nature and size can change rapidly. Therefore, 
although the information in this thesis largely pertains to MSEs, I use the more generic 
term ‘small-scale’ to capture all small business.  
Site of the Study 
I chose the Brisbane region for the study because I have settled here locally, 
which means I share common knowledge with the participating designers. I have also 
run my local and national operations from Brisbane for 12 years. Further, I teach in 
local fashion institutions and have formed professional networks that connect me with 
stakeholders across the industry. The Brisbane region provides a unique fashion 
studies position because, unlike major fashion centres, national or global, it does not 
have fashion authority (Maynard 2010; Buick and King 2015). It is neither ranked in 
the global fashion cities (Global Language Monitor 2017) nor does it attract significant 
fashion press or generate a large number of major brands.  Attention on a city not 
known for fashion requires a discussion of place and the significant elements that make 
that place. I contend that Brisbane is a non-fashion place because it is neither a fashion 
industry city, intended as a centre of mainstream fashion production, nor a hub for 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
19 
small-scale designers, unlike the rising second-tier cities of Barcelona, Antwerp or 
Seoul, for example. In this study, the theory of place introduces an alternative 
viewpoint: one of businesses surviving within a specific regional geography, without 
benefitting from the cachet of larger fashion centres. Brisbane is not unique in its lack 
of ‘fashion authority’. Parallels can also be found in other cities globally.  Themes 
surrounding place are addressed in the literature and contextual reviews as well as the 
discussion. Ultimately, this perspective on place also provides a model for designers 
located in similar urban geographies elsewhere.  
Methods 
Following the structure of the Doctor of Creative Industries (DCI) program, this 
study was conducted as two distinct projects, although this exegesis synthesises both 
projects and their findings. The table below shows the research design encompassing 
the two projects and the analysis for the overall study. Nineteen local fashion industry 
stakeholders formally participated in the study, which engaged reflective practice, case 
study and design research methodology. The instruments for collecting data included 
formal interviews, textual online searches, design thinking workshops and a focus 
group. Interviews were conducted on site in either the designers’ or my own 
studio/workspace. Workshops were held on campus and at the QUT CEA Fashion 
Accelerator. Data were collected over several months within a two-year period. 
Evaluated through discourse analysis and following Mills (2011), who uses a narrative 
style in her research on fashion entrepreneurship, and similar to Downs and Warren 
(2008), participant comments were analysed and used to move the thesis proposal 
forward. Participants were asked open-ended questions about their practice and 
encouraged to tell their entrepreneurial story. They were asked about their approaches 
to enterprise, whether they see their career trajectory as ‘normal’, if this has affected 


---

 
20 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
their entrepreneurial mindset and to what extent they access and value support. They 
were also asked to comment on their potential use of e-mentoring. They understood 
that the study was designed to investigate the survival and success of small-scale 
businesses in the local area.

Figure 1: Research design layout for the study 
Project 1 examined the rise of independent micro-designers operating on the 
margins of the larger fashion system, identifying the opportunities and challenges 
within their businesses. I examined the diversity of practice situated in place 
(Australia, specifically the Brisbane region). Although they comprised only a small

sample, my observations and own experience and knowledge of small scale business 
locally and internationally support that the designers in this study speak for the larger 
population of designers practising in other small cities not known for their fashion 
credibility. Data collection methods included direct observation of points of sale at 
markets, boutiques and online shops as well as desktop research to build a picture of 
practice. The core of Project 1 centred on empirical data collected via in-depth 
interviews for case study methodology (Yin 2013).

Project 2 investigated the business support available to the broader cohort of 
independent fashion designers in Australia, with a focus on web-based resources and 
e-mentoring. I located support offerings by gathering information through textual 
online research. I also conducted interviews with mentors, consultants, experienced 
entrepreneurs and educators. Their comments corroborate the experiences encountered 
by designers in Project 1 and helped build the case for necessary support. Further, 
Project 2 encompassed my professional practice to innovate, prototype and test a new 
support offering. This process included workshops with supervisors and design

---

 
24 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
In fulfilment of the DCI program requirements, I also incorporated a practice 
component into the study. I created an artefact: a draft for a web platform named The 
Fashion Business eMentor. I arrived at the idea of this product as a result of the Project 
1 findings. As a part of Project 2, the artefact served to test the hypothesis that 
designers could be more successful if they had effective access to fashion business 
knowledge. The platform concept aggregates business resources online, dispensing a 
comprehensive amount of information in a more findable manner. The website purpose 
is to assist established and emerging fashion designers in their endeavours to self-
inform, build successful and resilient independent businesses within their specific 
work locations and receive guidance tailored to their current business lifecycle needs. 
Although the majority of the research employed qualitative approaches, creating the 
prototype became a pivotal part of the design research. Koskinen et al. (2011, 5) 
discuss ‘constructive design research’, which refers to ‘design research in which 
construction, be it product, system, space or media take centre place and becomes the 
key means in constructing knowledge’. I therefore engaged in constructive design 
research, entwining design thinking and creative problem-solving, methods familiar to 
me as a designer, to develop the online e-mentoring tool and thereby discover new 
information on the characteristics of local independent fashion practice.  
The study findings ultimately validate the need for personalised and ongoing 
mentoring mechanisms that can support the resilience of local designers. However, the 
findings go beyond proving the hypothesis. In fact, the eMentor alone was not found 
adequate as a sole means of support for fashion business. Various other, more 
intangible elements also count in the independent designer’s toolbox, including 
community, mindset and approaches to enterprise; these are addressed in Chapter 5.

Chapter 1: Introduction 
25 
Exegesis Outline 
This introductory chapter provides the theoretical framework and proposes the 
thesis for the study. It outlines the project context, significance, scope, definitions and 
research design. It also defines the theoretical constructs pertinent to the study and 
outlines the thesis structure.  
Chapter 2 presents a literature review on the evolution of independent and small-
scale fashion entrepreneurship by investigating the production, marketing and 
distribution channels of designers in world cities and smaller locations. Politico-
economic agendas as well as the theory of place and creative clusters are examined to 
demonstrate their impact on business development support for designers, providing 
implications for further investigation in the Australian context.  
Chapter 3 gives a contextual review of independent fashion designers in 
Australia. It establishes the context of independent practice, including vignettes of 
independent small-scale fashion practice in Brisbane. This chapter also contains 
evidence of the support needed by small-scale businesses through empirical data 
gathered via in-depth interviews with business development program facilitators. 
Chapter 4 discusses the search for online support as part of the study design, as 
well as the process of gathering and organising content, best-practice web planning, 
testing, evaluation and recommendations. This practice-led chapter describes the 
design thinking, iterative process of producing the prototype.  
Chapter 5 analyses the study results and critically reflects on design practice, 
including tangible knowledge management, information optimisation, the impact of 
enterprise orientation and my role as a designer/researcher in the process of conducting 
this study and constructing knowledge.  


---

 
26 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
Chapter 6 concludes the study, summing up the effectiveness of the various 
components of the research in terms of their contribution to theory and practice. The 
chapter provides answers to the research question by noting the difficulties 
experienced by the local fashion industry, the adaptation evident in some disruptive 
business models and the potential for designers to build resilience by adopting a 
comprehensive online support platform.  
Conclusion 
Small businesses face uncertain futures in the current environment of shifting 
economies, yet constitute a growing portion of the workforce. While several 
government agendas encourage developing the knowledge industry (original 
intellectual property) and cultural economies, it is not clear how designers are to master 
commercialising their creativity. Without the necessary business training, it appears 
designers are learning through mistakes in an ad hoc manner, rather than managing 
their business with informed planning and foresight. This study also aims to dispel the 
myth that designers are inadequate business people and demonstrate how their survival 
and resilience can be measured with parameters other than those used for large-scale 
companies. 
This chapter has outlined the background, purpose, scope, significance, design 
research, structure and framework of the study for this thesis. The context of place and 
community provides a framework for the study, as Brisbane is a non-fashion capital, 
and its effect on designers’ success is pertinent to designers in similar places. The 
study’s aim is to examine how small-scale independent Australian fashion design 
entrepreneurs may be supported to build resilient businesses. The thesis proposes that 
resilience can be achieved by improving professional online e-mentoring support 
mechanisms. The focus on online e-mentoring is important, as it is a new and 


---

 
Chapter 1: Introduction 
27 
convenient way of offering support and reaching as many designers as possible. The 
findings highlight the type of support needed by small businesses today, and thereby 
further both theory and practice in the field. The thesis now turns to the research 
process, beginning with a literature review in Chapter 2.
