Chapter 2: Literature Review 
29 
Chapter 2:  Literature Review 
This chapter reviews scholarly research on three aspects pertinent to building 
resilient businesses in the MSE business landscape. First, it examines the rise of 
independent fashion design practice against a backdrop of politico-economic agendas. 
Second, it investigates the significance of place in the small fashion business 
environment, focusing on the influence of creative cities, agglomeration of businesses 
and a district’s fashion reputation. Finally, it investigates the literature on approaches 
to enterprise and the lingering tension between the creative and the commercial. It also 
surveys the existing research on fashion support, particularly the emergence of online 
mentoring. Although not the only reasons for the new bravura evident in small-scale 
business, these factors are pivotal in defining the contemporary fashion environment 
and moving towards understanding the details behind fashion business resilience.  
The emergence of the new independent designer in the early twenty-first century 
is driven by several factors, including politico-economic changes, the rise of 
technology, the significance of place and approaches to enterprise. These influences 
mean that designers work through diverse motivations in unfamiliar businesses 
configurations, setting new benchmarks of success. Rather than achieving the largest 
amount of profit in the shortest amount of time, the small-scale entrepreneur is content 
with working locally and planning for the long term through slow and incremental 
growth (McRobbie 2015; Luckman 2015). However, this has not always been the case 
for designers, and many remain inhibited through bygone clichés of what a fashion 
business once was or should be. This chapter now turns to the relevant literature, which 
explicates the rise of the independent fashion designer and elucidates the path that has 
led to the designer‒entrepreneur of today. 


---

 
30 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
THE RISE OF INDEPENDENT FASHION DESIGN PRACTICE 
The Fashion Designer: A Brief History 
Chapter 3:  The following brief account yields insights into how the fashion 
designer’s multifaceted role has shifted over time, from tailor/dressmaker, principally 
preoccupied with making commissions as fitter and patternmaker,  to couturier; from 
créateur to designer, conceptualising, innovating and experimenting; to multi-faceted 
roles such as technical designer, trend forecaster, manager, procurer, marketer and 
retailer.  Although fashion designers fundamentally lead aesthetic design, creating new 
styles for fashion collections, fashion theorist Yuniya Kawamura (2010) explains that 
their role has evolved alongside transformations in politics, economics and the fashion 
industry, from Fordist to post-Fordist economies, through neo-liberal ideologies, 
beyond the creative industries era and into the current knowledge economy. A 
relatively modern occupation, the designer has diversified in skills, function and 
identity. Today’s designer demonstrates an entrepreneurial spirit underscored by a 
penchant for innovation. 
The role of the fashion designer emerged principally from the tailoring trade. 
Breward (2003) recounts that tailors outfitted the higher orders in earlier centuries, 
individually producing bespoke garments. Their establishments provided an 
anonymous service, facilitating client-led interactions. In the mid-nineteenth century, 
the new figure of the couturier emerged with Charles Worth, who, according to 
Breward (2003) reversed the tailor‒patron exchange by imposing design ideas on the 
client. Couturiers initiated custom-designed garments that capitalised on their 
recognisable design signature, soon becoming firmly established as the highest 
authority in dress (Troy 2002) and thus transforming a trade skill into an artistic 
profession. Their creations required extensive handwork, were made of high-quality 
materials and did not benefit from economies of scale; they were therefore 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
31 
unaffordable for the masses. Reputations began to build around identities and thus 
beyond skills, services or products (Ferrero-Regis 2008). 
From the late 1880s, couturiers started to license their original models to cater 
for the growth of the middle class. Breward (2003) explains that strong demand for 
readymade garments in the first half of the twentieth century led to the imitation and 
reproduction of couture designs. In the 1920s, the rise of standardisation and mass 
production signalled the growth of large factories. The division of labour on assembly 
lines and the use of large-scale equipment such as machine cutters enabled the 
affordable assembly of clothing. Encompassing all stages of garment construction in 
one factory, this economic system became known as Fordism. Among other newly 
defined functions, the factory system also called for the distinct activity of design. 
Greenhalgh (2003) explains that from the outset, this semi-professional function 
loosely referred to preparing an initial drawing or study for reproduction. This 
capability elevated the designer’s role within the factory hierarchy.  
After the Second World War, couture remained part of the fashion landscape for 
the elite, thanks to its revival with Dior’s New Look. Yet in a bid to increase waning 
post-war sales, and along with the emergence of younger generations as consumers, 
Kawamura (2010) explains that designer Yves Saint Laurent produced the first Prêt-
à-Porter (ready-to-wear) collection in the early 1960s, thus providing a more 
affordable alternative to couture. This meant that instead of creating single or limited-
edition couture garments, designs were reproduced several times over while 
maintaining significant quality. Yves Saint Laurent thereby heralded the boutique 
luxury brand: high fashion for the elite. The strategy was successful, and the term 
‘créateur’ appeared to define this new role, simultaneously introducing the allure of 
designer celebrity (Kawamura 2010).  


---

 
32 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
The fashion designer’s position developed further in the latter part of the 
twentieth century and was increasingly presented in the media as high profile and 
prominent (McRobbie 1998; Ferrero-Regis 2008). The personality of the celebrity 
designer became closely linked to the brand, and the role acquired an entrepreneurial 
aspect through an increased focus on marketing strategies. The success of marketing a 
brand name (not merely a product) imbued with illusions of aspirational lifestyles like 
Polo Ralph Lauren, Kate Spade, Stella McCartney, and Gucci become more evident 
when conglomerates set out to engulf luxury brands in the 1980s, launching the 
financialisation of fashion (Maynard 2010; Steele 2010). 
The last decades of the twentieth century saw the rapid expansion of 
globalisation and the attraction of cheap, offshore labour markets. Consequently, from 
the 1970s, large plants in the developed world were dismantled and replaced with 
multi-site manufacturing. In Western economies, the disintegration of industry meant 
that firms increasingly demanded flexible local labour, adaptable specialisation, 
contracts segmentation and outsourcing (Gereffi 2016). It also meant that a brand no 
longer oversaw all phases of production. This flattened system, known as post-
Fordism, contrasts with the Fordist mode of production based on vertical integration 
(Storper and Scott 1990). Meanwhile, much work was carried out by designers in the 
wings of mass-production systems. Griffiths and White (2000) define these as 
‘invisible’ designers, whose work shifted between generating ideas, solving visual 
problems, adjusting cut and fit, sourcing details, relying on supply chain systems and 
producing highly consumable merchandise. This role continues today in large-scale 
companies, where designers work closely with other professionals within the industry, 
often in a leadership or decision-making role (Granger 2012). They do not necessarily 
have a public identity, as is the case with luxury brand designers such as Maria Grazia 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
33 
Chiuri for Dior, Pierpaolo Piccioli for Valentino or Demna Gvasalia for high-end trend 
label Vetements. 
In the late 1990s and early 2000s, the acceleration of production led to a 
paradigmatic shift in the fashion industry: the advent of fast fashion. While retailers 
saw its flexibility in design and speed-to-market as key strategies to maintain profits, 
fast fashion also prioritised hyper affordability and rapid stock turn (Tokatli 2008). 
This industry adjustment presented yet another iteration of the designer’s role. 
Designers for fast fashion are valued for their swift identification and response to 
trends. They are part of a collaborative product development team, which includes 
several players with different skills and strengths (Cachon and Swinney 2011). For 
example, the team’s specialist tasks may be separated into trend research, concept 
development, technical specifications, textile design and/or pattern cut and fit (Payne 
2016). This means that production designers become highly specialised in one area, 
but may not be apprised of all the skills across the entire process. In addition, fashion 
design and production roles are decreasing across all large-scale manufacturing, as 
computer-aided design (CAD) increases productivity and reduces the need for human 
resources (Jhanji 2018). This reduction in jobs has led to many designers in Western 
economies seeking alternative work arrangements.  
This short chronological survey over the last century outlines the evolution of 
the fashion designer from the skilled trade of tailor to artistic professional couturier to 
business-savvy créateur to functionary invisible designer and to specialist fast-fashion 
product developer. These roles are part of the broader fashion system, including 
luxury- and trend-driven elite designer brands as well as affordable mass-produced 
apparel.  


---

 
34 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
The development of the independent designer’s role has a separate story and was 
not quite so linear. Independent designer enterprises sit in different market segments 
of high, medium or low price and quality, and offer a variety of products to an often 
niche clientele. As discussed in Chapter 1, the independent designer is not an employee 
of a large fashion firm, and therefore does not fit neatly into any of the usual designer 
roles. On the contrary, the independent designer tends to encompass all of these roles. 
They have embarked on their own small business, producing, marketing and 
disseminating their creations. To build a picture of the practice of the small 
independent designer in the last few decades, this review now turns to the three main 
aspects selected for this literature review that have affected the development of 
independent design, beginning with the effect of macro political and economic trends. 
Neo-liberalism, the Creative Industries and the Rise of Contemporary Fashion 
Entrepreneurship  
The two macro-economic trends of neo-liberalism and globalisation, along with 
the rise of technology, coalesced at the turn of the twenty-first century to significantly 
alter the economy and, subsequently, the fashion industry. These factors have arguably 
accelerated, facilitated and increased the number of independent designers operating 
today. The first of these, neo-liberalism, emerged in the wake of the Thatcher and 
Regan eras, even though the concept was actually introduced several decades earlier. 
Harvey (2007, 2) describes neo-liberalist ideologies as 
an underlying rationale for government policy which proposes that human 
well-being can best be advanced by liberating individual entrepreneurial 
freedoms and skills within an institutional framework characterized by strong 
private property rights, free markets, and free trade. 
This movement resulted in privileging certain kinds of creative activities and 
viewed the use of culture as an economic resource (O’Connor 2015). Recognising the 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
35 
demise of the local manufacturing industry during the 1990s, advisors to the British 
government theorised that the output generated by the so-called creative industries 
(from, for example, the entertainment industries, fashion and the intangible production 
of the knowledge economy) was worthy of recognition and had economic value 
(O’Connor 2015). This led to the attempt to revive the economy via the invention of 
‘cool Britannia’ in the 1990s, drawing attention away from industry and finance and 
towards the creative industries. As a result, the Blair Government established the 
Creative Industries Task Force, which set out to measure the economic contribution of 
entrepreneurial creatives (Flew 2012). Within this ideology, individuals are 
encouraged to take responsibility for their own, preferably entrepreneurial careers. The   
creative industries shift thus promoted the passage of post-industrial economies 
towards the knowledge economy, thereby valuing ideas rather than things. The ability 
to think, conceptualise and create rather than merely manufacture goods (which was 
the basis of the industrial economy) gained importance. In this context, neo-liberalist 
proponents promote the job decline caused by globalisation, cheap labour markets and 
increased use of technology as opportunities for individuals to determine their own 
futures. Although jobs exist within large-scale corporations, such as those of product 
developers, which are still considered creative, designers are turning increasingly to 
self-employment in the Western post-Fordist era (Lewis, Larner and Heron 2008). 
Further, the economic, intrinsic and cultural advantages of having a lively small 
business sector are constantly advocated in this newly creative economy (Flew 2012). 
The emergence of this very flexible workforce is encouraged but, paradoxically, its 
neo-liberalist proponents provide little substantial or practical support. On the one 
hand, the Fordist system guaranteed a job, but limited individual creativity; on the 
other, it created uncertainty, but fuelled innovation by necessity. Independent 


---

 
36 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
designers now make choices based on a system of values and motivations that are more 
complex than simply maintaining a secure job. These complexities are further explored 
in Chapter 3. 
Fashion Design in the Digital Age 
Given that globalisation disrupted the former Fordist system and arguably 
disadvantaged the designer by reducing fixed employment, the adoption of digital 
technologies may have countered some of these challenges and instead given designers 
opportunities. The contemporary small business is distinguished by the use of digital 
communication, which increases market reach (British Fashion Council [BFC] 2015; 
Anderson 2008), and designers’ adaptation to changed consumer sentiment and buying 
behaviour (Voinea and Filip 2011; Crewe 2013). According to Anderson (2008), e-
commerce platforms facilitated by ICT have arguably provided the best competitive 
advantage small-scale businesses have encountered in the digital age. Importantly, the 
mix of disparate and sometimes unconventional processes, such as combining minimal 
financial investment and pre-industrial production methods with digital marketing 
methods, defines the entrepreneur in the current era.  


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
37 
 
 
Figure 4: Anderson 2008, The Long Tail 
What ICT has meant for marketing and distributing fashion is that not only large-
scale corporations, but smaller, niche producers, are accessing markets formerly not 
available to them. Experiences within my own business and observation of others’ 
business activities corroborates this argument.  Many are accessing markets directly 
either through larger platforms like Etsy or Lustr, or their own website and social 
media, resulting in a direct impact on sales. This is what Anderson (2008) refers to as 
the ‘long tail’ of distribution (see Figure 4). Mainstream bestsellers are at the head of 
this, but the niche market tail is now so long that it represents a significant share of the 
economy in its own right (Anderson 2008). ICT has provided lower entry points for 
small-scale entrepreneurs, fewer complications with retailers not accepting late 
delivery, a built-in retail margin and an online customer who pays up front and pays 
freight. These are all aspects that challenged former business models (CEA 2016). 


---

 
38 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
Although ICT has afforded the designer direct customer contact beyond the reach of 
the local community, understanding and keeping up-to-date with rapid technological 
changes and harnessing them as a marketing tool is another challenge.  
Beyond the immediate affordances of ICT, Luckman (2015) notices independent 
designers navigating transactional exchanges and knowledge transfer in an 
environment of constant change caused by digital communication. According to 
Luckman’s (2011) observations, designers demonstrate a willingness and enthusiasm 
to stay abreast of new innovations such as collaborative consumption, the sharing 
economy and social media marketing, which are predominantly facilitated by ICT. 
Luckman (2015) sees aggregating websites—for example, Etsy—as not only 
providing designers with millions of potential customers, but supportive online spaces 
for new entrepreneurs. In addition, agents’ fees, runway costs, marketing and shipping 
charges are substantially reduced, if not eliminated. However, designers do not 
necessarily maintain any documented plans or strategies to harness these new business 
tools, not to mention using instruments for effective measurement, analysis or impact 
on their bottom line (Neale 2017). This means that although the viewpoints of 
Anderson and Luckman concur on the point that the affordances of the digital age have 
arguably facilitated and improved the small-scale entrepreneur potential viability, the 
skills, experience and fundamental business knowledge behind exploiting these tools 
remains largely untouched. Indeed, Larner and Molloy (2009) explain that the 
changing structures of work and the shift away from former paradigms in the Western 
post-Fordist economy could not have been possible without advances in technology, 
globalisation, flexibility and systems de-structuring (Larner and Molloy 2009). 
Contemporary designers have the advantages of digital information and software at 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
39 
their disposal, but are using them intermittently throughout their production, marketing 
and distribution activities. 
Along with the role of support networks, within fashion design communities 
situated in both large fashion capitals and lesser known regions, place provides another 
distinguishing backdrop for the rise of the contemporary designer. I now examine the 
close relationship between fashion and a designer’s location. The following section 
considers what makes, or can potentially make, a creative location for the independent 
designer. 
THE LOCAL INDEPENDENT DESIGNER: 
The Significance of Place and Fashion City Status 
Social geographers Manuel Castell (2011), Saskia Sassen (2013) and Allen Scott 
(2008) outline several concepts that help us understand the shifting economies and 
clusters of work reforming urban geographies since post-Fordist deindustrialisation 
and the cultural production within them. According to Sassen (2013), global cities are 
centres of new ideas and innovation that are generally highly diverse in terms of 
language, culture, religion and ideologies. Scott (2008) believes that contemporary 
economic activity devoted to producing cultural outputs is increasing in these centres, 
and that cultural production became more concentrated in selected localised areas from 
the early 2000s. Scott (2008) claims that generating cultural products leads to 
agglomeration of businesses, giving a city its place-specific identity and facilitating 
innovation among its creative class. Further, according to (Bovone 2005, 359), 
‘Fashion, fashionable goods and services, and the social actors that produce and 
consume them constitute a complex, organic circuit that is transforming the post-
industrial city’. Nonetheless, independent designers often form creative hubs even 
within ‘not-so-global’ (Larner and Molloy 2007, 381) or even non-fashion, cities. 


---

 
40 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
Ultimately, place provides the practitioner with several confluences. These include the 
synergic effects that enhance reputation, inspiration that enriches creativity, 
infrastructure that facilitates production, proximity of related services that reduce costs 
and niche demographics that increase markets.  
Fashion Cities and Non-Fashion Place 
Current scholarly work on the fashion city (Skov 2011; McRobbie 2013) is based 
on an industrial, cultural and urban geographic understanding of place. According to 
this mainstream interpretation, a fashion centre is considered a place where production 
and design, fashion retail and events such as fashion weeks, as well as awards and 
trade fairs, increase a city’s economic productivity. According to Rantisi and Leslie 
(2006) and Skov (2011), Paris, Milan, New York and London have established their 
supremacy as global or tier-one fashion cities because of long-standing traditions of 
expertise, industry and culture. These fashion ‘traditions’ have been established 
relatively recently in some world cities—for example, New York and Milan only 
became fashion cities in the last century. New York emerged as a fashion city in the 
1930s, with the development of the Garment District as part of the wider 
redevelopment of the city’s urban planning. Milan emerged as a global fashion city in 
the early 1970s when several fashion designers, who eventually became 
‘entrepreneur‒designers’ (Segre-Reinach 2006, 124), opted to settle in Milan rather 
than Florence.  
Tradition, culture, identity, dense relationships and communication are also seen 
as fundamental elements in the construction of place (Scott 2000, 3). Elizabeth 
Wilson’s (2003) study of the urban modern city and its relationship to fashion and 
identity explicitly discusses the union of place, the city and fashion. According to 
Wilson (2003), in the 1800s, the modern city and urbanisation provided a backdrop for 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
41 
the display of fashion. This meant that Parisians strolling along grand boulevards or 
through chic and bohemian districts could see and be seen as the new bourgeoisie. This 
emerging visibility transported fashion from the court and salon to the centre of urban 
life. However, there is now a perception that globalisation and digital communication 
have transformed cities yet again, making central districts less authentic and 
traditional. Indeed, Marc Augé (2008, 77‒78) contends that spaces that cannot be 
‘defined as relational, or historical, or concerned with identity’ are non-places. Augé 
(2008) identifies supermodernity, temporality, individuality and the fleeting as 
elements of disintegration of place, yet also recognises that place reconstitutes itself; 
non-places thus reinvent themselves through new reconfigurations of daily practices 
and routines. Following this reasoning, the banal practice of wearing scant clothes in 
an effort to keep cool in Brisbane could be interpreted as a place-making or place-
restructuring activity. I suggest this is an adaptation to climate, which in turn has led 
to an identification of local lifestyle, itself an enduring distinction of place. 
Agglomeration 
Agglomeration of industries is another significant element that can provide 
advantages to designers and draw them to a city. Designers may settle in close 
proximity, choosing small quarters or single streets in distinct neighbourhoods near 
suppliers. Working within tightly knit communities also reduces the effects of long 
distance logistics and regenerates the local economy (Gonzalez-Feliu, Semet and 
Routhier 2014). Urban researchers such as Florida (2006) and Scott (2006) thus 
conclude that concentrations of creative practitioners represent a dynamic element in 
a region’s economic development and growth. However, a study conducted by 
Wenting, Atzema and Frenken (2011) found that fashion designers in Amsterdam did 
not necessarily benefit from the business aspect of agglomeration, but were more 


---

 
42 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
interested in the superior networking possibilities that this arrangement afforded. 
Similarly, Heebels and van Aalst (2010, 361) discovered that creative entrepreneurs in 
Berlin used their environment to ‘reproduce and strengthen their creative reputations’ 
rather than rely on production resources. Additionally, the very buzz of a city also adds 
to the stimulus embraced by designers (Tran 2011). For example, Milan has the newly 
fashionable Ticenese quarter, which, following the regeneration of the Navigli district, 
is where many designers and artisans have established their headquarters (Bovone 
2005, 359). Additionally, according to d’Ovidio (2016), London provides pockets of 
vibrancy and creativity based on interaction with worlds other than fashion. This 
means that creative communities of likeminded peers such as artists, architects and 
musicians, not just fashion designers, create communities of inspiration within certain 
parts of the city. This dynamic is so effective that, as Currid (2006) observes, it is 
creative communities that give New York its competitive advantage rather than 
prowess in finance and management, as many believe: 
New York’s great attraction may be that it is an integrated production and 
distribution system that provides access to high volumes of knowledge, ideas, 
information, skill sets, and greater possibilities for individual success within 
one’s field (Currid 2006, 343). 
Thus, according to these researchers (Currid 2006; Hracs, Jakob and Hauge 
2013; Heebels and van Aalst 2010; Scott 2006; Florida 2006), creatives are attracted 
to particular locations because of the opportunity to share knowledge, aesthetics, 
values and inspiration, factors that in turn enhance their creativity, add value to their 
output and ultimately bolster their city’s reputation.  


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
43 
Fashion Reputation and Local Identity: Paris, New York, Brooklyn and 
Milan 
Reputation and identity can work reciprocally in cultural production. That is, not 
only does the clustering of designers enhance a city’s creative identity, but designers 
can benefit from the synergic effect of their city’s reputation, simply because, for 
example, a garment is ‘made in Paris’ (Craik 2009). The first tier fashion cities of New 
York, London, Milan and Paris have long histories as fashion centres. Although Paris’ 
reputation may have been earned through designers who formerly produced there, 
fashion cities can also imbue the product of an unknown designer with value, attract 
clusters of high-profile brands and in turn entice more shopping visitors (Craik 2013). 
Naturally, according to Skov (2011) and Hagel et al. (2015), the opposite is also 
possible: a city can benefit from the outputs of new and innovative designers. Hagel et 
al. (2015) elaborate on this and suggest that as cities become larger metropolises in the 
future, localised production will add significantly to their sense of identity.  
Second-Tier Fashion Cities 
In contrast to the developments of designer clusters in world fashion cities, Skov 
(2011) reviews the preference of independent designers to remain in their second-tier 
(Rantisi and Leslie 2006) fashion centres, content to work locally and forge a sense of 
place and identity unique to their immediate surrounds. According to Skov (2011, 
138), fashion designers practising in places not known for their fashion credibility, 
such as Iceland, Ireland, Hong Kong and even Australia, have the place-making ability 
to ‘fill a cosmopolitan form with local content, and a heightened sense of the here and 
now’. However, as Rantisi (2011, 261), observes, in addition to requiring production, 
marketing, design and distribution capabilities, an emergent second-tier fashion city 
requires the social connections of a ‘network of relations’ between local fashion 
producers. Rantisi (2011, 262) identifies fashion boutiques and fashion weeks as 


---

 
44 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
critically important ‘intermediaries’ in establishing a sense of fashion identity and 
place in a second-tier fashion city. 
Ideas about fashion cities seem chiefly based on elite or high-profile fashion 
brands, but many local, lesser known designer firms are making the business choice to 
remain small and localised. Larner and Molloy (2013) argue that the success of the 
New Zealand designer fashion industry is underpinned by the designers’ approach to 
remain locally based niche players, but situated at the forefront of international fashion 
trends (Larner and Molloy 2013). The designers are conscious of the minimal fashion 
status of their ‘not-so-global cities’, as Larner, Molloy and Goodrum (2007, 281) put 
it, but believe their output is not hindered as a result. Indeed, this has worked well for 
New Zealand designers, some of whom have survived since their debut on the world 
stage in the late 1990s and now boast sales in the tens of millions, including Karen 
Walker, Trelise Cooper and Zambesi. Newly identified fashion cities with alternative 
offerings are now competing for global market prominence (Skov 2011). Notably, 
factors other than the culture, tradition and identity that Sassen (2013), Scott (2008), 
Castells (2011) and Augé (2008) discuss are prompting second-tier cities to jostle for 
geographic redistribution on the twenty-first century fashion map. There is a sense that 
all cities are now a potential locus for showcasing fashion. 
Applying these ideas of place to small and remote locations becomes 
problematic if evaluated by relating these criteria to sites such as Brisbane, the focus 
of this study. On the one hand, if the notion of place emerges only when and where 
tradition, culture and identity are present, a ‘repository of distinctive cultural traditions 
and conventions’, as Scott describes (2000, 4), then Brisbane could be defined as a 
place. Despite its relatively brief history, Brisbane is not devoid of culture, history and 
identity. However, the question is how much or what kind of culture, history and 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
45 
identity is suitable for endowing the city with a sense of place, or more specifically 
fashion identity. Conversely, if the notion of the fashion city can only be related to the 
presence of a long-term industrial structure, with quality manufacture, artisanship and 
complex production, distribution and retail systems, such as in New York, Paris and 
Milan, then Brisbane cannot be considered a fashion city. Yet Wilson’s concept of the 
modern city as a locus for parading fashion may counter Brisbane’s non-fashion 
stigma. Brisbane’s residents clearly shop for fashion—they dress for and inhabit the 
city daily and they see and exchange visual information about dress. Complex systems 
of retail exist, as well as some small manufacture and supply. Nonetheless, structures 
of commercial fashion exchange can be observed in all cities with a sizable population, 
simply because demographics have attracted fashion retail chains since their 
beginnings in the 1970s. I would argue that commercial retail does not add specifically 
to Brisbane’s fashion traditions, culture and fashion identity. Alternatively, the small 
‘designer’ quarter of Brisbane’s James Street and the settlement of luxury brands in 
the centre give the city some fashion credence, which is discussed in Chapter 3.  
Rantisi (2011) also identifies the need for ‘social ties’ in order for a second-tier 
fashion city to emerge. Moreover, a culture of dress, other than adaptation to the 
subtropical climate or homogenous cliques, is difficult to detect in Brisbane. Circles 
of fashion conscious dressers do exist, such as gallery personalities or theatre 
audiences, but their numbers are too small to support local business. Instead, lifestyle 
fashion such as beach and activewear dominates purchasing choices. It could be argued 
that in line with Augé’s (2008) discussion, the city is indeed building identity 
(subtropical lifestyle), but its fashion distinctiveness remains questionable. I would 
suggest instead that the city is building a strong case for lifestyle identity influenced 
by climate, and that fashion is part of that lifestyle. In Brisbane’s case, fashion is 


---

 
46 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
relegated to consumers styling the commercial, rather than designers exuding a 
reputation of originality, influence and fashion credibility, thus rendering the city in a 
state of fashion ambivalence rather than distinctive fashion place.  
Beyond the effects of Brisbane’s consumer behaviour on the reputation of the 
city’s fashion status, local designers have little influence over fashion, either locally 
or at large. Apart from a few shining lights such as Easton Pearson, Lorna Jane and 
Sass and Bide, they are unable to elevate Brisbane as a fashionable city. It is neither a 
fashion industry city, intended as the centre of mainstream fashion production, nor a 
hub for small-scale designers, as are the second-tier cities of Barcelona, Antwerp or 
Seoul, to name a few examples. Yet Brisbane’s current lack of fashion significance, 
influence and identity, and its non-fashion place status, are later revealed in this study 
as marginal factors in the success and resilience of its local small-scale independent 
designers.  
Examples of this non-fashion dynamic are not limited to remote locations such 
as Brisbane. For example, in contrast to Volonte’s (2012) discussion of design in 
Milan, d’Ovidio (2016) maintains that a geography’s cultural output or reputation does 
not necessarily preclude its ability to inspire designers. D’Ovidio (2016) suggests the 
reason designers do not aspire to work in Milan is a combination of major firms 
dominating the city’s fashion activities and the local government adopting a laissez 
faire approach, and thus not actively promoting or attracting fresh talent (d’Ovido 
2016). Milan does not provide the same creative flair and inspiration that New York 
or London can deliver (d’Ovidio 2016), thus demonstrating that not every city has the 
creative élan that may be expected of it. However, the narrative of the contemporary 
city as a cultural hub in which creative communities in art, music and more come 
together is a mainstay of policy in the wider creative industries. This means that while 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
47 
studies by some urban geographers claim that place and practitioners can influence 
one another, this does not appear to be the case in all cities, let alone Brisbane. This is 
also evidenced in the empirical data collected for the study and discussed further in 
Chapter 5.  
From the brief discussion of fashion centres and second-tier cities, it appears that 
the interaction of the individual designer with their headquarter city’s relative fashion 
reputation is a dichotomous interchange. Neither being a global city with fashion clout 
nor a regional area with valued traditions determines whether a city is a good place for 
creative birth or rebirth. According to Castells (2011), it is instead the social dynamics 
of the economy combined with interrelated activity. The implications of place are 
demonstrated further in Chapter 3, with a deeper discussion of the changes taking place 
in Brisbane, which is neither a first- nor a second-tier city but, I contend, a non-fashion 
city. 
THE INDEPENDENT FASHION ENTREPRENEUR: 
From Career Professional to Precarious Independent to Resilient Entrepreneur 
Alongside the tangible politico-economic backdrop and effects of place explored 
above, contemporary independent fashion design also assumes intangible influences. 
Fashion design carries high value: a value identified by designers as well as society at 
large. This is no surprise, as the role of the fashion designer is portrayed as rich and 
diverse, part of a world of glamour, attracting recognition and, along with the profile 
of many luxury brands, still considered a ‘dream job’ (Gregg 2008, 288). Upon 
launching, independent fashion entrepreneurs imbue themselves with a perception of 
status and worth, self-defining as a ‘label’ (Mills and Pawson 2006b). Sociologist 
Angela McRobbie (1998) observed this self-attribution of value and worth some 20 
years ago. In the intervening years, self-branding and other intrinsic motivations have 


---

 
48 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
risen in significance for designers, since they have turned away from the search for 
secure employment in the fragmenting labour market. For some designers, according 
to McRobbie (1998), the perceived freedom of expression that accompanies a self-
actualising creative career has become a strong motivator. For others, the image of the 
couturier, créateur or celebrity designer, invested with fame and fortune, seems a 
worthwhile pursuit, and an adequate compensation for the difficulty encountered in 
the quest for self-determination (Mills and Pawson 2006b). However, the pursuits of 
creative self-expression and self-endowed image-making have also obscured 
designers’ business knowledge shortcomings. 
In the mid-1990s, McRobbie was one of the first scholars to investigate the 
careers of young and emerging British fashion designers. McRobbie’s (1998) study 
was conducted while globalisation was becoming increasingly pervasive. One aspect 
of her investigation was the designers’ illusion of becoming famous for their work, 
demonstrating the disconnection between their perceptions of fashion business 
ownership and marketplace realities. 
McRobbie’s subjects were graduates emerging from art schools who aspired to 
take on high-profile créateur roles (the forerunners of celebrity designers). She 
discussed these graduates’ disappointed expectations in the lack of positions available 
in fashion houses in Britain, caused by the early effects of globalisation; their attempts 
to gain work experience on the Continent; their return to Britain; and access to a degree 
of government support through the Blair Government’s Creative Industry initiatives. 
The dwindling positions available in the industry, as well as the drive to succeed and 
‘have their own name’ (label), led to the rise of the so-called independent designer at 
the time.  


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
49 
According to McRobbie (1998), after being unable to find work in the industry, 
these designers chose self-employment, setting up their own small businesses. 
However, McRobbie (1998) points out that regardless of the good media exposure they 
may have received for their graduate presentations or any subsequent press (hence 
‘fame’), the designers found micro-entrepreneurship challenging. Unlike the 
opportunities for exposure that exist today via the internet, for these designers, 
marketing opportunities and sales channels were limited. At best, they obtained 
coverage in print magazines or presented to wholesale agents or at trade fairs and 
boutiques. McRobbie highlights a lack of business knowledge education and 
production skills among most of her subjects, exacerbated by limited opportunity for 
distribution, as the major factors in their consequent lack of fame and fortune and, 
often, ultimate failure (McRobbie 1998).  
However, fortified by the rising current of neo-liberal creeds, and taking 
responsibility for their own incomes, McRobbie’s (1998) subjects were not to be 
thwarted by economic challenges. They embarked on what is sometimes referred to as 
a ‘protean career’ (Bridgstock 2011): working in several jobs at the same time. These 
jobs often included freelancing for larger fashion firms, teaching or working in retail. 
McRobbie (1998) and her informants perceived the inability to truly launch their labels 
on the celebrity stage to be something of a failure. And yet, this manifold activity, 
which attempted to assuage precarious incomes, was the beginning of an evolution in 
the nature of work that has become the norm for many practitioners across various 
creative industries today including film producers, musicians and web designers.  
Over a decade after McRobbie’s initial study, Malem (2008) investigated the 
precarious income of independent designers, their recognition of the problem and their 
survival strategies. Her observations are not as dire as those of McRobbie (1998), as 


---

 
50 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
she identifies improved adaptation on the part of the independent designer. This was 
demonstrated by increased interpretation of the expanding socio-political philosophies 
and a pre-emptive move towards survival within the commercialised creative 
economy. She notes that the designers were leveraging the internet, using business and 
marketing techniques rather than relying solely on their design skills, as was the case 
with McRobbie’s participants; this improved their chances for survival but, as they 
conceded, they were still failing their expectations by not achieving the notoriety and 
income they believed was an indicator of success.  
McRobbie’s more recent (2015) study of fashion designers in Berlin paints a 
much different picture than that rendered 20 years ago. Designers in the EC and today’s 
Britain cannot expect government support to start up their own businesses, as was the 
case at the beginning of the CITF era. McRobbie describes these more self-reliant 
designers in Berlin as ‘neo-artisanal’, recounting their involvement in humble 
neighbourhood initiatives: sharing, collaborating with and educating others 
(McRobbie 2015, 118). There is little evidence of these designers chasing the fame-
and-fortune/celebrity-designer status desired in earlier decades. Sometimes they 
demonstrate just the opposite in their more inward focus within their creative clusters. 
Rather, these designers seek a modest existence, content with producing garments 
locally and anticipating staying in the role long term through slow and incremental 
growth. Their ambitions are limited, and they are happy to make a living doing what 
they ‘love’ (McRobbie 2015). However, although similar designers in a different study 
by Heebels and van Aalst (2010), have settled into an anonymous and low-risk 
position, turnover is still difficult to maintain, meaning that the need for additional 
income streams persists; this demonstrates what has become the norm for many 
designers. Nonetheless, McRobbie (2007, 2015) argues that fashion design provides a 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
51 
pathway for financial independence and that in an economy where job security is a 
thing of the past, many designers find self-employment more attractive than traditional 
alternatives. Despite the blurring boundaries between self-employment and 
entrepreneurship, designers seem content with the compensation of self-determining 
careers and identifying themselves as a business or brand (Mills and Pawson 2006b).  
Addressing the recent phenomenon of designer/makers emerging on the margins 
of the fashion system, Luckman’s (2015) research on crafting practitioners in Adelaide 
also demonstrates how the motivations and practices behind these business models 
differ from those of earlier independent designers. Rather than expecting regular 
income, designer/makers are motivated by factors ranging from ethical philosophies 
and making do to the sheer joy of working with their hands. Many independent fashion 
designers produce garments individually rather than through automated mass-
production methods, because they do not have the factory system, manpower or 
equipment to engage in such methods (Scott 2006). Their economic constraints are 
considerable, and with comparatively high local labour costs (Markusen et al. 2008), 
they are adapting with low funds and disruptive methods, combining handmade 
techniques and digital technologies. For example, CAD has become so affordable that 
some patternmaking work can be outsourced to tech-based companies. These services 
can create sets of graded patterns within an hour, which could take a full day if 
performed by hand. Other small-scale production methods include producing short 
runs (under 50 units), using shared work spaces and adopting refurbished or 
rudimentary equipment that may be mechanical rather than digital. Adapting these 
reconfigured and unconventional production methods requires considerable know-
how and collaborative support, which designers are seeking out on an as-needed basis. 


---

 
52 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
Although not commercially efficient, these adaptive production methods also 
align with the slow philosophy and sustainable ethics correspondingly embraced by 
the market, thus converting a challenge into an opportunity (Johansson 2010; Gwilt 
2011). While this study does not focus on sustainable fashion production, the small 
entities that are its subject do represent a groundswell of designers working within the 
framework of sustainable practice. Alison Gwilt (2012, 85) indicates that sustainable 
fashion production can only be achieved via a limited number of specific methods, 
including systems for the garment lifecycle, ‘fabrication choices, including zero waste 
techniques, empathetic design, reduced logistics, alternative patterns of use including 
reduced laundering, repair and maintenance’. Therefore, the independent designer who 
chooses this production path is philosophically committed to morals and values that 
opportunely align with the new consumer’s desire for transparency and ethical 
practice, providing previously unattainable sales and viability opportunity (Carrigan 
and Attalla 2001; Kozinets and Handelman 2004). However, as Gwilt (2012) posits, 
working within a sustainable production model is complex and requires relatively new 
and specific knowledge, which in turn necessitates explicit skills and support if these 
practices are to be correctly implemented. 
Creative and Commercial Tension and Approaches to Enterprise 
This thesis rests on the theoretical construct of commercialising creativity, which 
engages the exchange of value for original output. Numerous studies (Caro and Basso 
2014; Lauzikas 2013; Malem 2008; McRobbie 2015; Bridgstock 2013; Mills 2011) 
debate the tensions creative practitioners encounter when profiting financially and/or 
even making a living from their endeavours. Creative practitioners are uneasy with the 
interchange of their creative pursuits, attracting income and interacting with 
customers. As Mills (2011) states, they prefer to be free of the distracting burden of 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
53 
business obligations. Creatives’ apprehension of commercial pursuits is not debated in 
this thesis, but rather taken as a point of departure. 
Fine (2002) argues that fashion studies have overlooked the critical relationship 
between production and consumption. Similarly, rather than studying social impact 
and aesthetics, this research takes the less familiar path of studying the business of 
fashion—more specifically, small-scale and niche business. Arguably, a study that 
seeks to understand the interrelation of creativity and the entrepreneurial practice that 
makes production and consumption possible crosses disciplinary boundaries with 
business studies. Indeed, with specific and uncertain conditions emerging in the 
economy disintegration of former ideologies about what a business actually constitutes 
fosters misgivings among practitioners and observers. A one-sided approach to 
viewing fashion business is harmful to the industry and does not propel it forward 
through the uncertainties of the creative economy.  
As already implied, the roles of fashion designers in large-scale manufacture 
differ from those of the small-scale independent designers investigated in this research 
in many ways. Principally, the small-scale independent designer single-handedly takes 
on the many functions that exist in large-scale firms in their position as 
owner/manager, where they must be a ‘jack of all trades’. However, according to 
Cohen and Musson (2000, 33), ‘whenever business is coupled to another type of 
pursuit [such as creative endeavour], conflict is caused by competing priorities and 
ways of thinking’. The problem is that designers generally have such little business 
knowledge and background that they are unaware of the fundamentals of business, 
from simple understandings of the concept of cashflow to broader concepts such as 
business models. Designers are rather more interested in identity and creative activity, 
as discussed above. Analysis of the creative‒commercial tension among designers 


---

 
54 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
prompted Mills (2011) to design an original conceptual framework that emerged from 
conventionally accepted entrepreneurial approaches and delineated three basic 
enterprise orientations: creative enterprise orientation, creative business orientation 
and fashion industry orientation, which are laid out below. 
Table 1: Adapted from Mills 2011, 257: Enterprise orientations

These creative orientations capture many of the elements previously addressed 
in this study, including personal motivations for leading a more creative lifestyle on 
the part of designer/makers to participation in the fashion industry for fashpreneurs. 
As noted in Chapter 1, I have formulated the term ‘fashpreneur’ to encompass founders 
that start fashion-related businesses without formal training, usually mid-career. Mills’ 
(2011, 257) framework offers a means of recognising designers’ various experiences 
and can be used as an apparatus for adapting support more appropriately to designers’ 
needs (see also chapters 3 and 4). Mills (2011) also found that designers often had to 
readjust their business approaches, which leads to tension because it causes them to 
digress from their original rationale for starting a label. This tension that lingers 
between creative, commercial and, indeed, other more customer-oriented activities is 
difficult to resolve, but professional and specialist help may assist. 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
55 
Supporting Independent Fashion Practice 
This scholarly review of independent fashion design has so far pointed to various 
opportunities and challenges arising from new forms of business practice. These 
include, but are not limited to: understanding the effects of politico-economic forces 
on entrepreneurial trends; implementing plans and strategies for harnessing ICT; 
interacting advantageously with place; understanding adaptive and/or sustainable 
production methods; and adjusting to more suitable business approaches. This third 
and final section is industry-oriented and examines the literature on specific support 
mechanisms, which begin to address these factors. Professional business support is 
also of particular relevance to the study of remote or disconnected fashion locations. 
Studies specific to fashion business support by United Kingdom (UK)-based 
Virani and Banks (2014) and Rieple et al. (2015) already provide some insight into 
support mechanisms. But initiatives that foster fashion enterprises in Australia are 
sparsely documented, despite the array of industry body and government websites, 
business guide publications, incubators and co-working initiatives available. 
Therefore, by investigating the opportunities and challenges faced by local 
independent designers and the mechanisms that surround practice, this study’s purpose 
is to uncover extant means of support and systems, including incubators, accelerators, 
coaches, mentors, classes, meet-up and community groups, and fashion workspaces, 
available for small business and find ways of improving their accessibiity. 
The literature is abundant on support for small business entrepreneurs (Hanlon 
2007; Rieple et al. 2015; Virani and Banks 2014). According to Hanlon (2007), support 
is delivered in many ways in both personal and virtual formats, and may be offered 
personally through incubators, consultants, mentors and specialist advisors. It may also 
be presented in self-help guides, books, government initiatives and online formats. 


---

 
56 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
Support mechanisms encompass a wide range of provisions: economic, emotional, 
informational and strategic. Support can be paid or unpaid, tangible or intangible, 
convenience- or value-based (Hanlon 2007). However, although literature on 
entrepreneurial support mechanisms is vast and diverse, little is specific to independent 
fashion design. I therefore rely on generalised scholarship of support mechanisms, 
especially from the information technology industries, which still has value in this 
discussion. This section reviews the two specific support formats of incubators and e-
mentoring. Fashion incubators encompass a variety of support arrangements within a 
socially based format, whereas e-mentoring provides the convenience of online access 
with potentially unlimited reach. 
Incubators 
The social and supportive functions of networks and clusters found in 
communities of practice in the context of place have been formalised into initiatives 
such as incubators. Tamasy (2007) outlines the effectiveness of incubators in general. 
Aligning with the rise of neo-liberal agendas, business incubators have become a 
popular policy instrument in many countries since the 1980s, fostering 
entrepreneurship, innovation and regional development (Organisation for Economic 
Co-operation and Development 1999). However, although incubators are proclaimed 
as a great success, Tamasy (2007) questions their effectiveness in building strong 
businesses, since little research has been conducted on the long-term trajectory of 
entrepreneurs who have participated in incubator or accelerator programs. 
Nonetheless, fashion incubators have increased in popularity and almost become a rite 
of passage for many aspiring fashion entrepreneurs (Virani 2014). 
In their study on UK-based, and specifically London-based, initiatives, Virani 
and Banks (2014) observe that support provided by fashion incubators can be targeted, 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
57 
providing specific knowledge in particular areas. Fashion incubators deliver all-
inclusive support, providing personalised one-on-one assistance from experienced 
experts and peers. These full immersion programs given to participants with specific 
entry requirements are generally conducted in a shared workspace. Participants are 
typically small-scale designers embarking on an early development phase in their 
business lifecycle. Incubator programs often run showcasing opportunities and have 
university links, industry network-building opportunities and connections to 
manufacturers, suppliers and retailers; they also charge a service fee (Virani and Banks 
2014).  
Virani and Banks (2014, 8) compare incubator programs with ‘targeted partial 
business support’ services, which include mentoring, resources, export advice, awards, 
information on ethical production and opportunities for knowledge transfer. However, 
whether a series of workshops, seminars and demonstrations, online intelligence 
reports or case studies and news, all the information in their study is embedded in the 
London fashion scene and therefore has little relevance to designers working in 
Australia, let alone Brisbane or small, remote and non-fashion locations. This further 
validates the need to investigate access to support on behalf of local designers living 
in non-fashion cities. 
E-Mentoring 
 Whereas incubators and targeted business services provide a well-populated 
social setting for designers, mentors present a more intimate one-on-one support 
dynamic for the designer. With the potential to deliver a highly individualised 
program, a mentor is an experienced professional who guides a mentee through various 
practice-related issues or problems in regular face-to-face sessions. Ideally an 
individual who can steer the structured planning of a business and demand ongoing 


---

 
58 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
accountability, the mentor is often a substitute teacher, filling gaps remaining from 
formal education or solving problems that arise in moments of crisis. Meanwhile, 
students and business owners are drawing increasingly on information readily found 
on websites, blogs and YouTube to fill this same gap by immediately solving a 
problem or finding new information.  
The recognition of this trend to self-inform has led to the increase in more 
structured online mentoring sites. Kyrgidou and Petridou (2013) lend insights on the 
current interest in e-mentoring. The use of electronic media in mentoring is of 
significant interest to this research, as it provides access otherwise unavailable in 
distant locations and ultimately informs the practical outcome of this project. 
According to Headlam and Wells (2004), the rise of ICT has presented a flexible 
platform for mentoring, independent of time and geographic location. Further, as 
mentioned in Chapter 1, fashion design is a uniquely gendered occupation with 
predominantly female practitioners, and to their advantage, women are more likely 
than men to seek help with their business problems (Mills and Pawson 2006). 
According to Solomon (2007, 168), entrepreneurship is ‘an ongoing process that 
requires a myriad of talents, skills and knowledge’; Kyrgidou and Petridou (2013, 549) 
claim that the role of ‘entrepreneurial learning appears quite critical with regard to 
women entrepreneurs, particularly at the initial stages of their careers’. Kyrgidou and 
Petridou (2013) have also found that e-mentoring can facilitate a dynamic, two-way 
relationship that benefits both mentor and mentee. According to their study, which was 
based specifically on female users of online mentoring (although not necessarily for 
fashion businesses), the sense of community that arose from this form of mentoring 
led to enhanced self-confidence and an increased tendency to interact with others, 
which their research participants had not not previously experienced in their businesses 


---

 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
59 
prior to online mentoring. Ultimately, Kyrgidou and Petridou (2013) claim the 
community that emerges around electronically mediated knowledge platforms such as 
e-mentors grows business resilience. 
Although the literature on business support mechanisms is plentiful, research on 
fashion business support is less abundant. Research specifically on online fashion 
support is scarce, yet the ubiquity of online sources and learning technologies pre-
empts the necessity for research in this area, and isolated or smaller fashion 
communities are more and more dependent on news from afar delivered via the 
internet. Online connection may not be a substitute for business support, but may bring 
some of the advantages of international and well-networked fashion centres, thus 
providing an alternative addition to the local designer’s support environment. 
Conclusion 
This chapter has investigated three aspects of independent fashion design. It has 
examined the influences on independent fashion designer practice, politico-economic 
changes, the rise of technology and the significance of place. The literature review has 
also analysed the historical context that led to the evolution of the fashion designer’s 
role, which in turn has prompted deviations in practice evident today. While 
technological developments and globalisation have accelerated the growth of mass 
production and fast fashion, the figure of the independent design entrepreneur has 
continued to emerge, despite difficult retail trading conditions. Developments in ICT 
have increased the reach for niche designer products and potentially provided a 
marginally more viable economic framework than that experienced by earlier 
independent designers. However, diversity in practice has also led to a rise in practice-
specific challenges. Designers located in world fashion-centric locations are at an 
advantage, benefitting from established business procedures, regulated supply chains, 


---

 
60 
Chapter 2: Literature Review 
creative networks and retail promotional support via fashion weeks. Access to 
information and support is strongly facilitated in fashion centres, as is business 
infrastructure, which is well demonstrated in the regions of north and central Italy and 
the boroughs of New York, generally serving to buoy small enterprises and offer 
support through established networks. This is not necessarily the case for Australian 
designers, for whom location and the consequent lack of access to concentrated 
professional networks and creative clusters present more challenges than 
opportunities. The use of electronic media in mentoring is of particular interest to this 
research, as it provides a convenient access point for information that may benefit the 
isolated designer. The context of the Australian independent fashion designer and its 
effect on the local industry are discussed further in the next chapter.  
 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
61 
Chapter 4:  Contextual Review 
The literature review in Chapter 2 outlined a brief history of the fashion 
designer’s role and rationalised the emergence of independent design in the current 
creative economy. It also highlighted the importance of the cultural geography of 
fashion cities and emerging interests in e-mentoring. To view the configuration of 
these elements in the local industry, this chapter now turns to investigating 
independent fashion enterprise within the Australian and, more specifically, local 
Brisbane context. This data-driven chapter identifies the ‘target market’ for the study 
by presenting vignettes on the practice of four independent but diverse local designers. 
Interviews with these designers reveal the nuances of their practice and establish the 
specific support needs within the lifecycles of their ventures. Interviews with fashion 
industry experts and mentors are also analysed to establish a more advanced 
perspective of designers’ business development needs. Empirical data from the 
interviews are supported by media, industry and government reports, environmental 
scans (of points of production and sale) and information from industry websites. The 
data gathered are put forward as evidence that more professional and systematised 
support is required to improve the survival, resilience and success of these businesses. 
This contextual review examines the local setting in four sections: the Australian 
fashion industry, local fashion practice, vignettes on variations in practice and current 
business support mechanisms. 
The Australian Fashion Industry 
The fashion industry in Australia can broadly be divided into mainstream (large-
scale) and independent (small-scale) fashion. Large-scale industry (200 employees or 
more) accounts for only five per cent of fashion businesses in Australia (AFC 2014), 


---

 
62 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
and these are usually umbrella corporations that comprise several brands—for 
example, the Sussan Group includes Sportsgirl, Sussan and Suzanne Grae. There are 
nine large-scale manufacturers (above 200 employees) in Australia, none of which are 
located in Queensland (ABS 2015). Within these companies, there may be one or 
several creative teams in which positions exist for fashion designers. These positions 
could be as diverse as trend forecasting or post-production photo imaging and design 
direction. Medium firms (20+ employees) are reduced versions of the larger 
companies, potentially requiring employees to work across several areas of expertise 
(Weller 2007). Their product line may only be marketed under one brand, with several 
collections designed in a season. The brand may also cover several market sectors—
for example, City Beach produces men’s, women’s and children’s streetwear, skate 
and accessories as well as surf and swimwear. Most of the activity conducted locally 
within these firms is knowledge- and design-related, with manufacture largely sent 
offshore (Weller 2007). The small-scale fashion business community in Australia, and 
the topic of this study, comprises MSEs, mostly run by single owners working with 
small teams of 0‒4 employees. IBISWorld (2017) notes the existence of circa 12,000 
small businesses currently in Australia. Of these, around 500 are located in 
Queensland. (see Appendix P: Australian Textile Clothing and Footwear (TCF) 
Business Statistics Chart). 
Much as globalisation has caused significant disruptions in the restructuring of 
large corporations worldwide, similar changes affecting fashion businesses of all sizes 
have occurred in Australia. Since the 1980s, fashion manufacturing has drastically 
declined as a result of factors such as reduced textile and garment manufacturing 
industry protection through the Australia-China Free Trade Agreement, among other 
trade protection reductions (Craik 2015; Green 2008; Kellock 2010; Weller and 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
63 
Webber 2001). Currently, increased international competition, online shopping 
penetration, climatic variations, the weak Australian dollar, extreme distances between 
major cities and small population also add to the industry’s changes and challenges 
(Kellock 2010; Craik 2015). The move by large companies to offshore production has 
also led to the loss of training placements and a diminished high-skills base, 
particularly in the specialist expertise required for niche production and design (Walsh 
2009a). According to Weller (2013), only a small number of niche products are still 
manufactured onshore with a fractional market share. Textile, Clothing and Footwear 
(TCF) manufacturing employment has drastically shrunk from about 115,700 in the 
1980s to around 40,000 in 2017, with a mean income of $28,290 for garment workers 
(Craik 2015; IBISWorld 2017). Fast-fashion companies such as Cotton On Group, Fast 
Fashion Future and Next Athleisure (Johnson 2017) are enjoying exponential growth, 
while others, such as Pacific Brands, have downsized their local operations, pivoting 
from manufacturing to sourcing (Weller 2007), and have since been sold to United 
States retailer Hanes. Other iconic Australian brands such as County Road and RM 
Williams have also passed into either partial or complete foreign ownership (Ragtrader 
2013).  
By contrast, fashion retailing is growing annually, employing over 80,000 
Australians in both large and independent retailers (Johnson 2017). Other fashion-
related jobs, such as those in market and consumer research services, public relations, 
business planning, benchmarking, design technicians, computing, logistics and 
advanced retail management systems, are also growing, which points to the shift that 
has been occurring in the fashion workforce (Weller 2007). Fashion careers of the 
future include 3D print engineering, textile research and personal styling (Chitrakon 
2016). Consequently, although some designers remain in product development jobs, 


---

 
64 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
many, both emerging and experienced, are either finding work in newly emerging 
fashion-related positions or have started their own small business, building individual 
expertise and knowledge (Breen Burns 2016; Lawson 2006; Rieple et al. 2015).  
Until recently, the conventional career trajectory for an independent designer in 
Australia was to attend tertiary education and achieve a diploma or degree in fashion 
design.3 After graduation, the designer would either find a job in industry, working for 
a large-scale corporation as part of a design team, or launch his or her own label. Those 
that find themselves in a team are not necessarily familiar with all the requirements of 
running a business; those that launch their brand immediately are unlikely to have 
much business or background education, and plunge into their ventures using DIY 
management methods (Buckley 2016), with commercial expectations sometimes built 
on unfounded myths (Gerber and Daud 1995). Although financial and educational 
barriers to business entry are low, there is no guarantee that starting a label will bring 
the desired fame and fortune of celebrity designers discussed in Chapter 2.  
This study does not eliminate fashion entrepreneurs on the grounds of their 
various backgrounds. Rather, its aim is to identify variations in practice that have 
arisen as a result of unconventional routes to independent design practice. 
Local Fashion Practice: Brisbane 
As discussed in the literature review, the cultural geography of fashion cities is 
important in terms of understanding Brisbane’s position in the fashion industry 
landscape. Running a fashion business presents various obstacles, but running it in a 
non-fashion city brings further challenges. The discussion of fashion cities in Chapter 
                                               
 
3 More recently, some fashion institutions are offering distinct courses in fashion business, marketing, 
merchandising and/or technology. 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
65 
2 highlights that several elements factor into establishing a long-standing garment 
industry, and may change according to various social, cultural and economic shifts. 
Rantisi (2011) contends that these factors include production, marketing, distribution 
networks, internal networks within fashion locally, designers supporting one another, 
the local aesthetic and sense of place, intermediaries such as boutiques and fashion 
weeks, designer markets and so on. Therefore, the material production networks that 
enable a designer to actually make a garment locally are important elements upholding 
a local fashion industry. Retail channels, suppliers and local connections with other 
fashion producers are also vital. However, in Brisbane, the independent designer faces 
three main issues. First, the city has little fashion credibility among national or global 
fashion authorities (Buick and King 2015); second, the majority of its clientele is 
homogenous in style preferences (Fashion Weekly 2016) and, finally, there is little 
industry infrastructure for fashion production. These three main issues are discussed 
below in detail. 
Brisbane and Fashion Credibility 
Brisbane, the subtropical capital of Queensland, was settled on the Brisbane 
River in 1823, and not considered a city until 1924. In its short history, several socio-
economic ebbs and flows have occurred and continue to take place. While immigrants 
manned the city’s factories in the second half of the twentieth century, drapers lined 
the main (Queen) street offering mixed textile services. As a distant (from other 
capitals) city, Brisbane had an overall dull reputation for fashion. But today, urban 
policy and population growth is changing both its architectural landscape and cultural 
activity. The growing population increasingly embraces cultural activities, including 
fashion design, particularly fashion presented in open-air street markets. The city is 
acquiring a youthful sheen, to the point where it is impishly nicknamed ‘BrisVegas’. 


---

 
66 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
This demonstrates the two-way function of place: while isolation presents challenges 
to production, marketing and distribution, the designers’ output brings advantages to 
the area by developing a sense of visual identity, thus facilitating a new appeal for the 
region itself (Skov 2011); this in turn benefits the designers.  
Brisbane’s fashion ecosystem contrasts with those of Sydney and Melbourne, as 
it ‘falls outside the dominant centres of fashion’ (Buick and King 2015, 17). According 
to Skov (2011), a city cannot be considered a fashion city if, as is the case with 
Brisbane, it does not attract international (trade) buyers, or appear on the international 
Fashion Week calendar. Fashion credibility is achieved through originality of design 
and the endorsement of fashion authorities such as magazines (Skov 2011) and, in the 
digital age, bloggers. This then gives consumers a strong incentive to trust in a product 
and make a purchase (Hracs, Jakob and Hauge 2013). Local designers are thus faced 
with a marketing challenge: the consumer perceives the designers’ output as lacking 
fashion credentials based on the sheer provenance of an item of clothing.  
For designers, one answer to this marketing conundrum is to create a point of 
difference originating from a position of authenticity and a unique sense of place. 
Inspired by the light and natural surrounds of its tropical climate, the creativity of 
Brisbane designers contrasts with those from other geographical regions within 
Australia (Buick and King 2016). Similar to the mid-century New York designers 
creating garments for their sporty clientele, many local designers in Brisbane make 
garments that reflect the activities and preferences of their local customers—these can 
be worn to music festivals, the beach, fitness classes or resorts. Spell Designs (based 
in Byron Bay, and now nationally and internationally recognised) embrace the colour, 
light and slightly bohemian flavour of the relaxed local lifestyle (a vestige from the 
hippy era centred in nearby Nimbin in the 1970s). In turn, these attributes provide a 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
67 
point of difference compared with products from other cities, which is evidenced by 
the uptake of street shots of local designer styles by trend forecaster WGSN. Other 
designer/artists, such as Leecee Carmichael, pursue playful self-expression, creating 
products that reflect her Indigenous culture and the natural environment of Stradbroke 
Island, of which she is a traditional owner. Others still, such as Alice Nightingale, Gail 
Sorronda and Maison Briz Vegas, compete on a more philosophical or conceptual 
plane; these designers may face greater competition, as their frames of reference—for 
example, art or mid-century suburban culture—are more global. According to Skov 
(2011), regardless of their non-fashion status, small cities present an environment in 
which the quirky and unusual can flourish, detached from the scrutiny of mainstream 
observers. Local designers may thus feel somewhat freer in their creative expression. 
The Local Clientele 
Contrasting the Milanese local council’s more reticent approaches that d’Ovidio 
(2014) describes and that are discussed in Chapter 2, the Brisbane City Council’s 
(BCC) Creative Brisbane Creative Economy 2013‒22 policy aims to strengthen the 
city’s ‘liveability as a vibrant creative hub, and to ensure Brisbane will be the premier 
location for talented people to live, work and play’ (BCC 2013, 1). This manifests in 
extra funding, stipends, incubators and shared spaces such as the River City Labs and 
the Innovation and Creative Sparks Awards. Unlike Milan, or any world city already 
instilled with cultural capital, Brisbane has struggled to shrug off its parochial image. 
Cultural initiatives by both state and local governments, supplemented by local 
industry and universities, are slowly lifting this impression through their investment in 
cultural activities. Some of these undertakings, including the popular art and fashion 
exhibitions Andy Warhol (2007‒08), the Valentino Retrospective (2010), Easton 
Pearson (2009), Hats: An Anthology by Stephen Jones (2010) and Future Beauty, 30 


---

 
68 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
Years of Japanese Fashion (2014‒15), as well as 25 years of the internationally 
recognised Asia Pacific Triennial, have surpassed those of larger national capitals. 
Thus, the city’s policy is attracting and inspiring a growing population of young, well-
remunerated adults through cultural initiatives.  
Brisbane’s cultural emergence is not only as recent as the current council’s 
policies. The region came of age after the Second World War, when international 
service personnel and tourism promoted the state’s features of sun and lifestyle. Beach 
culture took hold, and South East Queensland designers started producing some of the 
most venturesome swimwear fashions in the country (Schmidt 2013). But apart from 
seasonal consumption by tourists, the region’s fashions and fashion businesses did not 
move beyond serving the local community. Today this market dynamic is slowly 
changing and, as more high-profile designers originating from the area (such as Sass 
and Bide, Lorna Jane and Easton Pearson) have gained national and international 
prominence during the last 30 years, the city is slowly replacing its fashion dullness 
with a reputation for young designers producing colourful and creative outputs (Trinh 
and Taylor 2017).  
The retail landscape is also gradually gaining sophistication. At the time of 
writing, Brisbane’s high/main street, Queen Street Mall, boasts international luxury 
brands such as Chanel and Louis Vuitton among the usual verticals and fast-fashion 
establishments such as Zara and large-scale manufacturer Country Road. This also 
indicates another complexity in Australian fashion geographies: large-scale 
‘Australian’ brands are being increasingly overtaken by international conglomerates. 
For example, Country Road is now owned by the South African company Woolworths 
Holdings, and the iconic RM William’s brand is 50% owned by the multinational 
LVMH (AFR 2016). This retail mix indicates the nature of the local client base. With 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
69 
a population of fewer than two million in the local government area and the 
predominant customer demographic of health employees and business services (Foster 
2010), it appears there is little demand for original designer fashion (O’Keeffe 2016). 
A few designer shops cling to inner suburbs such as Fortitude Valley, New Farm and 
Paddington. Nonetheless, the designers interviewed for this study realise that retail 
boutiques provide them with fewer options than, for example, grouping together at 
events that interest an alternative consumer.  
Open-air markets such as Finders Keepers4 and designer markets in the so-called 
cultural precinct, the forecourts of the Gallery of Modern Art (GOMA), the 
Queensland Art Gallery and State Library of Queensland, present just such 
opportunities. In 2015, at the start of this project, only two Finders Keepers markets, 
two Bris Style markets and one Designer Market at GOMA took place throughout the 
year. In 2017, this grew to 20 designer markets in November alone. Rising numbers of 
pop-up shops in laneways, fashion events, runway shows and exhibitions are also 
providing further retail opportunities. This public liveliness also potentially attracts 
tourism and other visitors to the city (Craik 2013), further building the city’s vibrant 
identity. Although there is not yet a dedicated small-scale designer quarter in Brisbane 
catering to a devoted, fashion conscious clientele, independent fashion designers are
