finding alternative and affordable brick-and-mortar spaces to retail their creative 
output. For example, pop-up shops, open and shared studios and work spaces, which 
invite the customer to view works in progress, all supplement the repertoire of 
unconventional and affordable sales outlets for the independent designer. As a result, 
the consumer base is diversifying, demanding an ever richer shopping experience. For 
                                               
 
4 Finders Keepers, now owned by Reid Gift Fairs, is a temporary weekend designer market event run 
twice yearly in Sydney, Melbourne, Brisbane and Adelaide.  


---

 
70 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
example, local commerce in the emerging fashionable area of James Street is reflecting 
global consumer behaviour trends, rejecting ‘cookie cutter’ fashion and instead 
seeking the authentic (Traill-Nash 2016). For this reason, other small initiatives are 
gravitating to this same area, including co-working spaces or gallery/workshops such 
as Artisan gallery/boutique and Metro Arts. Several lifestyle and industrial or furniture 
design shops, graphic design and architectural bureaus, all in related creative 
industries, have also settled here. The shifting retail landscape and visual appeal and 
attraction of creative industries in this part of the city has thus commenced, but is not 
yet complete. There are still many gaps in infrastructure and affordability for the 
fashion designer—these are explored below, alongside reasons why Brisbane’s 
cultural development remains in a state of flux.  
Local Infrastructure 
Although the vibrant lifestyle and developing market may present some 
advantages of place, unlike the agglomeration that exists in other fashion centres such 
as New York, Milan or even Melbourne, eager start-ups in smaller areas soon find that 
there is little infrastructure to support their businesses. Suppliers, manufacturers, 
specialist services, fashion bodies, magazines or high-profile designers that add to the 
advantages present in global fashion cities are difficult to find; local designers must 
contend with materials delivered from long distances, which not only incur additional 
time and freight costs, but the inability to interact directly with industry professionals.  
Whereas marketing is facilitated by ICT in the digital age, by reaching a wider 
audience, distribution of  is impacted by logistical challenges. The physical sales and 
dissemination of products for the pre-digital-age entrepreneur was limited to the 
wholesale-to-store model. Granger (2003) describes the conventional fashion business 
procedure of designers producing a sample range presented to buyers via wholesale 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
71 
agents, showings, trade fairs and/or runway shows. Subsequently, stockists place 
orders that require pre-financing by the designers to be delivered in stores to deadline. 
These forms of distribution still exist, but are very costly and time consuming. They 
also depend on seasonal calendars and the physical presence of the parties involved, 
especially in Australia and in particular Brisbane, where the independent boutique, 
once the wholesale customer, now barely exists. This distribution model has changed 
significantly, mostly to the advantage of the small-scale entrepreneur, marginally 
increasing their chances of survival. For example, some small-scale designers do not 
adhere to seasonal calendars or timetabled fashion weeks. Instead, they work at their 
own pace, with whatever materials (and equipment) they have at hand. Whereas 
conceptual designers may still opt for the conventional path of engaging 
agent/wholesalers to expand their reach, others, such as designer/makers and 
fashpreneurs, prefer to maintain a direct relationship with their customers and avoid 
the ‘middleman’. Thus, agents’ fees, runway costs, marketing and shipping charges 
are substantially reduced, if not eliminated. Although the fashion agent system was 
fraught with difficulty and even secrecy, it was able to provide designers some form 
of support or at least ‘inside information’. Much as designers’ business models are 
changing, so too are the support networks surrounding them (Klyver 2007). Designers 
are not settling into clusters of production, as observed in international communities. 
Although in the past Fortitude Valley may have been a small hub for tailoring and 
bespoke production (Ferrero-Regis 2014), the gentrification of the area has increased 
rents and made the location unaffordable to many. Instead, Felton (2010) observes that 
individual designers of every ilk are working in larger home studios, flung across the 
suburbs. Some congregate in the few networks of shared spaces, such as the New 
Garde in Newstead, thus embracing the social aspect of peer-to-peer exchanges 


---

 
72 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
discussed in the literature review. The pockets of manufacture in the outer southern 
suburbs, where there is a strong presence of Asian seamstresses, are under the scrutiny 
of the TCF union and provide more tension than solutions for designers (Nossar et al. 
2015, Sydney Morning Herald 2007). Some designers, such as Rant in a western 
suburb and George Wu south east of the city, use their homes as sales outlets; this is 
not ideal, as the designers cannot rely on passing trade or attracting visitors generated 
by the concentration of other nearby boutiques. Few use the agent/wholesaling 
arrangement, first because this avenue is slowly diminishing because of the demise of 
independent boutiques and second because the designers prefer not to work with a 
‘middle man’. The intermediary/agent costs additional commission and disconnects 
the designer from the customer. Only a handful of designers, such as Gail Sorronda 
and House of Ezis, occupy their own boutiques. Many instead opt to exhibit side-by-
side at designer markets; many more pin their hopes on e-commerce. Nonetheless, the 
designers tolerate these drawbacks and see the balance provided by Brisbane’s 
reasonable cost of living (compared with unaffordable rents in fashion capitals such as 
London or even second-tier fashion cities such as Sydney), pleasant lifestyles, being 
surrounded by family and friends and experiencing a less frenetic pace and little 
fashion calendar pressure. They also accept the absence of high-level professional 
organisations, and are slowly forming networked groups to assist one another. This is 
evident in their growing participation in fashion events such as Brisbane Fashion 
Month and networking opportunities such as the Design to Business meet-up (meet-
up.com 2016) group or co-working opportunities at the Little Tokyo Two (LT2 2018) 
space. However, these peer-driven affiliations are still very loose and do not constitute 
structured, professional business support. To elucidate the diversity of practice and 
consequent need for specialist support, I examined some of the practices operating in 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
73 
Brisbane for this study. Below, I present some of the business models, both 
conventional and marginal, uncovered in Project 1, and discuss their specific needs for 
support. 
Vignettes of Independent Design Practice 
Modes of independent fashion business practices occupy the entire industry 
spectrum, from established businesses at the centre of the industry to unconventional 
newcomers at its margins. Bourdieu (1993) uses the spatial metaphor of players 
situated throughout the sports field to describe the French fashion industry in the 
1960s. He employs field theory to explain how mainstream and conservative players 
at the centre compete with less conventional players on the periphery who want to 
reach the centre of the fashion field. In Brisbane’s case, the categories observed locally 
include conceptual trend-driven labels and custom clothing, situated at the 
conventional centre, and experimental fashion artists and design/makers on the fringes. 
Understanding the position of designers in the fashion field in this way explains the 
dynamics that lead to fashion change from the designer’s point of view. As with 
Bourdieu’s ‘sub-fields’ of cultural production (Bourdieu 1993, 53), further 
subcategories of local practice are identified in this study, and comprise specialised 
output for niche markets. For clarity, see the mapping developed for the study 
(Appendix B: Mapping of local Brisbane independent fashion design business 
models), which classifies categories and subcategories for some of the local small-to-
micro-fashion business. Keeping in mind Bourdieu’s spatial metaphor of field and 
subfield, the research for this study involved conducting a contextual scan of the 
designer/owners’ various approaches to enterprise: their aesthetics and philosophies, 
their education and training, their use of marketing and communication technology, 
their production processes and their workspaces. It also covered their business models 


---

 
74 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
and sales channels, anticipated length in business, challenges faced and support 
required. During interviews, industry observers and experts also confirmed the 
emergence of unconventional and adaptive business practice. They pointed to a 
struggle among designers to find relevant information, and the need for a streamlined 
support mechanism. The following section outlines four business models by presenting 
vignettes of practice for designer/makers Alice Nightingale and emerging designer 
Vorfreude the Label; conceptual designer Gail Sorronda; custom clothing, my own 
practice; and fashpreneur Meta Design Co. The experiences of other local designers 
are included where appropriate to supplement the data. 
Designer/Makers 
Designer/makers belong to a subgroup of fashion practitioners that values 
handmade or artisanal processes. These can include traditional crafting techniques 
such as embroidery, fabric manipulation or block printing and other handwork 
processes. The results are sometimes irregular, but this is the effect sought by both 
designer and consumer, and helps differentiate their products. Designer/makers rely 
substantially on in-house make-through processes rather than out-of-house assembly.  
Luckman (2015) explains the cultural trend known as the ‘maker movement’ as 
a philosophy or preference for creative work and lifestyle. The maker movement can 
also be seen as a peculiarly current phenomenon—while it recalls a pre-industrial era 
by facilitating the exchange of ideas and a sense of community (Howard et al. 2014), 
it nonetheless satisfies the demand for affordable access to industrial tools and shared 
workspaces. It could also be seen as a response to overconsumption and disposable 
fast fashion. Dougherty (2011) connects this DIY culture with the ability to innovate 
and manufacture at home. Designer/makers depart from other fashion designers’ 
aesthetics through their visual references to hipster, retro, vintage, art and Nordic 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
75 
motifs, which, according to Oakes (2009), tend to be perennial and not as fashion-
forward as conceptual designer labels. Instances of Brisbane labels that fall into this 
category include Alice Nightingale, Vorfreude the Label, Miranda Murphy, Kylie 
Watts, Kym Textor, Stitch and Stone and Zulipa.5  
These designers’ garments are generally sold at a lower price point than those of 
conceptual designers’ because their brands are less visible, their quantities are smaller, 
their fabrics are not of the same quality and the sales ‘middle man’ is cut out. For 
example, comparing the price point of two business models, the most expensive Alice 
Nightingale (designer/maker) dress sells for $180, whereas the most expensive 
Romance Was Born (RWB) (conceptual designer) currently retails at around $850. I 
have calculated that the RWB price point is ideally what all independent designers 
should be charging to make a viable living (see Appendix C: Costing calculations). 
However, designer/makers generally cannot demand this price point because of the 
average income of their target market, and their customers’ lower market value 
perception of ‘maker’ products compared with those of conceptual designers such as 
RWB.  
Far from the glamour of upmarket precincts that are home to conceptual labels, 
the designer/maker is likely to work and sell in idiosyncratic settings, which digress 
from the usual sales channels and add to their appeal. Consumers are invited to pop-
up shops, shared workspaces and home studios. The consumer’s gaze is directed to 
‘behind the scenes’ glimpses of work-in-progress, providing a sense of authenticity. 
To the consumer of maker goods, authenticity provides a fundamental difference from 
mass-produced items. First seen in the hospitality industry with open-plan kitchens, 
                                               
 
5 These microenterprises are at various stages, from start-up to established. 


---

 
76 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
coffee roasting and on-site brewing (Cope 2014), this form of experiential shopping 
adds cultural value by alluding to nostalgia for pre-industrial methods. The genuinely 
handmade is a key value-add in this customer’s view. Therefore, rather than buying a 
garment from a known city or high-profile brand, the purchase decision is driven by 
impressions of authenticity in seeing a product being made on site by the designer 
(McMahon 2012). Sometimes co-creation and customisation is also part of the 
transaction, again augmenting the shopping experience and strengthening the 
emotional connection to the product and maker (Ott 2012).  
Designer/makers are interested in retail diversity, both through necessity and 
philosophy. Trading can occur in formerly abandoned, redeveloped inner-city spaces, 
such as Bakery and Winn Lanes off Ann Street in Brisbane, and in turn facilitate a 
vibrant rebirth of arts and crafts enterprises in once depressed city centres. The 2011 
‘Renew Newcastle’ initiative (Westbury 2011) and the Melbourne Laneway 
regeneration in 2013 that also captured international attention with its mix of cafes, art 
and culture (Weller 2013) are examples of such retailing innovation. Although often 
un-renovated and unsophisticated, this perceived authenticity provides the very 
attraction by implying a sense of belonging to a counter-commercial culture compared 
with big-box retail. Thus, the use of recycled spaces is symbolic, and aligns with a 
broader ethos, forming part of a sustainable value proposition exhibited by marginal 
designer/makers who are building social and cultural value. 
Case Study Vignette 1: Alice Nightingale 
The following case study exemplifies the unconventional designer/maker 
business model and, more specifically, demonstrates where and why support is needed 
as these businesses take a foothold among conventional business models.  


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
77 
 
Figure 5: Alice Veivers in her home studio. Cover photo Savannah van der Niet. Look what we made, 
Frankie Press 2018 
Business Model Characteristics 
Alice Veivers, the designer behind the Alice Nightingale label, produces in a 
small home studio using basic or refurbished equipment, adds value to her garments 
by creating her own prints or yarn crafts such as knitting and embroidery, embellishes 
her creations with recycled objects and is inspired by mid-century suburban household 
culture. Veivers left school at 16 and gained her Fashion Design and Technology 
Diploma from a private institution. At the time of my interview, she was working from 
the lounge room of her rented suburban Brisbane home in Ashgrove, around 10 
kilometres from the city centre, individually patternmaking, cutting and assembling 


---

 
78 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
each garment. She has her own e-commerce website (Nightingale 2015), has had an 
Etsy shop (Etsy 2016) since 2010 and runs sewing workshops called the Craft Coven 
(Frankie Magazine 2016) from home. Alice Nightingale products are also sold at 
Finders Keepers (Veivers 2016). At the time of the interview, she had 4,583 followers 
on Instagram; she posts photos and videos daily. Alice herself features heavily in the 
posts, often shown working in her studio, and she also shares non-professional product 
images. Alice embodies her own ‘workstyle’ in her media self-portrayals and styling: 
she wears her own clothes and lives and works in a space that is consciously making a 
statement about her philosophies, blurring the boundaries between work, private and 
social life. Alice believes her price point to be fair, pitched between mainstream market 
competitors Gorman and Dangerfield at $160 for jumpsuits and $180 for dresses. 
According to Veivers (2016), the markets provide the main boost to her income and 
the online shops provide steady interim sales of one to two items per week. In line with 
Luckman’s (2015) observations that sellers need additional support to gain visibility, 
Alice’s ‘lucky break’ came in 2011 when she was promoted as a featured Etsy seller 
(alicenightingale 2012). The article came to the attention of fashion magazine Frankie 
editors and Alice gained more publicity in 2012. Since then, she has been steadily 
maintaining her independent income from her clothing line sales. Although her 
business shows resilience and has been established since 2009, she discussed several 
areas for further exploration: 
My next niche might be my old knitting machine. I want to learn more 
techniques. I’m thinking about how you can make your own fabrics (Veivers 
2016). 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
79 
Alice expressed interest in learning more about income calculation, revenue building, 
staff acquisition and benchmarking, services that could all be provided through a 
structured support mechanism.  
Business Opportunities and Challenges 
Alice and her contemporaries are sometimes confronted with the choice of 
scaling up or remaining small. Coupled with limited funds and the consequent 
incapacity to budget forecast, small-scale designers face difficulties with finances, 
revenue and growth planning. Alice recalled: 
My friend Clare Collyer has a ‘lifestyle’ online shop, Zulipa. Seed wanted her 
hand-sewn rope baskets but it was too much to handle. This impacted on her 
creativity because she kept making the same basket. She asked herself what 
to do now (Veivers 2016). 
The designers in this case study do not operate with pre-financing and are reliant on 
growth through turnover and the support of their ‘day jobs’. They are nonetheless 
prepared for ‘financial sacrifice’ until they reach profitability, which may speak of 
their optimism and desire for self-determination, but also suggests self-exploitation 
and financial vulnerability.  
Instead of expanding as rapidly as possible, most small-scale designers opt for 
slow growth by choosing not to use outworker production. This is the case for Alice 
Nightingale, who continues to work unaided. In fact, Alice is quite concerned about 
her obligations in the event of engaging staff or subcontractors, and rightly so. As one 
mentor interviewed reinforced: 
If you don’t do your due diligence and look at what you’re paying for and they 
quote themselves under minimum wage, even though you would think the 
responsibility bears on them, if they have undercut their competitors, they can 


---

 
80 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
actually say, hang on a minute, I know I did this job six months ago but I 
quoted under minimum wage, and they can come back and ask you for more 
money (Mentor 1 2017). 
With seemingly unfair practices such as this as well as an overly complex TCF 
Award system (Fair Work Commission 2017), it is no wonder that independent 
designers are cautious of hiring staff and subcontractors. Alice also fears employing 
staff because of a lack of knowledge and funds to access legal advice. Her tax and 
accountancy obligations also remain limited as long as the business is small. Industry 
Mentor 1 suggested that many designers are simply unsure of their next steps: 
I think that when designers start out they try and tackle that (accountancy 
legals and formalities) by themselves using excel spreadsheets. The idea of 
bumping it up to MYOB or Xero would seem daunting, where a designer can 
get lost in accounting if that’s not what they studied. What they want to do is 
design (Mentor 1 2017). 
The insight provided by this industry mentor demonstrates the complexity of the 
fashion industry, its different levels of operation and the vulnerability of designers 
working within it: 
When it comes to legals I guess you have to trawl through the internet to find 
the right person for you. When I was first looking I had quotes for one 
particular contract, anywhere from $2,000 to $15,000 and knowing what you 
get for those figures and knowing what you are being quoted on is quite 
difficult to decipher (Mentor 1 2017). 
Alice has become aware of these pitfalls and navigated her way through her first years 
of business by taking careful and incremental steps, mostly relying on family and 
friends. Conventional business wisdom describes five to seven stages of business 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
81 
development (Petch 2016), from start-up to exit. Now that Alice has stepped through 
the first phases of growth and establishment, she is poised for expansion. This is not 
an automatic rite of passage, but requires a conscious decision, and Alice was 
searching for professional guidance in the matter at the time of interview.  
Alice relies on her peer network and intermediaries such as market convenors 
for business guidance, in a one-on-one manner. Although established, she still admits 
to trial and error in her business operations, mostly because her style of business is so 
unconventional and organically grown. Nonetheless, Alice stated that she is eager to 
learn anything new that will strengthen her hold in the market and ensure long-term 
livelihood in her microenterprise: 
I went onto the Finders Keepers website but was bad at self-promotion at the 
time. I can’t fault the original organisers. Brooke Johnson is from Byron Bay. 
They have helped and supported me and the other stall holders so much, 
always giving tips and advice (Veivers 2016).  
Although an anomaly exists between the ideals of local production and the 
connectivity provided by ICT, Alice believes that designers can make a place for 
themselves locally, especially in Brisbane because it is smaller than Sydney or 
Melbourne. ‘There is so much competition in Melbourne. Everyone wants to go there 
because they think it is a fashion city’ (Veivers 2016). Alice explained the regional 
differences between the capital cities she visits, and maintained that working as a 
designer in Brisbane has distinct advantages: 
What a surprise they say when they find out I’m from Brisbane. Melbourne 
has a prejudice. They think that Brisbane is a country town, so I avoided 
talking about it when I was in Melbourne at the markets. But I would stick out 


---

 
82 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
as a Brisbane stall. I was the first at the Melbourne markets to stand out with 
bright colours. I seemed ridiculously quirky (Veivers 2016). 
Alice’s experience demonstrates that working in a non-fashion capital can actually be 
advantageous given that there is less competition and more opportunity to differentiate 
an independent, micro-designer's work. In sustaining a business in Brisbane Alice also 
demonstrates the point of difference that can be achieved if the designer remains 
authentic and derives some inspiration from their place of origin. 
Besides larger business decisions, the more practical aspects of sourcing and 
production also pose problems—they remain shrouded in trade secrecy, regardless of 
younger designers’ willingness to share their information. But Alice has found a 
workaround by changing her business model to incorporate agile supply chains, 
networking, thrifting and sharing, which leads to alternative solutions. Circumventing 
the usual supply chain channels has reduced her overheads, and contrary to popular 
perception that small businesses are not viable, Alice recalled: 
The partners of customers that come to the stall, 50‒60 year old men, ask or 
comment that I can’t possibly be making money. And I say, well yes, I am! 
They just still believe in old business ideas (Veivers 2016). 
Alice now claims to have a modest but sustainable income from her business, but also 
adds that other factors accompanying her work add value. As discussed in the literature 
review, these include the lifestyle, freedom of creative expression and the self-esteem 
gained by resolving challenges and owning a business. Alice embodies an ideal 
creative lifestyle that blurs the lines between work and home life, and this authenticity 
also appeals to her fan base, as her customer Alex Lawton (2016) commented in an 
interview.  


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
83 
Alice’s fashion business engages several unconventional methods to navigate 
uncertainty within the current creative economy. She is content to make a viable living, 
which she hopes will fund her ambitions for greater mobility in the future. Continuing 
her break with traditional business modelling, she hopes to be a roving seamstress one 
day, travelling England in a caravan with a sewing machine. Alice’s designer/maker 
perceivably idyllic work and lifestyle is an attraction to many emerging designers, 
including Vorfreude the Label, explored below. 
Case Study Vignette 2: Vorfreude the Label 
Vorfreude the Label was founded in 2015 by Brittany McIlwain after she 
graduated with an Advanced Diploma in Fashion Design and Technology. Unlike 
Alice, Brittany has had little experience in the industry. Although Vorfreude is also a 
designer/maker label, it has been included in the study because it points to some 
assumptions made by graduate or emerging designers about start-up fashion 
businesses. Brittany belongs to the millennial generation, who, according to Charrier 
(2016) and Choi (2003), believe that social media is the new answer to their marketing 
needs. Brittany stated that her perceptions about what a career in fashion constitutes is 
influenced by her generation’s immersion in ICT, and yet admits, ‘my generation is 
more naïve about technology and what they expect from it than you think’ (McIlwain 
2016). Her expectations also reflect many students’ assumptions about the normal 
career trajectory for a fashion designer starting their own business: 
I designed a complete collection at college and I’m now planning my next. I 
thought I could have it financed by a sponsor and then get stockists. I’d like 
to appear in fashion editorials. I haven’t paid for advertising, but I thought I 
would get social media followers. It would be great to sell out every season 
online or in shops (McIlwain 2016).  


---

 
84 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
 
Figure 6: Vorfreude the Label crop top, pink hand-printed linen, from the graduate collection, 2015 
Business Characteristics 
At the time of the interview, Brittany was working a protean career (Bridgstock 
2011) in three occupations: as a design assistant with local fashion label and 
manufacturer Black Milk, sales assistant at fabric store Spotlight and self-employed 
designer/owner at her own label. Brittany began her label with her graduate collection 
and a budget under $1,000. At the time of interview, she had sold a few garments and 
was working on her second collection, including a new set of fabric prints. She designs, 
cuts and assembles daywear with an ‘indie vibe’, as she describes it, for a local market 
of her own generation. Her design aesthetic aligns with that of indie magazines such 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
85 
as Frankie and she says she is passionate about sustainability. In her case, this 
manifests itself in the use of natural fibres (cotton and linen) and organic printing dyes. 
The proliferation of many systems of production, marketing and consumption 
not only cause disruption within the fashion system, but confusion among its 
practitioners. Many, like Brittany, believe they should be following a pre-determined 
path, as laid out in fashion education or media reports. But this is no longer the case. 
As observed by Mentor 1: 
If they’re a new emerging designer, they need a little bit of handholding when 
it comes to the production process. They’ve got their designs down pat, but 
they don’t really know how to take the next step. What I find is that producers 
or makers don’t have the time to nurture those inexperienced designers, so 
they often push them to the back of the queue, which pushes back their 
production, which can be a real stumbling block for them (Mentor 1 2017). 
The mentor further contended that all they want to do is design and ‘put their 
work out there, but they don’t understand all the formalities’ (Mentor 1 2017). 
Business Opportunities and Challenges 
Brittany has placed high hopes on her design career but, at the time of interview, 
was struggling with the current results. However, much as independent designers are 
innovating and adapting to tightened economic circumstances, which have become 
even more evident post-global financial crisis, saving money is not the same as making 
money, and designers are still struggling with some fundamental business 
development issues. Brittany explained: 
I do everything on the garment, the fabric print, the patterns, the sewing. The 
customers are mostly very impressed. I’m surprised at how appreciative they 
are. But sometimes people ask me for the price. They don’t realise how much 


---

 
86 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
time is involved. They are so used to going into a store and don’t even ask 
themselves, what’s behind the making of this? What is their thought process 
when buying it? It’s disheartening and you ask yourself why do it (McIlwain 
2016). 
This emotional reaction is easy to understand, but difficult to console. Brittany’s 
question reveals a fundamental problem independent designers encounter: they can 
never compete with mass-market retail on price, and the public is not accustomed to 
paying the amount a designer should charge. Therefore, Australian independent 
designers must first bring their unique proposition to the public. Second, they require 
a supportive ecosystem of likeminded colleagues, customers and experienced mentors 
to guide them through the challenges posed by this elementary dilemma. Good support 
may help Brittany resolve the myths and misunderstandings surrounding business 
activity and its sporadic and unpredictable nature. Brittany reflected, ‘I thought owning 
your own label was going to be less complicated. Instead, there are a whole lot of other 
factors that affect it that I didn’t even know about, especially when it comes to 
customers’ (McIlwain 2016). This follows Mills’ observations (2011) that many 
designers battle with the notion of readjusting their tasks away from the activities that 
were the original intent of starting their fashion business, such as shifting from design 
to management and customer relations roles. 
Brittany echoed the sentiments of many designers interviewed for the study 
regarding the difficulty of balancing business management and creativity (McIlwain 
2016). As noted by one of the fashion mentors interviewed: 
I actually think there’s the creative side you can have whether or not you have 
studied fashion, but not all of our fashion students have the business side and 
I really wish they did. I feel they’re lacking in that. I find they can design great 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
87 
things and they can come up with a great product but to actually sell it and 
organise all the timeframes is not always easy for them (Mentor 2 2017). 
All ]the designers in the study preferred to spend more time and energy on creative 
tasks and less on business. The more established designers had found methods (and/or 
business partners) to manage the process, but those in start-up phases were more 
resentful of time spent on business and often held negative opinions of their own 
business capabilities. This may be the result of sheer lack of education coupled with 
lack of professional support and the mindset pervading the fashion community that 
business is hard and not within a designer’s aptitudes. 
Yet designers are adapting to the creative economy, and demonstrate business 
sense in many innovative ways. For example, they have embraced principles of the 
sharing economy, which has introduced changes in repurposed workspaces or a return 
to home and the suburbs as sites for creative work (Felton 2013). Suburban homes 
provide space and minimal-to-low costs compared with city locations. However, 
sometimes these adaptations can cause confusion among designers who firmly believe 
that business can only be conducted in one way. Some emerging designers and 
designer/makers are looking for ways to bridge the awkward gap of working from 
apartments and suburban share houses, as Brittany does, to ‘legitimate’ commercial 
spaces in the city. These premises still hold more credibility in the eyes of the designer, 
and are more accessible for customers. Brittany has not yet organised her workspace 
or retail space beyond the confines of her apartment, but is aware of the shared spaces 
options in the city and is actively looking for openings, including collective retail 
opportunities.  
Brittany also takes part in collaborative group shows such as Raw Runways. Yet 
another peer-organised initiative, Raw’s organisers describe the program as ‘an 


---

 
88 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
independent arts organisation for artists by artists’ (Raw Runways 2016), and the 
venture is typical of the current generation of websites and self-help communities that 
provide a marketing/networking platform for young creative entrepreneurs. But the 
events are sporadic and not underpinned by a central fashion support system, thus not 
reaching the potential designers might imagine.  
There is recognition on the part of the established industry experts and mentors 
interviewed that greater support is needed to uphold the industry locally as well as 
nationally. For example, one mentor complained about the uncoordinated sharing 
among local networks in Brisbane, suggesting the necessity of a place to announce 
events important to a community of designers: 
We are more than willing to share all that information. I’m not a closed book 
but we need a more streamlined system of information, just being able to 
communicate fashion information (Mentor 2 2017). 
A more comprehensive, fashion-focused platform may help reach this potential, thus 
furthering the networking, production and marketing efforts of local designers.  
The case of Brittany’s Vorfreude the Label demonstrates an emerging 
designer/maker keen to uphold sustainable practices, but yet to find equilibrium 
between creative and commercial pursuits. Brittany’s efforts and enthusiasm deserve 
the recognition, even if incremental, but this is thwarted by the uncoordinated support 
system she has encountered since graduation.  
Conceptual Designers 
The most visible small-scale design entrepreneur is the elite or conceptual 
designer. Conceptual designer labels are typically founded by one or two individuals 
and rely on substantial branding and persona-centred marketing strategies (Quelch and 
Harding 1996). This form of design practice is particularly attractive to fashion 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
89 
graduates, providing the opportunity for self-determined artistic expression (Ferrero-
Regis 2008). Conceptual fashion design, not unlike couture, is immersed in the label’s 
signature style and the designer’s drive to process trends or personal inspiration, 
thereby ‘conceptualising’ collections. Conceptual Australian designers are usually 
university qualified, contribute cultural capital and invest significant start-up capital 
into their small businesses (australia.gov.au 2016). Examples include Ellery, Camilla, 
Dion Lee and Brisbane-based Gail Sorronda. 
According to the AFC (2014), the ambition of conceptual designers is often to 
scale up and be represented in department stores and boutiques both nationally and 
internationally. The conceptual designers’ price point is mid to high end (from around 
$300 to several thousand dollars for a garment by a label such as Ellery) and presents 
an aspirational fashion-forward image to the clientele. Conceptual designers’ higher 
price point attests not only to their perception of value-add that is increased through 
innovative fashion and creative acclaim (Caro and Basso 2014), but to the costly nature 
of this business model. In contrast to designer/makers, conceptual designers tend 
towards automated, although limited, production through outworkers and cut-make-
trim (CMT) factories, which promise a more professional look and feel in the final 
product, even if runs are short. Apart from significant start-up capital, costs include 
pre-payment of quality materials and production as well as substantial investment in 
marketing. Shop leases are also prohibitive for those who run their own boutiques, so 
this form of business requires considerable business know-how and financial 
management. Industry Mentor 2 observed: 
I think sometimes they need more help in the business side than the fashion 
design side. They need help with finding manufacturers than detecting styles. 


---

 
90 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
Often they’re quite competent in that area, but they just don’t have enough 
business skills (Mentor 2 2017).  
Sales avenues for conceptual designer brands require the corroboration of boutique 
retailers, fashion influencers and their fashion networks (Rantisi 2004). Diversification 
within the business is commonplace and assists in balancing cash flow. This practice 
may include diffusion lines or side projects that showcase the designer’s signature 
aesthetic (e.g., Martin Grant’s uniforms for Qantas, Ellery’s eyewear, Limedrop’s 
undergarments). 
Case Study Vignette 3: Gail Sorronda 
Gail Sorronda is a local independent conceptual designer label founded by 
designer Gail Reid in 2005. Gail’s personal motivations to grow her small business, 
modes of sourcing and production, aesthetics, design, marketing and branding, sales 
channels and retail practices, networking and community support, and customers are 
distinct from those of the other case studies investigated in several ways.  


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
91 
 
Figure 7: Gail Reid in her boutique, James St Brisbane 
 
Business Characteristics 
After several years of self-employment within the European industry, Gail, a 
Bachelor of Fine Arts (Fashion) graduate, returned to her Brisbane home. At the time 
of interview, she was working in her home-based studio on artistic rather than trend-
driven concepts. These ensuing toiles (calico prototypes) are sent to the off-site 
patternmaker to be resolved for production and are subsequently made by outworkers 
under the supervision of a contracted production manager. The designer sees herself 


---

 
92 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
as part of a continuum in the fashion landscape, not distinguished by market segment 
or category. She concedes that she would like to grow the business, but through small 
and conservative steps. Gail’s personal motivations are strongly based in art practice; 
in the interview, she spoke of her background in the arts as a starting point for design 
inspiration and product development: 
Everybody’s process is different, but I think the most important thing is 
knowing your niche and knowing what you can offer that is different to 
everybody else, and sticking to it. I think identity is really important for 
survival. I think a lot of people do try to please others, even doing diffusion 
lines or diversifying too much, or following trends too much can get you into 
trouble (Reid 2016). 
However, she is not seen as an experimental or artwear designer because her work has 
commercial intent. Sourcing and production runs may be limited, but Gail relies on the 
conventional supply chain, often procuring materials from known fashion industry 
sources such as agents, trade fairs and factories, both nationally and internationally.  
Business Opportunities and Challenges 
Regardless of the inspirational processes behind the design and product 
development, the marketing and branding applied by conceptual designers are perhaps 
the most distinguishing factors of this business model. Featured in glamorous fashion 
events such as the Mercedes Benz Fashion Festival and prestige international 
magazines (Vogue Australia, Vogue Italia, Harper’s Bazaar), Gail Sorronda does not 
advertise in magazines (media placement prices are prohibitive for MSEs, at 
sometimes more than $10,000 per page), but several high-fashion publications and 
blogs have featured the label in editorial articles. Gail Reid does not use PR agents. 
Gail’s originality, creativity and international activity has attracted high end media 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
93 
coverage. Gail generates social media coverage herself or with the help of a sales 
assistant in-house. Gail’s husband helps with social media and business management. 
Her mother also assists in the boutique and with events. She alone does the design at 
home and rarely has student interns. She sub-contracts work to patternmakers and a 
production manager for manufacture.   
Gail Reid’s originality and creativity has attracted journalists keen to present 
unique and original ‘local’ work (Barker 2015). As also experienced by Alice 
Nightingale with Frankie, magazine articles boost the label’s visibility, but the 
editorial platforms, and hence the target market, is different. Gail explained: 
Black magazine and other New Zealand magazines have been good 
supporters. I was in Italian Vogue recently and I love being in there because 
they always present it beautifully. They’re somebody you respect and it kind 
of helps to validate what you are, professionally and creatively (Reid 2016). 
Gail’s social media activity is also comparable to Alice’s: she has 6,248 Instagram 
followers (at the time of interview), posts an average of once per day and attains around 
100 likes per post. But the images are highly polished, from photo shoots of the 
designer’s collection or images of the retail boutique to magazine editorials of her 
garments. Images of Gail working in her studio do not appear to be part of the social 
media strategy, unlike Alice Nightingale. 
Sales channels for both Alice and Gail are similar, but not the same. Whereas 
Alice sells directly to overseas customers through aggregating online platforms such 
as Etsy, Gail’s ‘fashion reach’ is greater, not only through her website, but through her 
high-end media exposure, which arguably nudges her into the mainstream. This is not 
only due to the press her label enjoys, but partly because she has been physically 
present at international events. For example, in 2010, Gail presented at Who’s Next 


---

 
94 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
fashion fair in Milan, received feedback from Anna Wintour, had her collection 
presented on the runway with Dolce & Gabbana and was stocked for a time in that 
label’s Spiga2 boutique in Milan. This demonstrates the higher profile that conceptual 
designers can achieve, and validates their ‘elite’ (Craik 2009) category. Although, 
according to Gail, the marketing of the label is ‘pretty organic’ (Reid 2016); echoing 
chief editor of Vogue Australia Edwina McCann’s comments (Brisbane Fashion 
Month [BFM] 2015) that public relations (PR) is overrated, Gail explained that she 
rarely uses PR agents:  
We did have PR agencies on and off depending. If we did fashion week for 
instance, we’d bring someone on board, just to help with the manpower of 
organisational processes, just trying to maximise exposure, but it’s fully 
sponsored (Reid 2016). 
Gail Sorronda thus circumvents her lack of locational fashion credibility with 
appearances in high-stakes fashion magazines and at events, which strengthen her 
appeal to the local market. However, she noted that the local media also reaches many 
people.  
It’s not dressed up and as glossy, but people can just relate to it, which helps 
us. You’ve got your aspirational element but you’ve also got something else. 
You would think it would be the opposite but what is perceived as successful 
isn’t necessarily so (Reid 2016).  
The designer believes people like the idea of supporting a local designer or even just 
local business: ‘It’s as simple as that’ (Reid 2016). 
Conceptual designers may share a preference for quality, ethical practice and 
unique creative output with other independent designers, but whether driven by or 
setting new boundaries, their aesthetics are influenced by trends. This can also be a 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
95 
cause for concern for designers and points to the need for a supportive ecosystem of 
critics beyond the customer base. Gail remarked:  
We have access to so many different opportunities and we get lost. It almost 
paralyses us. We do a bit of this, a bit of that and we never really focus to 
achieve something that can flourish (Reid 2016). 
Arguably, much like the designers studied by Wenting, Atzema and Frenken (2011) in 
Amsterdam and Heebels and van Aalst (2010) in Berlin, conceptual designers such as 
Gail who have decided to settle locally could benefit from interaction with likeminded 
peers. Gail does indeed circulate in the local fashion design community, but concedes 
that a community-based and professional fashion support system could advance this 
interaction.  
Custom Clothing 
Unlike the conceptual designer, who is at the centre of the process, researching 
and generating garment designs/products for the market as a fait accompli, custom 
clothing services see the client steering the activity, building the design collaboratively 
with the designer from scratch. As a pre-digital and arguably pre-industrial practice 
(outlined in the literature review), custom design is highly specialised and service-
oriented, and may be considered conventional in terms of design and production. This 
business mode aligns with my own practice.  


---

 
96 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
 
Figure 8: Heim 2017, ‘Hadley’ gown 
While operating as a custom bridal designer in Brisbane, I relocated the studio 
several times. I started at Park Road, Milton, which attracted a well-to-do local 
clientele. Because of prohibitive lease increases and the need for more space, the 
business then moved to suburban Alderley, which taught me the benefits of being 
situated on a busy thoroughfare with a large shop front. The impending redevelopment 
of that site and my own change of heart led to another move to a scaled-down studio 
in Fortitude Valley, which, as it had no window display, relied almost entirely on 
referral marketing. During my 13 years of operation in Brisbane, I also noticed many 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
97 
of my competitors relocating a few times, to and from the city. Invariably, these 
decisions were driven by good retail exposure and more affordable leases (Felton 
2013).  
Business Characteristics 
The custom clothing business model generally produces one-off pieces such as 
bridal and eveningwear with a high price point (often several thousand dollars). Gowns 
are made to measure and usually designed with considerable customer input. These 
nuanced processes require specialist knowledge and understanding of production, 
dedicated supply chains and points of sale. Knowledge of corset construction and/or 
components is necessary, as is knowledge of lace types, suppliers, cutting techniques, 
construction, care and handling. All these elements are specific to the product as well 
as the market segment, and not necessarily addressed in general fashion education 
courses or indeed understood by the designer when they set out to establish a 
specialised business. Further, these precise knowledge and understanding essentials 
may alter throughout the business lifecycle as the market and trends shift. This 
suggests that designers sometimes require specific solutions for their niche 
entrepreneurial needs. For example, one of the QUT CEA Accelerator designers who 
works within the same segment states:  
I occasionally use Foundations Reveal, which is a corsetry website/forum. 
They don’t have a great deal of information on the business side of things. I 
think they’re starting an internet mentoring program specifically for 
corsetieres, but it’s overseas (Designer 4 CEA 2017). 
The designer is concerned that although it may cater to a specialist genre, the 
information may not be helpful to her in Australia. 


---

 
98 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
Business Opportunities and Challenges 
Custom clothing necessitates the customer’s physical presence and thus cannot 
be successfully conducted online. This constraint has led to innovation in practice. 
Custom clothing designers diversify or find secondary forms of revenue, as already 
noted among the conceptual designers described above. For example, custom clothing 
designer Paul Hunt has recently collaborated with journalist/stylist Kellie Alderman to 
create the ready-to-wear label ‘Hunt Kelly’ (Baker 2016). Whereas a Paul Hunt 
couture piece costs upwards of $6000, this secondary line sells wardrobe staples such 
as classic loose white t-shirts and maxi dresses through the Facebook page (only) at a 
mass-market level price point ($75‒120), thus increasing revenue for the designer. 
Diversifying a product or service range requires the need for agility while embracing 
an entrepreneurial disposition, capabilities that can be enhanced though learning and 
support.  
Custom clothing designers must have support for their highly specialised areas 
of production. In addition, wholesaling and dissemination occurs behind closed doors 
in Australia at hotel ‘showings’ in Sydney and Melbourne twice a year. While custom 
designers are highly secretive, particularly concerning proprietary methods and 
specialist suppliers, this can cause isolation, meaning that specific problems are 
difficult to resolve. These designers would benefit from a more open forum on their 
specific needs. 
Fashpreneurs 
Another business model that embraces an entrepreneurial mindset is what I term 
the ‘fashpreneur’. To this point, I have described some of the variations of micro-
entrepreneurship that typify independent practice. Other variations in fashion design 
entrepreneurship include ‘mumpreneurs’, mothers conducting businesses from home 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
99 
(Foley 2016), and/or ‘minipreneurs’, sole traders following the DIY crafting route 
(Luckman 2015) and selling on platforms such as Etsy (Etsy 2012). Mum and 
minipreneurs are similar to what I identify as ‘fashpreneurs’. Often professionals 
transitioning from another unrelated and/or non-creative area, fashpreneurs have 
decided on a change of career and a specific move to fashion entrepreneurship. This 
may be because they are affected by economic changes in the workplace, would like 
to pursue a more creatively satisfying, self-determined career (Carson 1999; Tate 
2007; Springsteel 2013; Chitrakorn 2016) or have an entrepreneurial idea that fills a 
gap in the fashion market.  
 
 


---

 
100 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
 
Figure 9: Meta Designs Co. 2016, ‘Map Print’ top 
Case Study Vignette 4: Meta Design Co 
Kathy Seton, a highly qualified anthropologist, began the label Meta Design Co. 
from her suburban Brisbane home when she was in her late forties. She prefers the 
creativity of making things by hand to the rigours of writing reports in a government 
job. Kathy buys fabric from retailers such as Spotlight or travels to Japan to find unique 
materials from which she makes ranges of simple skirts and tops. At the time of the 
interview, the brand had no website, only an Instagram account with images of clients 
and friends wearing the clothes, which are mostly sold at open-air designer markets. 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
101 
An Etsy page has recently appeared. The price point is low, at around $50 for a top 
and $60 for a skirt. Kathy finds that customers are always looking for something 
different, and this influences her unusual fabric choices, which are not based on current 
trend criteria. For example, the bestseller is the ‘map’ print garment (made of fabric 
purchased from a retail rather than wholesale supplier). Kathy still works in her ‘day 
job’ and hopes to transition to full-time independent practice in the near future.  
Business Characteristics 
Kathy’s oblique transition into fashion practice is more common in the 
designer/maker area than any of the other subcategories of independent practice, and 
for this reason, the designer/maker subcategory is sometimes not considered 
professional. Kathy considers herself an ‘accidental dressmaker’ (Seton 2016), and 
looks for all the help she can get, especially regarding the terms and processes within 
the industry. The term ‘dressmaking’ is somewhat problematic in this study, as it 
encompasses the basic function of cutting and assembling a garment, which all 
independent designers undertake or oversee at some point in their production. The 
difference between the designer/maker represented by Alice and the 'fashpreneur' 
represented by Kathy is that Alice has fashion design education and ‘skin in the game’.  
That is, she has been working almost exclusively in fashion throughout her career so 
far, whereas Kathy has stepped in sideways from an unrelated career and is now having 
to ‘catch up’ with fashion background knowledge. However, Kathy (and Alice) see 
dressmaking as a fundamental crafting skill that lies at the heart of their personal 
motivations and ‘workstyle’ (Larner and Molloy 2009), an activity from which they 
derive joy and satisfaction; it thereby surpasses mere production. Hence dressmaking, 
although devalued in the past as a homemaker hobby, is attracting new value in the 
landscape of contemporary business modelling. 


---

 
102 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
Indeed, Luckman (2015) observes the professional‒amateur or ‘pro‒am’ aspect 
of current maker practices undertaken by new founders. Some practitioners improve 
their processes through short vocational courses, internet sources (YouTube), ‘learning 
by doing’ and, most significantly, forums in online communities (Luckman 2015; 
Gauntlett 2013; Bearne 2012). Regardless of the lack of formal fashion education, 
barriers to entry (both financial and educational) are low—therefore, these 
entrepreneurs launch their small business with confidence and common sense 
(Springsteel 2013). However, exciting as the prospect of starting a label may seem, 
there is a strong need for expert guidance, as Mentor 2 pointed out: 
They are missing the insider knowledge that people don’t like to give away. I 
think she (another fashpreneur mentee) needs very bespoke and specialist help 
with her pattern cutting (Mentor 2 2017).  
The mentor does not believe the designer’s work can be mass-produced in China, but 
rather needs a smaller production house. She also explained that another designer 
already manufacturing in China needs help with penetrating international markets 
more than production.  
A subgroup of fash, mini or mumpreneurs that falls into the product development 
category is the ‘mono-brand’. This means a designer creates and markets a single 
fashion-related ‘hero’ product, such as a fashionable surgical theatre cap 
(luvmyscrubhats.com 2017) or monogrammed baby blanket (Spatz.com.au 2017). 
Perhaps unknowingly grounded in good business sense, these business models can be 
very successful, as evidenced in the popularity of the bucket bag produced by New 
York label Mansur Gavriel (Chitrakorn 2017). If successful, they can grow rapidly. 
But rapid growth can be surprisingly difficult, and sometimes leads to failure 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
103 
(McDonnell 2017), thus requiring yet another set of support mechanisms to aid the 
expansion phase, scaling production, financing and distribution.  
The variations on niche business models emerging in the current creative 
economy are represented in the vignettes above, and all need some form of business 
support. Whether dealing with difficulties in manufacture and retail or simply building 
business expertise, fashion entrepreneurs require access to specific support. 
Business Support 
Unlike the expert networks that exist in world fashion locations, as described in 
Chapter 2, many Australian designers lack access to networks with experienced 
professional infrastructure (Weller 2007). During Project 1, through the contextual and 
literature reviews and qualitative interviews, it became clear that the field of 
independent design practice in Australia was not only under-researched, but 
complicated by the presence of many business model variations. The findings pointed 
to the need for a platform aimed at Australian independent designers to help them 
navigate this challenging environment. As a result, Project 2 set about developing a 
support platform; this process is laid out in the next chapter. Over the course of 
planning, building and testing the website in Project 2, evidence of the need for support 
became even more apparent.  
Two main areas of concern emerged from interview data gathered in both 
projects. First, designers do not run their businesses in a straightforward manner; they 
add to their own difficulties through their non-conventional, unplanned, convoluted 
and ad hoc methods. Often unsure of what to ask, they are uncertain or uninformed 
about procedures for production, marketing and/or distribution, to name a few 
examples. They also find the right support difficult to access. Designers perceive 


---

 
104 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
support information as disjointed, unreliable, irrelevant, not local, difficult to retrieve 
later or unprofessional.  
These concerns were articulated by the designers from the QUT CEA Fashion 
Accelerator who participated in the Project 2 focus group. The group was established 
to test the support platform prototype developed for this study, and its outcomes are 
discussed in Chapter 4. However, some of the participants’ remarks are noted here as 
evidence reinforcing the need for professional support. These designers, as well as 
industry mentors, indicated the need for a comprehensive resource, confirming 
preliminary indications in the interviews with designers quoted above. 
Unconventional Business Approaches 
The designers interviewed for the focus group in Project 2 were all members of 
the Queensland University of Technology Creative Enterprise Australia (QUT CEA) 
Fashion Accelerator at the time of interview. Each year, the QUT CEA provides a 
fashion incubator program. Application is open to the public and around six designers 
who have already started their independent design careers are selected. Set up by the 
CEA in response to the perceived need for business support in the creative industries, 
the Accelerator has had several iterations since its inception in 2008. The designers 
were thankful of the face-to-face advice they were obtaining within the program and 
from their one mentor. The mentor’s strengths lie in production, while guest speakers 
and workshops cover all other areas of business. Knowledge is gained and shared, and 
learning is a process of finding out what questions to ask. A combination of knowledge 
and ‘all round’ experience appear important.  
I guess in terms of the information I get, I feel good because I get solid 
information from Thuy (the Fashion Accelerator manager/mentor) because 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
105 
she knows what she’s talking about, so, that feels good (Designer 3 CEA 
2017). 
The designers’ reliance on informal networks was the main aspect that emerged from 
the introductory questions in the focus group session. Designers were happy with the 
interaction they were experiencing in their incubator program, but found that more was 
required, and would still be required once they had completed the course.  
The designers who participated in the focus group preferred to create their own 
style of business practice rather than adopting the rigorous and linear approaches of 
conventional business models, which they encountered through guest speakers. 
Designer 3 echoed a similar workflow approach to the designer/makers interviewed in 
Project 1: 
I just remember that I got a lot of my early help from Etsy. I had an Etsy shop 
and they had a lot of really good business advice like how to cost your products 
and what things to take into consideration and your costing and things like 
terms and conditions and things to think about putting on your website that 
was quite good (Designer 3 CEA 2017). 
This designer also confirmed the responses of others who searched for what they 
required as that need arose, rather than planning first. In fact, many early-stage 
designers are not aware of the questions they should be asking regarding, for example, 
lead times for ordering fabrics, patternmaking and garment production management in 
general.  
One of my biggest struggles has been the time-lining, because going into 
something like this, never having done it before, you have no idea what’s 
involved so you start doing things, thinking you’re doing it in the right order 


---

 
106 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
and then you realise you haven’t. Yeah, I didn’t realise it would take eight 
weeks for some stuff (Designer 3 CEA 2017).  
Whether solicited or not, they did not seem to readily assimilate advice, opting instead 
to adapt an impromptu approach to their business practice:  
When I was manufacturing overseas, I would often get given advice that I 
never asked for from the owner! But I never really knew what I needed to ask, 
because it was so small and I just felt like I was nutting everything out slowly 
myself (Designer 1 CEA 2017).  
This may suggest both impatience and exasperation on the part of the designer, but 
does not necessarily guarantee smooth operations.  
Because most are operating at a low turnover level, independent designers are 
building community over commerce via their involvement in networking groups, 
social media interaction, blogging and online advice forums, even though they may be 
competitors (Kuhn and Galloway 2015). However, one industry mentor warned of the 
fallibility of peer networks:  
When I was starting out I only went to industry-specific networking events or 
workshops where I was able to learn something new. More often than not I 
found myself with people in the same boat. Much as we could rise together 
we weren’t really able to help each other (Mentor 1 2017). 
Aligning with the literature and early findings in the study, most designers are still 
reliant on friends and family for business advice (Craik 2015), but also realise that 
there is a limit to their friends’ knowledge of the complexities of the fashion industry.  
I would ask advice from my friends who were in business but it was difficult 
because they weren’t in fashion business, so they could give me good 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
107 
marketing advice or general business advice but not fashion advice (Designer 
1 CEA 2017). 
I’d ask family and friends that had a little finger in business, but yeah, a similar 
thing, it’s never been in fashion (Designer 2 CEA 2017).  
One mentor noted that designers trust and rely on their friends a little too much. 
Instead, she suggests they should turn to their friends for market research information 
rather than business advice. 
Yes, talk to close people, understand their perception of the market and 
understand their point of view from a customer’s point of view but then move 
on (Mentor1 2017). 
The mentor advises advancing to networking events where people have the same 
interest, and then on to networks of professionals.  
Access to Support 
Australian designers demonstrate ingenuity, creativity and entrepreneurship. 
They are connected, but have much fewer resources at their disposal than their 
international counterparts, who have, for example, support networks in the Garment 
District of New York, Italian local artisanal suppliers and professional linkages in 
Berlin and Amsterdam, as described in Chapter 2. Australian designers it seems get 
less support than their counterparts in some other places on the fashion periphery such 
as New Zealand, Canada and Indonesia. The New Zealand government sees designer 
fashion as an industry capable of raising the country’s international image and thereby 
attracting additional foreign investment (Larner and Molloy 2007). In Montreal, the 
government actively supports various incubators to improve the success of the 
designers, resulting in the city named as UNESCO City of Design in 2006 in 
recognition of its activity as a creative city (Tremblay 2014). Fashion diplomacy is 


---

 
108 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
substantial in Indonesia and other South East Asian territories with programs open to 
designers to visit Australia, the UK and other countries. These cases point to 
government policy that recognises the ‘fashion effect’ on displaying creative output to 
the world, thereby building cultural reputation. But it is not the case everywhere, for 
example Hong Kong designers also complain that they have no support. However, this 
may also soon be changing with the recent announcement of substantial capital 
injection for start-ups promised by Hong Kong policy makers.  The empirical evidence 
arising from the designers and mentors’ perspectives explored above indicates the need 
for a source of variable and customised advice that is useful and relevant for each 
owner/practitioner and business type.  
Most of the designers felt that they required information on sourcing and 
manufacturing and the more ‘secrety stuff’ (Designer 3 CEA 2017). The need for 
contextualised support was also expressed. For example, Etsy is based in Brooklyn, 
which means that not all its information is relevant: 
Well Etsy have a base in Sydney since 2012 I think, and I went to their Etsy 
business convention in 2012 or ’13 when they first moved over.6 That was 
really good because they started making a lot of Australia-centric stuff, but I 
guess because most of it was website based, or what do you put on your shop 
listings and stuff like that, it’s all kind of very generic (Designer 3 CEA 2017). 
Although the Etsy website and business support seemed useful at first, little is available 
on sourcing, particularly in Australia. The ‘secrety stuff’ mentioned by Designer 3 
refers to the standard practice of keeping ‘trade secrets’ that has always been and still 
is common in the fashion system. The designers interviewed in Project 1 discussed the 
                                               
 
6 Etsy Australia was closed in 2017 as part of a global restructuring of the company. 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
109 
loosening of this paradigm in the current sharing economy as a reaction to 
conventional fashion business modelling and a collaborative aid to survival. Designer 
2 remarked on the secrecy she still observes in the fashion industry: 
I come more from digital graphic design. For that I find a lot on the internet 
and for free as well, but fashion design is different. I feel like a lot of people 
don’t even get started because they don’t even know where to start. (Designer 
2 CEA 2017)  
Several respondents together: It seems like very secretive … on the whole. 
Yeah that’s true.  
They definitely don’t want to share their factory (Designer 2 CEA 2017). 
Other: because there’s so few (sarcastically). 
They realise there are ample factories and services, but suppliers’ contact details are 
difficult to find. Further, the advice these designers receive is disjointed and often not 
taken seriously: 
I would get some (advice) from like, Shopify, like emails and things about 
business and certain things you should do, and tips, but I didn’t really adopt 
them too much (Designer 1 CEA 2017).  
Some of these concerns may be dealt with in fashion education programs, but the 
reaction of the focus group designers (most of whom were transitioning from other 
careers or had little formal fashion education) was that they learn so much during their 
workshops at the Accelerator and then forget it, unable to recall or retrieve information 
when required. This makes a case for lifelong learning, but how that learning should 
occur is unclear. According to Mentor 1: 
We all want to see the industry thrive, as much as we all want to share and be 
a part of it, there are people out there who keep the contacts to themselves. 


---

 
110 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
Finding the ones that are willing to share their contacts is quite difficult 
(Mentor 1 2017). 
Designers were also asked to share information about the websites they preferred to 
access for business advice, and to describe any challenges that their business/practice 
faced that could be addressed by a business support website. They were then asked if 
they had questions relating to their business that they would prefer to access 
anonymously and/or more conveniently via the internet. When asked if they perceived 
a need for online support, the consensus was unanimous: 
I think there’s definitely a lot of room for a platform that could offer that. If I 
had known there was one I would have definitely been on there! (Designer 2 
CEA 2017) 
This was reinforced by a comment a week earlier from the experienced former 
business owner:  
The concept is so great. Imagine, starting out as a designer with something 
like that! (referring to the resources on the website) (LP 2017). 
The interviews from the focus groups strengthen the argument for a comprehensive 
support mechanism. Appendix D: Support requirements of various small-scale fashion 
business models features a table summarising resource requirements across the diverse 
businesses. Empirical data for this table was collected from 13 designers as well as 
other stakeholders consulted for the study, as listed in Chapter 1. The snapshot of the 
examples in the table shows the diverse range of independent designers’ target markets 
for this project and explains why business solutions must be customised to be relevant. 
For example, the custom designer has a different client base and production method to 
the ‘Fashpreneur’. Each business model has its particular opportunities and challenges 
which need to be addressed. 


---

 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
111 
Conclusion 
This chapter has described and analysed some of the diversity in independent 
fashion design practice that has arisen in the current creative economy. Based on 
interviews with locally embedded Brisbane independent designers and mentors, the 
chapter identified several subcategories of small-scale business practice, including 
workstyle-oriented designer/makers, concept-driven elite designers, high-end custom 
couturiers and transitioning fashpreneurs. The operations of these enterprises differ 
significantly from the large-scale retail industry and present both opportunities and 
disruptions to the dominant fashion system.  
The chapter has also shown that Australian designers are subject to particular 
sets of challenges. These stem not only from the aforementioned diversity, but from 
these designers’ motivational, educational and financial backgrounds, understanding 
(or lack) of business knowledge and locations. The use of non-conventional work 
spaces and places, and a sense of pride or branding the locally made add to the 
variations in business strategies that designers are using to build resilience. Even if 
successful, such as in the cases of Alice Nightingale and Gail Sorronda, independent 
designers are still wary of their fragile financial position. Meanwhile, various factors 
have led to the increased viability of small-scale enterprises in the current creative 
economy, including encouragement from a philosophically aligned customer base. The 
affordances of ICT, including e-commerce and sites such as Etsy, have facilitated and 
ameliorated some of the operations for small-scale designers, which have led to new 
approaches in fashion marketing and retail. However, operations could be smoother 
and potentially more successful with the aid of well-informed support. The findings 
support the study’s proposition that the diversity of practice among independent 
designers requires an adaptable and professional support mechanism.  


---

 
112 
Chapter 4: Contextual Review 
The next chapter demonstrates that support is abundant and freely available, but 
not readily accessed. Categories of business concerns will be compared with a matrix 
of business support mechanisms available to local designers. The next chapter also 
reveals how, in isolation, conventional modes of support are inadequate for addressing 
the nuanced and varied modes of production, marketing and distribution of either 
conventional, emerging or marginal business practices. The chapter presents the 
practice component of the study, which consists of prototyping the design of a support 
mechanism. It outlines the web design process, beginning with a scan of the current 
support environment, the researching and drafting stages and then testing of the web-
based prototype with designers.  
 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
113 
Chapter 5:  Practice 
The previous chapters identified the emergence of innovative modes of practice among 
independent designers in the current creative economy. The new diversity in practice has in 
turn led to additional entrepreneurial challenges for which designers seek support and 
solutions. This chapter presents the support mechanisms currently available to designers—for 
example incubators, consultants and industry bodies and, more specifically, online resources. 
According to industry mentors, as well as the designers interviewed for this research, business 
owners are not taking full advantage of existing virtual resources. The data from this study 
demonstrate that current support mechanisms are either not working, should be adapted to 
designers’ specialty needs and/or the offerings available require further refinement. Through 
an iterative design process, the aim of this chapter is to test the hypothesis of Chapter 3: that 
designers need support, but their current options are inadequate. This chapter makes the case 
for configuring a single, overarching online resource, the Fashion Business eMentor, and 
outlines the prototype development process.  
This practice-based chapter is structured according to the five stages of the design 
thinking process stated in Chapter 1: empathising with the designers (searching for support), 
defining the problem (determining that an online solution is needed), ideating (developing its 
structure, locating content and categorising themes), prototyping (using a web-building 
application) and, finally, testing (seeking feedback through a focus group). One of the project 
objectives is to find ways of alleviating the perceived pressure of business commitments 
through straightforward access to information, thereby improving productivity, efficiency, 
business resilience and ultimately success. The website prototype developed in this chapter 
proposes to resolve the problem identified in the first section by advancing the findability, 
credibility and relevance of fashion business support.  


---

 
114 
Chapter 5: Practice 
As part of the professional practice component of this study, and to further build 
knowledge on innovative means of support for small business, the project now turns to design 
research. Here, I draw on the constructive design research theory of Koskinen et al. (2011) 
discussed in Chapter 1, which underpins product construction as a key method of forming 
knowledge. Knowledge forming occurs in the planning, doing, reasoning and acting of the 
design process (Stappers 2007) and can include the use of physical objects such as notes, 
headings and sketches on paper, which become tools to aid community discussion and 
interaction (Koskinen et al. 2011).  
Additionally, combining design thinking with creative problem-solving guarantees a rich 
outcome of original data. The five pillars of design thinking (Plattner 2017) are to empathise, 
define, ideate, prototype and test a concept: 
 
Figure 10: Design Thinking, Stanford D School (Plattner 2017) 
 
I empathise with the designers through my own lived experience and my discussion with 
them for this research. I defined the problem by ascertaining the various difficulties in 
accessing information. I ideated solutions by collaborating with various stakeholders. I ran 
collaborative workshops to build a well-considered scaffold for the resource platform 
prototype. I then tested the platform to find parameters for new iterations and document the 
findings. This chapter goes on to detail this design thinking process for the research.  


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
115 
The environment and the tools independent designers use to help solve their business problems 
may be of vital importance in terms of the ability to innovate and produce new ideas. If, as 
Schon (1983) suggests, the interaction between thinking and problem-solving is a ‘dialogue’ 
between our bodies, tools and the environments we find ourselves in, then the configuration of 
those environments is of crucial importance to the types of problems that can be solved or types 
of ideas generated. Thus, the Fashion Business eMentor tool is ‘configured’ here as an external 
tool for designers to solve their own business problems. In the course of practice, I developed 
an organisational structure for the support of new fashion business models.  
Step 1: Empathising with Designers’ Modes of Operation 
In the initial ‘empathising’ stage of the design thinking process, I sought to understand 
the needs of the target audience through identifying the key fashion business themes that posed 
concern. As a designer and small business owner, I can empathise with designers operating 
their own ventures at any stage of the business lifecycle. Admittedly, my own niche is different 
to that of many of the designers investigated in this project, but several commonalities 
prevail—these include the need for business support and accurate information on suppliers, 
costing structure and industrial relations. Importantly, I can recognise the designers’ and 
secondary users’ shared need for findable, credible and relevant information that can be 
accessed quickly and conveniently.  
This research project led me to group the questions I frequently encountered as an 
entrepreneur into categories. Synthesising these into sections such as planning, production, 
marketing and accountability helped make sense of the jumbled information available. Similar 
needs, concerns and ultimately themes arose in my discussions with designers. Research into 
self-help publications also revealed a similar pattern. In this way, I developed a taxonomy of
