the knowledge and skills designers require, and hence grouped the areas of support needed into 
themes. These were subsequently used as criteria to assess and categorise the various support 


---

 
116 
Chapter 5: Practice 
offerings discovered throughout the research. Ten themes of business resilience then formed 
the mainstay of the content, categorised as follows:  
1. Personal capabilities  
2. Entrepreneurial opportunities 
3. Design and product development 
4. Sourcing and production 
5. Marketing and branding 
6. Sales channels and negotiation 
7. Business practice and legal 
8. Networking and community support 
9. Customers 
10. Finance and revenue. 
The next step was to comprehensively investigate the most significant avenues of support 
available to small-scale entrepreneurs. The support mechanisms I sought out included 
networks, incubators, publications, courses, consultants and mentors, but ultimately focused 
on material available online. The reason for focusing on online support relates to the core target 
audience: busy, independent designers. Often unable to leave a production or sales site, the 
small-scale designer who has many roles is time poor and unlikely to prioritise an excursion 
away from the workplace to another physical location in order to pursue the answer to a 
particular problem. By focusing on the advantages of online rather than physical support 
formats, I empathise with the designers’ needs and modes of operation. Online resources 
provide designers with the following advantages: 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
117 
• 
Convenience: ease of access and saving time for designers who have many roles to 
play within the small-scale enterprise. 
• 
Anonymity: to find out information without asking others and feeling ‘stupid’ 
(Designer 1 2017), as quoted by one interviewee in Chapter 3. There is a degree of 
embarrassment associated with ‘not knowing’ industry-specific information. The 
internet provides a discreet and convenient way of accessing such information and 
acquiring otherwise inaccessible knowledge. 
• 
Familiarity: web search is now the most common source of retrieving information. 
• 
Information: infinite amounts otherwise inaccessible. 
• 
Currency: continually updated information. 
• 
Affordability: either cost-free or relatively affordable (compared with visiting an 
expert, e.g., business consultant, lawyer, accountant). 
• 
Interactivity: to an extent a substitute for the lack of a ‘human’ in the support 
process, interactivity has become an important part of the user experience (UX), and 
therefore facilitates user adoption of online resources.  
However, my question was: to what degree would the available online support improve 
designers’ business resilience? Moreover, would this advice be appropriate to the diverse forms 
of practice I identified in Chapter 3, and within a ‘not-so-global’ fashion city?  
Step 2: Defining the Problem 
In the initial ‘empathising’ stage of the design thinking process, I set out to understand 
the needs of the target audience through identifying the key fashion business themes that posed 
concern. The next step was to define the problem—that is, the overwhelming and confusing 
information available online—by examining the existing online offerings that could assist 


---

 
118 
Chapter 5: Practice 
them. With a designer’s information needs in mind (the 10 themes), I researched several 
fashion industry bodies, both national and international, and their websites and support 
offerings (see Appendix E: Support mechanisms). The purpose of this online textual research 
was to confirm the abundance of information already available, list the potential sources of 
material, analyse accessibility and probe for omissions.  
Countless support organisations exist within the fashion system, upholding a variety of 
agendas, focuses and purposes. Located both nationally and internationally, in assorted formats 
and with diverse funding sources and functions, these include, but are not limited to, incubators, 
industry lobby groups, government departments, industry organisations, news sites, magazines, 
publications, trend forecasters, global retailers, video services, education providers and private 
citizens arranging meet-ups (meetups.com). The size and credibility of fashion interest bodies 
vary, with peak international organisations located in the tier-one fashion cities, such as the 
BFC in London and the Council for Fashion Designers of America (CFDA) in New York. In 
Australia, peak bodies include the Council for Fashion and Textile Industries Australia (TFIA) 
and the AFC.7 The common mission of these organisations, as they describe it, is to foster the 
development of fashion designers and their brands in either local and/or global markets. They 
tend to adopt an educative, top-down approach, providing wisdom and factual information in 
diverse formats and structures. These structures or themes depend on each organisation’s 
specific agenda. For example the CFDA prioritises the philanthropic support of glamour 
designers for an elite clientele. Therefore, although widely available on the internet, the 
organisations’ support mechanisms are strongly grounded in place. This is also the case for the 
BFC, which fosters not just British but especially London-based designers.  
                                               
 
7 These two industry bodies have now amalgamated formally to become the Australian Fashion Council. 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
119 
After reviewing the various support mechanism available, I collated information from 19 
highly visible organisations and arranged the metadata into a set of tables including 
descriptions and analyses, comparing the organisations’ offerings. These include formal, 
informal, consultative and commercial offerings of support (see Appendix E: Support 
mechanisms). Formal offerings from bodies such as the BFC and the CFDA were useful in 
terms of general industry information, but tended to provide long-winded reports. Informal 
offerings, such as magazines and blogs, generally omitted business support, focusing instead 
on current brand news and trends. Consultative offerings, such as mentors and incubators, 
provided little information in an accessible or anonymous online format, mostly redirecting 
enquirers to one-on-one meetings. Commercial offerings such as open-access and e-commerce 
platforms (for example, Etsy) offered information and support for independent labels, and had 
far more comprehensive suggestions across the 10 themes. However, for an independent 
designer based in Brisbane, some of these sites would be too generic, neither tailored to specific 
needs nor capturing the diversity of independent design practice.  
Although none of the support offerings met all the needs of independent fashion 
designers, I assessed some of the resources as appropriate some of the time. Of all the resources 
analysed, the BFC had the most comprehensive support across the 10 themes of resilience. 
Appendix E shows the data for two searches: the first shows a detailed analysis of the merits 
of each, and outlines which resources collected from the varied support offerings would be 
suitable for inclusion in a dedicated online platform. The second tests the support offerings 
with a search for garment costing information across the four categories of support offerings. 
In conducting the textual research into online offerings, I formed a picture of how to best 
aggregate the wealth of information available. I envisioned a data-based website that could link 
to the information required by designers, filtered through the pathways of the 10 themes.  


---

 
120 
Chapter 5: Practice 
Although virtual resources may seem convenient to access, searching for them is 
unexpectedly onerous and time consuming. This is because there are no standards, 
organisational parameters or equivalencies across the various fashion sites. Much information 
is generalised or repeated, and other information from my list of 10 themes was missing. As a 
consequence, and according to interviews with practitioners as well as the literature, designers 
often abandon their searches for business support in frustration, opting instead for guidance 
from family and friends instead of professional advice. This practice can result in unsuitable 
decisions for the designer’s business, subsequently reducing chances of survival and success. 
Online advice can be confusing, sometimes conflicting, of inconsistent quality and 
overwhelming (Mentor 1 2017). In the words of Mentor 1: 
You can Google just about anything. But there’s no filter system. There’s [sic] so many 
opinions and so much is open for interpretation whether the information is credible or 
not is debatable. A lot of that information might be generalised, not specific to their 
region, or not specific to certain taxes that could be charged and not specific to state, 
national, global situations.  
Further, doubts arise regarding the existence of potentially better information that remains 
‘unfindable’. Information tailored to a designer’s size, stage, location and individual situation 
was almost non-existent. Among the resources analysed, only the BFC and the Australian 
Government Department of Industry, Small Business website filtered information and then 
presented it in colour-coded groups. The credibility and professionalism of information was 
also not evident, and relied on the reader’s intuition or experience. This represents a difficulty, 
as noted by the designers interviewed, as many do not know the questions to ask in the first 
place (Designer 2 CEA 2017). Additionally, gaps or anomalies appeared in the information, 
including inaccuracies on some templates—for example, current wage awards, duties, freight 
costs and taxes, with no provision for local regulations. This reveals the danger of false or 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
121 
misunderstood information. The designer’s location is therefore a consideration when 
searching for relevant information, and demonstrates the need for a place filter.  
Researchers have identified the need to find order in a rapidly expanding information 
environment (Wichowski 2009; Onaifo and Rasmussen 2013; Spagnolo et al. 2010). Although 
search engines are powerful tools, designers require more nuanced information, which is often 
only identifiable by specialists in the field. This is because the fashion industry features a large 
lexicon of trade-related jargon, not all of which is comprehensible to all stakeholders within 
the system, depending on their area of specialisation.8 Organisations are adapting to ensure 
information ‘survival’ and findability (Wichowski 2009), but because the fashion industry is 
so diverse and information has accumulated so rapidly, no single organisation has attempted 
the task of filtering and tagging all the information available. Indeed, only a few years ago, 
some information scientists claimed this to be impossible (Ray 2010; Shirky 2010). However, 
the technology underpinning artificial intelligence (AI) is changing this viewpoint. Its potential 
application in solving the problems discovered here is explored further in Chapter 5. 
Step 3: Ideating the eMentor Concept 
Defining the problem of information overload led to ideating a solution: developing an 
all-inclusive web platform that provides accurate, timely solutions to the business problems of 
independent fashion designers through user-relevant access. In a sense, this is the function of 
a mentor—but as this support is offered online, the idea of this ‘electronic mentor’ inspired the 
working title: The Fashion Business eMentor. The target audience is a community of small 
fashion business stakeholders—not only those who aspire to be high-end conceptual designers, 
but lower profile and entrepreneurs dissimilar from one another. Initially conceived and 
                                               
 
8 This would be the case in many industries and adds to the notion that this research and model is transferable 
across disciplines. 


---

 
122 
Chapter 5: Practice 
prepared for a local population of diverse independent designers and comprehensive in scope, 
the purpose of this specialised portal is to collate high-quality information of professional 
validity. Ultimately, it may serve as a model for similar local communities. This platform is 
meant as a place for designers to find guidance and reassurance about aspects of business that 
the small-scale practitioner may struggle with, a place to look up industry terms without fear 
of sounding ‘stupid’ (Designer 2 CEA 2017), a place to visit in the absence of knowing who to 
ask and a place to find security, and thus to build inner business resilience.  
Gathering Content 
In the ideation stage of the design thinking process, I used creativity and innovation to 
look for broader solutions. Cognitive scientist Andy Clark (2001) argues that we externalise 
many of our thinking tasks into the environment and tools we manipulate, such as through 
sketching and note taking. Discovery can occur when we ‘gear internal resources to play a 
specific role in a reliable, extended problem-solving matrix’ (Clark 2001, 135). To prepare for 
this stage, I assembled content by gathering information based on the 10 themes.  I searched in 
fashion resource and support organisation websites. This search method proved difficult 
because although information was abundant, it was scattered and diffuse. Some information 
was oriented towards large-scale interests, other information was of a generic nature; some 
material was at a basic pre-start-up level and some was detailed, comprehensible only to 
‘insiders’. I encountered theoretical, practical and anecdotal texts as well as coercive copy to 
entice purchase of further information. As my perspective for this first search was that of a 
designer/entrepreneur, I tried a different method, reversing the process and searching on the 
websites for specific categories. Based on informal discussions with designers and my own 
experience, searching for topics within websites (e.g., for ‘marketing and branding’) was more 
likely to align with the way a designer would search for a solution on a website. Once selected, 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
123 
the table of themes proved a useful tool for categorising, collating and comparing data (or lack 
thereof); see Table 2: Table of themes.  
My experience as a designer, entrepreneur and educator helped me evaluate the 
credibility of the material I found. This included gauging the language and terminology used, 
the depth and range of topics, information in the ‘about’ section, references to other fashion 
system players and accuracy of business information. To assess a piece of content, such as a 
video or costing template, I used value judgements, asking myself if I would have found this 
information potentially useful to my business at any point. Warnings that alerted me to poor 
information included ‘salesey’ or persuasive writing and ‘click bait’ that numerically defined 
solutions, such as ‘5 things to think about before you start your own fashion business’. This 
was bound to lead to either non-specific or confused information written by a blogger with 
secondary information and experience. A few websites offered templates for production or 
marketing, some of which were very simplified and would identify the user as an amateur if 
they applied the information in their interactions with other industry stakeholders, potentially 
disadvantaging them. In this way, my experience as a practitioner became an important part of 
the research and practice, not only because I understood the specific terminology used, but 
because it afforded insight and contextual understanding of the challenges design entrepreneurs 
face at various times in the business lifecycle. 
Collating Content 
I collected the links to the information in a Word document, thus preserving a repository 
of content for the website collated into the 10 themes (see Appendix F: Website contents). 
However, these themes still required distilling for use on the website. I reduced the themes 
from 10 to 6 by incorporating two themes into one where possible (discussed below). This was 


---

 
124 
Chapter 5: Practice 
to align with commonly applied website navigation practice9 and simplify the search process. 
These topics eventually formed the persistent menu tabs on the website and were embedded in 
the header. Table 2 below demonstrates how the key concepts presented earlier evolved into 
the 10 themes of categorisation and were then condensed into the six main topic tabs for the 
website header bar. Of the 10 themes, I combined Entrepreneurial opportunities and 
Networking and community support into YOUR Opportunities, first because, contrary to 
conventional business theory, opportunities in value-added cultural products such as fashion 
design are more likely to arise from people and their personalities rather than products (Herbig 
and Milewicz 1993). This means that customers are more likely to buy products where they 
can relate to the designer or the person endorsing the product rather than the innate value of 
the product itself.  The classic white t-shirt is an example of a product that in itself has little 
fashion value, originally worn by sailors as underwear. However, cinema star James Dean was 
able to elevate the status of this humble garment to an iconic wardrobe staple permeated with 
fashion capital and ‘cool’. Since then, many designers offer their version of the white t-shirt, 
imbued with their particular personality or brand DNA. Second, the site speaks directly to the 
independent designer who emerges in this research as a task-centred practitioner and is 
unexpectedly less likely to realise the importance of their self-identity in the position of 
business ownership. Therefore, the website logic deliberately turns the focus of the designer 
on themselves rather than towards business categories, which may be the reasoning behind 
other more business-oriented websites. I combined Design and product development and 
Sourcing and production into YOUR Production, as these are substantially operational facets 
of the business, and although most of the designers interviewed inclined towards these aspects, 
I did not allow them to overshadow other aspects in size and perceived importance. Marketing 
                                               
 
9 Website users’ short-term memory retains only 5‒7 items at a time (Miller 1994) 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
125 
and branding and Customer relations were combined into YOUR Marketing, as all the concepts 
within this area have considerable overlap, representing the interface between designer and 
consumer. Sales channels, Negotiation and Finance and revenue became YOUR Money, as 
money belongs to many sections simultaneously under varying terms and constantly changes 
custodianship. This is perhaps one of the most perplexing issues many designers face, but once 
again need not be so. Business Practice became YOUR legals. Although a current vernacular 
term, ‘legals’ gives the impression of formal documentation, taxation obligations (which could 
also belong to ‘YOUR Money’), business planning and entities. This was a tenuous 
classification at this stage of the design development. The choice of the final six tab names was 
later tested with the workshops and focus group.

Although I possess a broad understanding of the fashion industry, I do not have complete 
knowledge of all aspects. To ensure balance, I consulted material I had delivered as a fashion 


---

 
128 
Chapter 5: Practice 
educator, and drew on my experience as a member of the Industry Reference Committee for 
the TCF Training Package. The government-based TCF industries Training Package provides 
approximately 500 units of competency, with comprehensive information and knowledge 
recommended for fashion design students (training.gov 2016). I cross-referenced information 
from units applicable to small fashion business, specifically from the MST60116—Advanced 
Diploma of Applied Fashion Design and Merchandising qualification (training.gov 2018), and 
added information to the content repository for the website where applicable. I used the 
curriculum for vocational education and training rather than higher education—instead of a 
national syllabus for university-level fashion business education, each university produces its 
own education design, which, although more advanced, means that standards and information 
differ. 
Using the key concepts and table of themes, I created a simple site map diagram to reflect 
the website information architecture to check the flow of navigation (Figure 11). However, 
designing, producing, distributing and marketing fashion are all interconnected yet specialised 
activities that one person, the independent designer, aims to do alone. I later realised that my 
original plan was too simplistic, as a designer is likely to navigate in an unplanned way. The 
structure required more sophisticated sorting of key concepts. Further, although I had initially 
organised key concepts into categories from the table of themes, the flowchart exercise 
prompted me to reconsider and regroup some of the concepts. This is because there is not 
always a linear pathway to solutions, as all 10 themes are inherently interrelated, and designers’ 
business problems will be as diverse as their modes of practice. 
 


---

I also wanted to curate a selection of resources in different media channels and formats 
and have options to go deeper, with supplementary information for each category. For example, 
I chose a small number of blog posts to provide accessible insights into key processes: I used 
‘cost structure’ for keyword searches in fashion industry and small business organisation 
websites. These searches provided material in various formats, including PDF articles, videos, 
templates and blog posts. I chose one of each format where possible, selecting up to five 
resources per concept. I skimmed the content to assess it for credibility and relevance (see 
Appendix G: Cost structure search for results of this search). These links would later populate 
the single pages of the website.

research strategy, this collection of themes and tabs required further ideating by a larger

Workshop 1 
In engaging design research, Ehn (2008) argues that design objects provide a site for 
community focus and discussion. While physical objects become tools for transforming 
designs, being able to see and touch objects mediates dialogue and helps clarify ideas. In a 
workshop-style meeting, all the keywords in Table 2 were allocated a Post-it note. This 
facilitated the next phase of ideating: brainstorming and generating dialogue in a team. The 
themed notes were rearranged in a workshopping session with my supervisors, thus devising 
solutions to the previously identified problem of categorisation and organisation. The images 
below show the method of using the physical notes to organise, rearrange and categorise 
themes. The large white pages delineate the six main categories and predicted website tabs in 
Table 2. They contain the yellow Post-its of the key concepts and pink notes for the 
subcategories.  
Figure 12 shows the allocation of Post-its, Figure 13 the reorganisation into different 
groups and Figure 14 grouping in subcategories. Linking back to the original table of themes, 
the key concepts within Marketing, Revenue and Sales were interchanged several times during 
the session. Some specific examples of contentious points in content organisation converged 
on Finances, Revenue and Costing as well as Sales and Marketing. The term Revenue alone 
can evoke diverse interpretations and reactions—for example, does that mean all of the 
business’ income, the designer’s drawings from the business or income from another source 
(such as a part-time job)? As seen in Figure 14, Money, Finance, Revenue, Income and similar 
were confused, because sometimes these expressions are seen in terms of the designer being 
the owner or generator of money, sometimes the creditor, sometimes the debtor. See Figure 13 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
131 
and Figure 14 below, where Money and Revenue are crossed out and keywords rearranged. 
These terms may seem clear to practitioners in finance, such as accountants and bookkeepers, 
but to the inexpert in business, they raise problems of interpretation, understanding and 
subsequent action.  
This problem of interpretation was also encountered in the testing phase, documented 
below. Similarly, the understanding of Sales and Marketing was confused regarding whether 
the relevant information is about wholesale, retail or both. As the two areas are intertwined in 
the current creative economy, a distinction—and, therefore, targeted information—is difficult 
to discern.  
Another issue that emerged during this workshop was the repetition of concepts, which 
were redefined under different names. A further concern was that some themes were very free-
flowing and general, such as the terms Inspiration, Vision and Philosophy, which prompted 
questions of suitability of content and/or headings. Some keywords were thus rearranged and 
even discarded, but this allowed the sequence of simple reactions to become organised and 
form a solution.  
This sorting process serves to illuminate the nature of a small business owners’ practice, 
demonstrating the diversity of skills required, from the specific to the generic, plus the inherent 
overlap of concept areas and the multifaceted nature of their work. The creative thinking 
deployed in this exercise also acts as a mediating factor in a variety of complex and iterated 
processes that continually loop between brain, body and environment—according to Clark 
(2001), it is this larger interaction that can solve problems.

This exercise in categorisation generated robust discussion, and it was clear that not all 
agreed on the organisation of the categories. We changed our minds, crossed out, rearranged 
and in some instances returned to the original organisation. We did this because some 
categories overlapped and seemed redundant, or confused the logic behind grouped themes and 
key concepts. This demonstrated the importance of designing the website from the designer’s 
standpoint, which led to retaining the tab and use of the personal pronoun ‘YOU’.  


---

 
134 
Chapter 5: Practice 
The viewpoint of the experienced fashion academics added rigour and objectivity to the 
selection and organisation of themes and key concepts. It demonstrated contested interaction 
with the designed world (Koskinen et al. 2011), revealing that individuals who interact with 
the designed world do so from varying positions. The sorting process also highlighted the many 
forms of knowledge required by a designer to achieve business success. 
Workshop 2 
At this point (shortly before the planned meeting for the focus group), my supervisors 
and I decided to have an interim discussion with experienced fashion business colleagues, 
because the table of themes had not only been rearranged, but indicated a fluid interpretation 
of some fashion business concepts. The two fashion business experts, Carla van Lunn (CvL) 
and Lydia Pearson (LP), have each been fashion designers, business owners and fashion 
educators. In preparation for the discussion with these experts, I applied the physical topic 
organisation above to the mind-mapping method used below (Figure 15), which captured all 
the themes and reorganised them as per the Post-it session. Instead of using Post-it notes and 
for ease of presentation, I used the mind-mapping software Scapple to organise and pre-empt 
the website content headings and subheadings. Presumed menu header tabs were red, orange 
indicated page topics and key concepts were filtered alphabetically. Below is an early version 
of the mind map generated for the presentation and discussion with the fashion business experts 
(see enlarged version in Appendix H: Initial Scapple mind map). The images in figures 14‒17 
show five key groupings of the following six themes: entrepreneurship, production, marketing, 
income, legals and reporting. Each theme in turn had many subcategories. 


---

135 
 
 
Figure 15: Scapple mind mapping and organisation of themes 
 
The two fashion experts were invited to a meeting together to continue ideating solutions 
to the problem of organising and categorising business themes and concepts in a manner that 
would be comprehensible and useful to designers. At first, they were introduced to the concept 
of the support website for fashion entrepreneurs. They were then given only the mapping 
pictured above; they did not have access to the website work-in-progress or the Post-its. Their 


---

 
136 
Chapter 5: Practice 
initial reaction was one of overwhelm and confusion. They found the mapping difficult to 
comprehend, and suggested further grouping into ‘bubbles’:  
CvL: There’s a lot of bubbles and it’s a bit overwhelming. 
LP: Maybe have more bubbles within bubbles. 
CvL: You can go through the dropdown menus, but if you don’t even know where to 
start … 
They began to regroup ideas on the mind map, questioning the placement and grouping of some 
elements. For example, they scrutinised the need for ‘YOU’ and personal capabilities: 
LP: I must say, looking at all the different pages, this one, ‘you the entrepreneur’, I 
don’t know that you necessarily need to move so far into these areas like relationships, 
motivation, resilience, risk-taking and self-determination. People can find those things 
in other areas and I would think if you need to make it a bit smaller (the site), you may 
be able to remove those. There are probably things in here that are good, like time 
management. 
CvL: Or if you found fashion-specific versions of work‒life balance for a fashion 
designer. They’re not so industry-focused. 
LP: Ethics yes, because you absolutely need to talk about that, but it could maybe go 
in another area like intellectual property or law.  
Surprisingly, the industry experts were not enamoured with the necessity of the category 
‘YOU’ for the website, feeling this information could be found on other websites. In line with 
Bridgstock’s (2011) and Mill’s (2011) findings, I contend that this is precisely the factor that 
is overlooked at the designers’ peril. Their identity, personality, wellbeing and above all 
mindset or approach and philosophy towards business requires addressing whether the tension 
between the creative and the commercial is to abate, and making way for better resilience, both 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
137 
individual and organisational. Therefore, although the industry experts did not agree, I 
persisted with maintaining the ‘YOU’ category to the next stage of the process. 
The industry experts were then presented with another version of the mind map, which 
showed the connections between the themes and the key concepts: 
 
Figure 16: Top level connections for potential menu tabs (see larger PDF versions in Appendix H) 
This is a very telling visualisation, demonstrating the complexity and overlaps in the 
fashion system, and to an extent justifying designers’ improvised (rather than linear) approach 
to dealing with problems as they arise. The density around certain areas shows the importance 
of, for example, the ‘supply chain’ compared with the relatively sparse area around ‘personal 
capabilities’ (see Figure 17). This also reveals the interconnectedness and non-linear nature of 
the knowledge demanded of the fashion entrepreneur. 


---

 
138 
Chapter 5: Practice 
 
Figure 17: Comparing ‘personal capabilities’ with ‘production management’ 
One could interpret these interconnections (or lack thereof) as another finding of the 
study. They imply gaps in particular content areas that must be addressed, as well as the relative 
importance designers place on denser concepts. For example, the high interconnections around 
supply chains points specifically to Australian designers’ needs, which arise from the shortage 
of local raw material manufacturers or garment production, tipping the domino effect towards 
logistics and project time-management issues. These issues do not exist to such an extent in 
traditional manufacturing centres, even if much production has moved offshore (Dicken 2008). 
Residual smaller manufacturers provide an invaluable service to niche designers, as seen in the 
regions of northern Italy and new initiatives such as Makers Row in the US, which connects 
local manufacturers with small-scale producers. These connections are almost non-existent in 
the analysis of support offerings researched locally.

search and tested the route all at once. Figure 18 below visualises an analysis of this pathway 
example.

The industry experts also discussed other ideas, including the format of the 
resources presented, interactivity, search capabilities and future use. Much of the 
website or platform content comprises links to relevant information. One industry 
expert proposed adding a component of ‘original’ content—that is, tips or advice in 
the form of blogs and or videos that could be a primary source of information for the 
user, and that could be supported with further links, reading and more.  
CvL: I suggest videos, for example, if you’re new to the site or if you’re a 
beginner, I suggest you start here, if you’re about to manufacture offshore I 
suggest you start here, and add a welcome video.  
CvL: What’s also good is to get the community to contribute 
recommendations for example. Whether you have forums or a Facebook 
group or other social platforms that are affiliated.  
This was an important observation regarding my ongoing engagement with the 
study and creating a community of designers around the platform. Up to this time, I 
had felt that curating the content for the site was my principal role, informed by my 
past experience—yet this remark from the industry expert prompted the idea of 
custodianship, making original content and fostering a more personal relationship with 
users via the website in its future version. I discuss this possibility further in Chapter 
5. The industry experts felt that the site map covered most of the key concepts in 
fashion business, and they needed more time to review the topics if additional 
information was required. They thought that some topics were not necessary and could 
simply form part of a glossary. Another point that arose in the discussion was the 
platform business model or commercialisation options: 
CvL: Yes, but you’ve got such a wealth of experience would you not offer 
some paid component? 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
143 
LP: That’s what I think. You should tier it.  
CvL: Yes, mentoring or online courses.  
LP: Or a tiered subscription where you get basic information, so a young 
designer who doesn’t have any money can still get some information. 
LP: I think there ought to be a component where you could pay, like Spotify 
you know. If you pay 99c a week you can have it ad free. And so you could 
have some extra tier of information. 
The prototype was subsequently reconsidered as a business endeavour in its own 
right. I adjusted the web-based model to be freely available online for anyone to access, 
with a tiered membership system as proposed above that provided more in-depth 
information for those willing to pay. Incorporating these ideas also had a bearing on 
the navigation, flow and content of the website design. Table 4 below presents a list of 
all the interactive functions proposed for the website. The site business plan is 
presented as a separate document submitted as a part of the package of practice for this 
study alongside the website, as noted in Chapter 1.  
The two workshops demonstrated the capacity of design research methodology 
to construct knowledge. This knowledge construction occurs through a participating 
community’s involvement in combining theory, practice, prior knowledge and 
experience within a structured framework (Koskinen et al. 2011). Contemporary 
theories of creativity have tended to emphasise the systems models, where creativity 
is seen to emerge from interactions of various kinds. For example, Csikszentmihalyi 
(1996) contends that creativity takes place in a three-part system involving a domain 
(culturally structured body of knowledge and rules—for example, the 10 themes), a 
field (a group of individuals who act as ‘gatekeepers’—for example, the industry 
experts) and the individual (the independent designer). Through the workshops, I 


---

 
144 
Chapter 5: Practice 
provided highly informed and experienced participants with the framework of the 
complex themes surrounding fashion business and tasked them with ideating solutions 
to the problem of categorising and, if possible, simplifying the organisation of 
resilience themes. All participants had different viewpoints, priorities and values, but 
common ground and agreement were found for most of the categories, which meant I 
could proceed to the next phase.  
Step 4: Prototyping First Draft 
For the purposes of this study, the first draft for the proposed larger and more 
advanced platform does not yet constitute a completed website. Instead, the practice 
for this study includes the preliminary design thinking methods for finding and 
categorising information relevant to designers, and encompasses web planning from 
wireframe to mock-up stage. After the workshops, I reorganised the content and links, 
considering both my own proclivities and the participants’ suggestions. The links were 
formatted into metadata lists (Table 3) and the content copied into the website on the 
relevant pages, as shown below:

Navigation and User Experience 
In constructing design research knowledge, the expert participants contributed 
ideas during the ideation sessions that were ultimately incorporated into the website. 
These included options for searches within the site, searches beyond the site, business 
stage diagnostic, possibilities for customised advice, a forum, expert contributors, a 
blog, newsletter and commentary, subscription membership, database, directory of 
local suppliers, calendar of local events, local networking groups and shared spaces, 
glossary of terms, original content, expertise and information. For the first draft of the 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
147 
prototype to be tested, the final six themes of business remained as per Table 2: ‘Home, 
You, Your Opportunities, Your Marketing, Your Production, Your Income’. 
One of the study goals is to improve the findability of resources, which is 
achieved through best-practice, intuitive web design. For example, too many clicks 
can frustrate the user. Hidden navigation (such as the so-called hamburger menu icon 
of three parallel lines) is problematic because ‘discoverability is cut almost in half by 
hiding a website’s main navigation. Also, task time is longer and perceived task 
difficulty increases’ (Pernice and Bidiu 2016, 1). Interior pages will not appear in 
search engine optimisation (SEO) rankings if there are too many links to reach interior 
pages: 
Exactly, that’s why having a search engine is super important. I think a search 
engine where you can just input what your [business] level is, and that could 
refine the search a little bit more, would be very helpful (CvL 2017). 
Efficient web navigation requires the searcher’s goal to be reached within one or 
two clicks. This can be achieved through subcategorising into ‘mega dropdown’ 
menus. This technique is used when information becomes too detailed, as illustrated 
in the screen captures below. The tabs were developed from the themes discussed in 
the ideation session and narrowed to six, following feedback from the industry experts. 
Mega dropdowns require subheading categories to help targeted navigation, but allow 
the user to find their destination in one click (Crestodina 2016). The industry experts 
interviewed during Workshop 2 also advised this change, which was then adopted. 
Ideally, hovering over ‘Your Production’ should open the list shown in Figure 21 
(Design R&D, Sourcing, Managing Production, etc.) and also reveal the page 
inclusions (Critical Path, CMT, Contracting, Costing, etc.) so that the user can locate 
‘costing’ in one click/hover from the home page. This represents a change to the initial 


---

 
148

navigation path shown in Figure 19 above, and is represented in Figure 20 and its 
proposed implementation in 21 below.

The BFC already employs this technique, as shown in the screenshot below. This

Figure 22: BFC 2017, mega dropdown menu and colour-coded topics 
I also used four categories, as the independent designers interviewed in the initial 
investigation often discussed the main stages of business, and I experienced similar 
phases in my own business—for example, that the start-up phase is quite different to 


---

 
150 
Chapter 5: Practice 
the growth phases. After further consultation with designers and mentors, I created the 
following categories: 
• Prep (red) 
• Start Up (yellow) 
• Scale Up (dark blue) 
• Exit (light blue). 
I added an ‘exit’ category, although this departs from conventional fashion 
business practice or the BFC’s ‘the only way is up’ approach, since they have ‘Future 
Brand’ as their final category (BFC 2017). There are many advantages to viewing a 
venture in this way—it may be because a designer has been resilient and worked for 
many years. Owners may exit a business because it is too difficult or failing, or they 
have changed their priorities. The business sale or planned strategy may stem from the 
tech start-up world, but is influencing all entrepreneurial practice in the current 
creative economy (CEA 2017). In the light of this study, the exit strategy partially 
represents resilience, and ultimately return on investment in the long term. 
The written content is supported by visual imagery. Although generic in nature, 
as the website does not sell a product that requires photographs, the images instead 
serve to appeal and inspire the visitor. To make the website less business-like, images 
of designers, their products, their shops and more could provide users with a personal 
connection. The images also act as a reassurance that the user has landed in the right 
place and is likely to find relevant results for their searches. Sliders encourage 
exploration of the website through the following five topics: 
• Create your designs 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
151 
• Share your passion 
• Find your answers 
• Make more money 
• Realise your dream. 
These may seem idealistic in tone, but this means they reference some of the 
websites and social media pages currently circulating in the designers’ online 
environment. Their appeal and suitability also require further testing. 
Wireframe 
The wireframe visually represents a website’s structure. It organises the order, 
size, functionality and relative importance of pages provided by the workflow 
(Scapple) diagram; it also organises the look, feel and navigation of the site, rather 
than the content editing, sorting and naming of tabs. Web designers can use wireframe 
software applications such as Invision, Sketcher or The Grid, or work by hand; this 
latter choice allows the designer to follow their intuition and take risks. As Jenkins 
(2016, 1) explains: 
This is an efficient and loose process, which allows for the quick and 
spontaneous progression of ideas. The wireframe blocks and identifies regions 
for the placement of content on the page. The blocking process is not 
concerned with dimensions or accuracy rather, the placement of elements.  
I preferred to work by hand, first because the scope of the research did not allow 
the time to learn a new software application and second because I am experienced in 
thumbnail iterations from my former graphic design practice. My wireframe sketches 
are shown below (Figure 23).

as inspiration for the overall site design aesthetic as that could appeal to multiple audiences and suggests ‘work in 
progress’ 
Visual Design 
Websites are designed objects, integrating visuals, text and multimedia into a 
UX designed to create a mood, provoke an emotional response and shape a brand 
identity. Website aesthetics are ideally organised in advance through creating a mood 
board containing various elements, including company name and brand information, 
logo, fonts, sample text, colour palette, textures and patterns, photos and illustrations, 
video clips and other project details. However, as I intended to use the WIX 
application, which had several templates to choose from, and had already collected 
visual content, I opted not to take this step. Instead, I started designing using the 
templates available, at most adjusting the aesthetics within those templates. The 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
153 
imagery was kept to a minimum, as the website should convey a clean and organised 
impression. The look and feel of a site can have the effect of alienating or embracing 
particular fashion aesthetics, which is why I chose neutral imagery as much as 
possible. For the first iteration, the neutral imagery provided a ‘blank canvas’ that was 
not distracting for the focus group designers as they experimented with the site. This 
is not to discount the importance of aesthetic elements to the user UX of design layout, 
especially for the visually oriented designer audience. I have not fully addressed the 
website aesthetics, as this is an area of expertise beyond the scope of the project. Fine-
tuning the aesthetics could be considered in detail after receiving focus group 
feedback. As a placeholder for the branding, I used a photograph I took of tangled 
yarn, which I intended as a metaphor for Ariadne’s thread to symbolise both the 
beginnings of the apparel manufacture process and the ‘tangled’ information on the 
internet. This could potentially serve as a future logo, with the twisting yarn forming 
the ‘e’ in eMentor.10. 
Below is a mock-up of the first page of the website, created using a WIX 
template (Figure 24). The tabs in the header at the top each lead to mega dropdown 
menus. The persistent list at the side is grouped and allows for further filters. The round 
buttons beneath lead to interactive pages and the footer contains relevant details such 
as About, Contact, Legals, Copyright and more. The subcategory topics lead to at least 
five links, each in a different format. This is the ideal number for users to pursue in an 
average 10-minute internet search (Crestodina 2016).

Chapter 5: Practice 
Step 5: Trial and Test  
Step five moved into a new phase of the design process. The aim of the site is to 
improve the findability, credibility and relevance of the support information designers 
can access. Once the draft site was built, I needed to test the prototype with a group of 
designers. Support services can be measured in their effectiveness ‘to accelerate and 
improve the formation and growth of the enterprise by providing clients (e.g., small 
fashion business entrepreneurs) with a wide range of business assistance that includes 
counselling, networking opportunities and physical facilities’ (Lose and Tengeh 2016, 
370). I therefore set up a focus group initially to test the platform concept and 
navigation. Lasting approximately 60 minutes, the focus group session involved five 
independent fashion designers who currently work in a fashion incubator and are at 
various stages in their business lifecycle (see Appendix J: Focus group participants 
and session plan for details). The designers were given access to the prototype website 
and asked to explore the interface for around 10 minutes. The investigation included 
engagement, exploration and exit questions.  
The focus group was to be challenged with a designer ‘problem’, to be solved 
with and without the help of the resource. The table in Appendix J: Focus group 
participants and session plan outlines the initial session plan for all focus group 
activities and details of the ‘problem to solve’. The first session plan was changed 
because less time was allocated for the session (held at the fashion incubator space) 
than initially anticipated. The ‘problem search’ was thus eliminated and the designers 
simply asked for their opinions across a series of 10 questions (see Appendix K: 
Revised focus group session plan, which includes the revised questions). It was 
unfortunate that the costing problem could not be addressed in this session, and this 
became a limitation in the project data collection (see Appendix O: Costing challenge 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
159 
for details). This costing problem can instead be addressed in a future test of the 
website’s next iteration. 
Improving the Findability, Credibility and Relevance of Support 
During the focus group session, designers were instructed to access the prototype 
website. They were then asked to describe their UX of navigating the website, how 
useful they found the content of in terms of their business activities, whether that 
content complemented or duplicated the physical support they were already accessing 
or if they found new information that they did not realise was available, and to whom 
they would recommend the website. 
The designers’ curiosity was piqued when they viewed the online resource, and 
they exhibited a hunger for knowledge in the area. It was surprising to note that the 
participants showed particular interest in the resource: lengthy silences ensued as they 
navigated the site. After the interview, one designer remained seated at her laptop and 
remarked how excited she was about the site. All designers appeared to find new 
information, and were asked to describe their user experiences: 
D1: Good. 
D3: Yeah really good, it’s really clear. 
D4: It’s well set out. 
D2: I feel like all the dropdown menus all make sense. It’s easy to navigate. 
D1 (former graphic designer) I’m really not sure about this background 
graphic. I keep trying to read and it’s like … 
HH: The string? 
D1: Yeah, it’s too distracting and doesn’t need to be there. At all. White space! 
D3: In terms of useability, if the links were in different colours that would be 
really helpful.  
HH: Once you hover that they change colour?

D3: Even if you use different colours to start with. 
D2: Have the details first and then the source.  
D3: That would probably be good too actually.

findability and relevance of the content was not explored to a great extent, perhaps 
because of the short time allowed for the session. Discussion regarding content focused 
on the tab names and dropdown menus and what may potentially lie beneath them. 
This was a shortcoming of the focus group session, and indicated that another session

Personal stories are closely linked to self-identity and the personal qualities 
Bridgstock (2011) describes as ‘meta capabilities’. Bridgstock (2011) views these soft 
skills, such as emotional resilience, networking capabilities and being able to sell 
oneself as vital to creative entrepreneurial success. I concur, based on my own business 
experience, that the need for technical skills is not outweighed by the need for 
interpersonal skills and emotional intelligence.
