---

 
166 
Chapter 5: Practice 
Although the seasoned industry experts discussed garment costing fairly frankly, 
it seemed as if the focus group designers were avoiding in-depth discussion of sales 
revenue and income. This is concerning, as these factors directly affect designers’ 
survival and business resilience. It also suggests attention for future research. As noted 
above, data was on how the website can improve a designer’s viability and is therefore 
a study limitation, indicating opportunities for further investigation.  
The focus group proved a valuable extension to the design research 
methodology, because it identified additional areas of change and improvement for the 
platform and the needs of designers embedded in the local community. It also revealed 
the important of holding more focus groups or similar workshops to refine the content, 
findability, credibility and relevance of material to local designers. The designers 
seemed quite excited about the idea of the resource, and yet I suspected they were still 
not sure exactly which questions to ask to find the solutions they sought, again 
indicating the necessity of researching how designers acquire knowledge in the first 
place.  
Conclusion 
Building on the textual and empirical data gathered in previous chapters, the 
work in this practice-based chapter led to the organisation of 10 themes of resilience. 
The organisation of these themes was facilitated through design research methodology, 
which led to the iterative process of developing an online support resource for fashion 
entrepreneurs. Based on my own industry knowledge and in collaboration with experts 
and mentors, I began the process of creating the resource by empathising with 
designers and their perceived business difficulties. I identified shared elements 
between my own business activities and those of local independent designers. I defined 
the problem (the various difficulties in accessing information) and ideated a solution 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
167 
(the platform concept) with teams of experts. I collected high-quality professional 
content via textual internet research. I curated the content through my lived experience 
as a design entrepreneur and distilled the 10 themes into six menu headings for the 
website through consultation with practitioners. I produced the ‘homemade’ version 
of the website using the open-source website-building application WIX, which 
simulates the capacities of an anticipated larger, more high-tech version of the site, 
currently a working model to test the conceptual strength of the platform with 
designers. I tested the platform prototype and found parameters for new iterations. This 
prototype can be used as an example of expectations and could be presented to web 
engineers to develop the site further.  
The product that has emerged from this design research is the starting point for 
a resource rich in information to help address the business problems small-scale 
entrepreneurs encounter in Australia. I anticipate that it will contribute to the support, 
survival, resilience and success of independent fashion designers in the future. This 
digital toolkit does not replace physical support mechanisms such as the incubators, 
mentors, professional and social networks, education and fashion organisations 
identified at the outset of this chapter, but acts as an online assistant to them. It can be 
used in fashion education and remain a permanent ‘go-to’ resource for students once 
they have graduated. 
Scaffolding information for the website is the first step in building the support 
resource for designers, although accessibility is another concern that potentially 
requires more advanced technology. The consensus is that an ‘owner’ is needed to 
continue to drive such a platform, as it is unlikely to work autonomously. Therefore, 
although I imagined that the website could run itself, the need for ‘personal’ contact 
still appears important. Chapter 5 presents the way forward for the resource.


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
169 
Chapter 6:  Analysis 
This chapter reflects on the outcomes of the research conducted across two 
projects. Project 1 investigated local independent fashion designers and identified the 
challenges and opportunities encountered in operating a small-scale business. Project 
2 focused on support mechanisms that help designers build successful businesses. Both 
projects were synthesised in this exegesis to answer the question:  
In the light of challenges experienced by the local fashion industry, how can 
small-scale independent Australian fashion design entrepreneurs be supported to 
build resilient businesses? 
In discussing this question, the study dealt with several themes. The literature 
review in Chapter 2 profiled the contemporary independent fashion designer, 
demonstrating how practice is affected by politics, place, technology, globalisation and 
the complexity of the fashion system. The contextual review in Chapter 3 considered 
the opportunities and challenges faced by the local designer. Chapter 4 examined the 
interconnectedness of fashion knowledge, explored business support mechanisms and 
described the development of an online support resource. This chapter now discusses 
the study findings, which are distilled at the intersection of the previous three chapters. 
Although the study drove towards building an innovative support mechanism with 
more capabilities than platforms already available, it found that two factors are critical 
in building resilience and stabilising the uncertainty designers face. One factor that 
appears to underpin resilience is mindset, and the elusive concept of enterprise 
orientation. The other is, as hypothesised, suitable access to information (hence the 
eMentor).  


---

 
170 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
These two central themes are presented here in separate sections. Part 1 covers 
the designers’ approaches to enterprise, the ongoing tension between the creative and 
the commercial, unconventional business models and the influence of the environment. 
It also discusses how various parameters can be stabilised through design thinking. It 
deliberates on the acquisition of soft skills and the effect of the entrepreneurial 
mindset. Part 2 deals with information, the interconnectedness and abundance of 
fashion business intelligence, the need for effective access and an adaptable support 
platform. It addresses the application of advanced technology and the 
commercialisation of the platform initiated by this study. Beyond these themes, the 
chapter also reflects on my professional development within the framework of the 
DCI, industry engagement and dimensions for further research.  
Empowering designers to take control of their own problems while 
acknowledging that their business models may diverge from the mainstream is a strong 
theme of the study. Regardless of whether a business is large or small scale, its 
resilience is its ability to quickly adapt to disruptions while maintaining continuous 
business operations, avoiding vulnerability and maintaining viability. Importantly, 
combining lessons from conventional business models with the experiences of new, 
small-scale business pioneers provides this path forward, potentially smoothing some 
tension between commercial and creative pursuits. 
PART 1: APPROACHES TO ENTERPRISE 
This study began by profiling the characteristics of independent designers and 
their business models. Designers in the study retained the mindset that they were 
deficient at business, laying blame on their lack of business ability if they were unsure 
of industry procedure or convention or encountered setbacks. As one designer from 
the focus group recalled: ‘I never really knew what I needed to ask, because it was so 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
171 
small and I just felt like I was nutting everything out slowly myself’ (Designer 2 CEA 
2017). Yet designers are more multifaceted than the ‘average’ business owner because 
they perform additional, wide-ranging tasks. They are not only the innovative creators 
of new fashion products, season after season, but are ‘entrepreneurial thinkers, 
recognise opportunities, take calculated risks, are resourceful, passionate and 
understand their capacity within the dynamic fashion environment’ (RMIT 2018, 1). 
Fashion entrepreneurs learn by doing testing, developing and implementing business 
ideas (Aspers and Skov 2006). Nonetheless, they rarely identify as business experts 
and few understand the imperatives that underlie business theory. To a business 
professional, titles, categories and models hold a clearly understandable logic (Raffo 
et al. 2000); not so for the designer. The data collected in Chapter 4 from the 
workshops and discussions with designers show that when dealing with business 
matters, designers become lost in a myriad of commercial concepts, considering and 
interpreting their enterprise from many different angles. This became evident in the 
study workshops, where everyone disagreed on the titles, reasoning and workflow for 
the website development, not to mention distinct requirements for each business. But 
I tender that this is merely a symptom of an underlying misconstrued approach towards 
business activity. 
Designer-Centred Orientation 
The widely accepted framework in business studies known as enterprise 
orientation typically examines both psychological and operational dimensions, such as 
risk-taking, pro-activeness and innovativeness, when applied to conventional business 
modelling (Runyan, Droge and Swinney 2008). But for the fashion entrepreneur, these 
are simply baselines. Mills (2011) provides more discrete orientations for creative 
enterprises as distinct from ‘normal’ enterprises through an original conceptual 


---

 
172 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
framework that delineates three separate orientations for creative businesses: 
‘Creative-enterprise-orientation, Creative-business-orientation and Fashion-industry-
orientation’ (Mills 2011, 257). Several other researchers identify similar variations on 
creative business orientation. For example, in his study on Canadian independent 
designers, Ott (2012) asserts that the fashion design sector can be broken down into 
artistic, commercial and artisanal segments, and describes the artisanal designer as 
both designer and business person. By contrast, Choi (2003) identifies differences 
among independent or ‘boutique’ fashion designers by classifying them as either 
design-oriented, business-oriented or a combination of the two. Referring to the 
design-oriented group as ‘artisan-designers’, she describes them as ‘artists, rather than 
business owners. They believe their work to be an artistic process and their final 
products, designed garments, the result of their artistic creativity’ (Choi 2003, 6). This 
description aligns with Elizabeth Bye’s (2010) definition, which emphasises the 
artisanal designer as a ‘fashion-artist’. Most recently, the AFC report by PWC (2015) 
recommends that designers have three capabilities: artistic (creative), vocational 
(know-how) and entrepreneurial (risk-taking). Commonly, these studies recognise that 
independent designers work across all three of these areas, with tendencies to lean 
more into one approach than another, depending on their strengths or preferences. 
Essentially, these models follow the established view that designers are frustrated by 
their twofold obligation to embrace creative and commercial undertakings 
simultaneously.  
Beyond this perceived interruption to workflow—that is, alternating between 
creative and commercial activity—the study found that designers have acquired a 
counterproductive business mindset. By not believing in their own entrepreneurial 
abilities, they carry a constant anxiety that their businesses should be more successful 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
173 
than they really are. This is explicitly stated in Brittany’s comments in Chapter 3, but 
less evident in the remarks of other designers interviewed. For example, one designer 
noted her lack of confidence with business responsibilities: 
It’s probably one of the scariest things for start-ups. Employing, costing, legal 
stuff. Because we’re creative people, we don’t want to know about it at all 
(Designer 4 CEA 2017). 
Clues of a counterproductive mindset also lie in designers’ constant search for 
diversification and new avenues for revenue. For example, it is surprising that the 
highly acclaimed local couture designer Paul Hunt has embarked on a basics line, 
presumably to boost sales or exposure or both (See Chapter 3, p. 98). Faith in a clichéd 
career trajectory is a mindset initiated in fashion education programs (training.gov 
2018), furthered by business theory publications (Granger 2012) and, according to 
McRobbie (1998), confirmed by popular media expounding ideal scenarios of what 
comprises a ‘successful’ fashion business. Much to their chagrin, researchers such as 
Larner and Molloy (2010) observe fashion reporters at once feeding from and fuelling 
the assumption that designers hope to succeed in a dream career but are most likely to 
fail.  
The business orientations discussed above take both psychological and 
operational dimensions into account. The psychological elements related to their 
creative activities, such as motivations for self-expression and self-actualisation, are 
based on the designer’s creative orientations. Yet the psychological elements behind 
their business approaches or mindset have been largely overlooked. Mindset is about 
belief: belief in what can and cannot be achieved, and under what circumstances 
(Neck, Neck and Murray 2018). It encompasses faith in one’s own ability and in one’s 
surrounding environment, networks and circumstances. Most designers in this study 


---

 
174 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
have a creativity mindset or, in Mill’s (2011) terms, creative-business orientation, 
which they favour at the expense of a positive business mindset or entrepreneurial-
orientation. Greater business confidence is required to counter this currently pervasive 
attitude, and would also help shake the unfavourable reputation for business naiveté 
that independent designers and creative businesses endure. Nonetheless, often without 
realising it, successful creatives use both a business and creative approach to their 
advantage (Leconte 2018). For example, according to Leconte (2018), many 
advertising creatives use scientific morphological analysis, otherwise known as the 
‘Zwicky method’ (Zwicky 1969), to solve marketing problems, while corporate 
enterprises use creative design thinking to solve business problems (Brown 2009). 
Designers operate under the misconception that they are not capable business people, 
even though their very creativity (researching, analysing, evaluating and problem-
solving to arrive at a new idea) counters this notion. 
The impression of what a fashion designer or business should be is at odds with 
the reality of fashion businesses today. Luckman (2015) describes several simple and 
even naïve businesses—for example, those run from spare rooms by stay-at-home 
mums. It is surprising how many businesses remain in this embryonic stage for a 
protracted period. Mulvagh (1998) reports one especially high-profile instance, stating 
that Vivienne Westwood lived in a council flat until the early 1990s. Yet this ‘business 
cringe’ surrounding simple business models is unfounded. On the contrary, this may 
well be the new standard for business. To be resilient, the mindset required is ‘doing’ 
rather than ‘being’—that is, to ‘make a living’ and not necessarily to ‘be a famous 
fashion designer’ (Aspers and Skov 2006). The Business of Fashion (2015) depicts 
fashion business trajectories splitting into two scenarios. It explains that although 95% 
of fashion businesses are doomed to failure within the first year, many start again. 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
175 
Those that sustain viability then become one of two types: scalable organisations that 
have influence (in other words, ‘famous’ fashion designers), or those that maintain an 
optimal size and simply continue to garner regular income year after year. Much like 
the ‘invisible’ designer discussed in Chapter 2, it is these non-famous or ‘invisible’ 
businesses that have been of interest to this study and provide the framework for new 
benchmarks.  
As claimed earlier, business confidence mindset is lacking in education and 
public discourse surrounding fashion business. An outcome of this study will be to 
dispel the notion that a creative practitioner cannot be a good businessperson. Rather, 
this research hopes to dissolve the barriers fashioning this binary and increase the 
designers’, and their intermediaries’, positive businesses attitudes to their advantage. 
Design Thinking 
At this point, the study findings show that elusive factors such as mindset 
towards business competencies affect designers’ decisions and activities and, 
consequently, the resilience of a business. Surprisingly, designers take some aspects 
of their natural business abilities for granted. These include their resourcefulness and 
ability to interpret and creatively adapt to new situations and solve problems through 
design thinking. Remarkably, designers do not proactively apply their innate design 
thinking abilities to their businesses, although design thinking poses an alternative or 
supplementary business management method. Brown (2009) and colleagues of the 
innovations organisation Ideo have pioneered the application of design thinking 
methods for corporate business. As noted in the literature review, design thinking 
encompasses the five main steps of empathising, iterating, ideating, prototyping and 
testing. Perhaps it is assumed that creative businesses naturally employ this technique, 
but I found little evidence of its application among the designers I interviewed. Rather, 


---

 
176 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
they admitted to ad hoc and crisis management scenarios. Once again, this may be yet 
another false assumption about approaches to creative business on the part of both 
designers and their observers, and requires addressing in the education and/or support 
offered to creative businesses. I contend that designers must be encouraged to use and 
consciously employ their natural abilities to ‘design think’ through problems not only 
for creative challenges, but for business challenges, thus changing their mindset. In 
turn, this will bolster their business self-perceptions, confidence and, consequently, 
their resilience.  
Soft Skills 
Beyond the creative‒commercial duality and design thinking approaches, 
designers would also benefit from developing interpersonal skills. Although I observe 
the designer as predominantly at the centre of the business structure, I found that 
designers in this study did not seek information about their personal capabilities. The 
industry experts believed this information could be found elsewhere (see Chapter 4), 
and the focus group designers did not prioritise ‘self-discovery’ in the discussion on 
the menu tab ‘YOU’ (see Chapter 4). Yet researchers such as Bridgstock (2012) stress 
the importance of soft skills or, as Bridgstock calls them, meta capabilities in running 
successful creative careers. The case for including self-development (and the section 
on ‘YOU’ for the website) goes beyond developing personal skills and, as Rae (2009, 
296) puts it, to ‘the personal development of their entrepreneurial identity and 
emergence, as (in some but not all cases) they recognise they are becoming 
entrepreneurs’. Unlike either creative or business skills, these include psychological 
and interpersonal aspects such as empathy with others and communication skills. 
Unsurprisingly, the majority of content in fashion media and self-help resources 
provide numerous personal interest stories on designers’ individual attributes and the 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
177 
effect of the aforementioned psychological/interpersonal aspects on their success 
(Giordano 2017). Therefore, I argue that although the designers in this study are very 
conscious of their creative abilities but seem bent on solving operational and supply 
chain issues as a priority, ideally, they could concurrently improve their interpersonal 
skills. That designers avoid this learning points again to the strain they perceive in 
acquiring skills beyond the creative. Indeed, Ott (2012) and Mills (2011) explain the 
phenomenon of business identity and designers’ perception of their businesses, but not 
of themselves. As though there is a void at the ‘eye of the storm’, the business 
circulates around the designer, but the designer does not critically analyse themselves 
and their own needs in the context of their identity, personal attributes and position 
within the business. Importantly, the effect this has on the designer’s relationship with 
their lifeline, the customer, is disregarded.  
Customer-centred orientations do not appear in the studies mentioned above, 
either. Without customers, no revenue can be gained, and without revenue, there is no 
business. Besides not prioritising profitability, few designers in the study discussed 
their market base and its relationship to sustained income. One could argue that success 
happens when a designer’s creative expression coincides with the preferences of the 
target market at the right time (for example, in the case of Gail Sorronda). This 
serendipitous occurrence is what Caves (2000) has termed the ‘nobody knows’ 
characteristic of creative industries: nobody knows the exact formula for a successful 
product, which explains why so many ‘talented’ designers do not seem to make it. But 
this could be allayed, to an extent, if the designer considered the relationship with the 
customer more purposefully.  
Independent designers are designer-centred, not customer-centred. Participants 
in the study spoke about their customers only in relation to their reaction to designs. 


---

 
178 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
Designers see their output as an extension of themselves; therefore, they appear to 
view customers as personal judges of character or creative ability. This demonstrates 
that designers are self-oriented rather than using a more business-oriented approach 
and turning their enquiry towards the market to ask what customers want, need and 
desire. For example, Gail recalled her encounters with customers in her boutique when 
they did not realise that Gail was the designer; she recounted their critique of her 
designs and said she took this on board as valuable input to her process. However, she 
expressly stated that she does not follow the customers’ preferences (as is the case with 
custom clothing designers, for example), but rigorously maintains her own style and 
creative process. Indeed, creatives are often introverts, and shy away from direct and 
regular customer contact (Higgs and Hender 2004). Once again, this reveals the 
designer-centric (rather than customer-centric) nature and beliefs of the independent 
fashion designer. Admittedly, this distinguishes the independent designer from the 
commercial brand, but in many cases, it also highlights a commercial flaw that 
independent businesses must address. Although design leaders such as Gail Sorronda 
are successful in their ventures, this approach does not work for all fashion businesses. 
Therefore, approaching business support from a purely designer-centric perspective 
may also be hazardous. Some of the new business models encountered in the study are 
orienting their businesses more towards building customer relationships and, indeed, 
finding common ground through sharing values and beliefs. But striking the balance 
between commercial demands and customers’ approval is still difficult interpersonal 
territory for many independent designers. Therefore, this easily fractured relationship 
requires skilled support. Viewed from my perspective as a business owner, and 
admittedly as an owner who ran a customer-centric business model, these 
communicative, customer-centred or ‘people’ skills are crucial. These were some of 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
179 
the early lessons I learnt with difficulty, and remain among the top skills in fashion 
business success (Kangan Institute 2018). Hence, the complex interplay of personality, 
motivations and desired results must be adroitly managed by the designer, the business 
structure and ultimately the entire support environment.  
Although this study finds that most designer businesses are designer-centred, 
and claims that this should be viewed as an acceptable situation, evidence emerged 
from this study that to be truly successful and resilient in business, designers need 
awareness and some fluency in other skills and business orientations. If they remain 
solely designer-centred, they are vulnerable to challenges. The designer’s stratagem is 
to know all that is required and have the mindset to accept and understand this and the 
motivation to embrace all these perspectives simultaneously. The job of support 
mechanisms is also to understand these perspectives and provide the right guidance 
accordingly. This can be achieved via a step-by-step roadmap, which leads from the 
familiar (e.g., design) to the difficult (e.g., sales and accounts). This building-on-prior-
knowledge method is a common theory in education (Piaget 1970; Vygotsky 1978). It 
also aligns with andrological (adult education) principles of self-directed rather than 
expository (lecture-style) learning, and offers a transition from education to business 
(Knowles 1970). Applying these principles, the updated support resource proposed in 
this study can attain a balance between the intuitive or tacit and hands-on or explicit 
knowledge. 
The Fashion Business Environment 
In this study, the fashion designers not only expressed a perceived lack of 
business ability, but some had misgivings about their business environment. This 
included their geographical place within a country and/or locality with its own 
economic conditions, and within a community, which may or may not be 


---

 
180 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
geographically bounded. As examined in the literature review, place is defined by 
various factors, and fashion place is layered with even more determinants (Rantisi 
2010). Although they constitute only a small sample, I contend that the designers in 
this study speak for the larger population of practitioners found in other non-fashion 
cities. I argue that Brisbane is a non-fashion place not only because no international 
fashion events take place in the city, but because it has scant influence on the fashion 
world culturally, nationally or internationally. Further, its supplier infrastructure and 
number of significant fashion businesses is minimal. However, regardless of 
Brisbane’s fashion status, its many disadvantages and designers’ complaints about the 
city, practitioners are managing to develop and grow their small businesses in this 
perceived non-fashion environment. I argue that local designers have surpassed 
reliance on the city and expectations that it bestow them with fashion credibility or 
even provide a business foundation. They are forging ahead regardless and proactively 
contributing to their own resilience, as evidenced in earlier chapters. The problem with 
place is merely one of perception—although designers and observers may think their 
place influences their resilience and success, this is not necessarily the case. 
Designer brands as diverse as Gail Sorronda and Alice Nightingale are 
demonstrating that there is a place, position and customer for every would-be label, 
even in a small city, and that these labels do not have to be inspired or affected directly 
by their locality. By developing designs that appeal to niche tastes, Gail Sorronda and 
Alice Nightingale have discovered or created goods for markets in their own right, 
regardless of their location. If anything, the designers count on the trending principle 
of ‘made local’ rather than ‘made in Brisbane’, and devoted customers obligingly 
support home-grown production where they can. Will Brisbane become a fashion city? 
Perhaps. This question is still relevant not only to Brisbane, but many other cities on 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
181 
the periphery of fashion. Regardless of the globalisation of fashion and emergence of 
digital ways of creating and selling fashion products, independent fashion designers 
and their customers see fashion practice as a multi-facetted activity, providing overall 
value in a combination of creativity, originality, quality, reputation and acceptance. 
Significantly, in light of this study, it is more important to understand how designers 
manage to eke out their living and maintain a positive and productive position, 
regardless of their city’s size or fashion influence. This answers the propositions from 
other researchers cited in Chapter 2, such as those of Skov (2011) and Larner and 
Molloy (2007), who question and tend to negatively interpret the effect of ‘not-so-
global cities’, particularly compared with established fashion centres. It appears rather 
that only after a solid foundation of independent designer activity and success has been 
established in a city can progress be made towards gaining fashion-city status. In 
Brisbane’s case, this may still take some time. 
PART 2: ACCESS TO INFORMATION 
An accurate understanding of the influences surrounding small-scale fashion 
business is essential to sustaining adequate support, whether via one-on-one 
mentoring, customised DIY offerings, industry body programs, formal education or 
government policy. Support mechanisms can take many forms, and are still evolving 
alongside emerging and unusual business practices. This study found that in the 
context of the fluctuating economy, support cannot be set in stone as may have been 
the case in the past—for example, in the form of courses or self-help books. Instead, 
knowledge is constantly shifting. The challenge is to capture the right knowledge at 
the right time and apply it to the appropriate business model. Beyond a hybrid form, 
support itself must be fluid and adaptable, constantly updating and adjusting to the real 
world on behalf of the designers it serves. 


---

 
182 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
Interconnected Fashion Intelligence 
The designers in the study found that besides troubleshooting immediate issues, 
creatively or otherwise, the amount of fashion and business knowledge required to run 
a small business is overwhelming. To make sense of the content freely available and 
demonstrate the interconnectedness of business intelligence, I organised information 
into 10 themes of resilience. My proposing and testing of these 10 themes represents 
a contribution to the study knowledge as well as alternate terms of success for fashion 
enterprise. These themes and their categorisation were discussed in workshops and 
reorganised in preparation for developing the Fashion Business eMentor platform.  
The eMentor 
This thesis is particularly concerned with support mechanisms, which means it 
stands to reason to apply some of the theories discussed in the previous section on 
business approaches to constructing the eMentor platform. In its current form, as 
presented in Chapter 4, the draft website for this project appears very corporate and 
undeviating. Therefore, following a more creative business-oriented standpoint, I 
propose a redesign of the platform architecture. For example, navigating from the 
inside out may prove useful, not only for helping designers access relevant 
information, but for capturing their sensibilities and innate problem-solving abilities 
when dealing with business complexities. Allowing the designer to organisationally 
remain at the centre of the virtual landscape, and all the activities to radiate, in larger 
or smaller proportions from the centre or, conversely, moving from the periphery to 
the centre, may prove effective. This radial style of website design is best visually 
represented in a non-linear space, such as by a cloud of interconnected nodes that 
designers can access from any point. This format would accommodate the creative 
process and, just as there are limitless ways to design fashion (Dieffenbacher 2013), 
so too are there several ways to approach creative business operations and the 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
183 
information required at any given point. Rather than presenting conventional 
assumptions about ideal business models from the top down, this approach initially 
aligns with a designer-oriented perspective. In this way, the designer can make sense 
of the information, and the support mechanism can gradually lead the designer to more 
difficult concepts such as customer orientation and business reporting. The eMentor 
aims to empower business owners to re-evaluate their business abilities and, rather 
than avoiding them, be inspired to develop them by building business confidence 
incrementally, starting from a point of the known and familiar.  
This may also solve the problem of not knowing where to place content when 
concepts overlap, a situation that caused confusion in all of the data collection sessions 
in Chapter 4. This more sophisticated visual representation of data (shown in 
rudimentary form in Figure 28) could be achieved in collaboration with data 
visualisation scientists. Pictured below is an imagined ‘dandelion’ style representation 
(Figure 29). Already seen in application (30), (view example here and here), this 
interactive website software shows various concepts grouped around a central theme, 
reducing the linear or hierarchical importance of tabs and inviting a ‘dive in anywhere’ 
approach.

Figure 30: Kate Ray: Website generated with 3D modelling software Thortspace 
 
This model has not been tested, but invites future opportunities for research and 
development. The designer- or founder-centred model challenges conventional 
enterprise theory (Buckley 2016), schematically representing the standpoint that 
independent small-scale fashion business can be conceived from another perspective. 
Thus, coincidentally aligning with the neo-liberal philosophies discussed in Chapter 2, 
the onus shifts onto designers to take responsibility for connecting with the knowledge 
and support they need. Rather than following a prescribed list, referring to how-to 
business structure models or defaulting to third-party experts such as business 
consultants, lawyers, accountants and conventional top-down business planners who 
will administer a classic business theory top-down or bottom-up approach, the 
designers can become empowered and gain confidence by developing self-generated 
solutions. The advantage of a non-linear cloud is that it reveals the interconnectedness 
of the business, meaning that exploring one aspect of it is always contingent on the 
need to understand its relationship with other aspects, as demonstrated in the Scapple 


---

 
186 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
exercise in Chapter 4. If support is also viewed as interactive, the non-linear cloud has 
the added advantage that designers can contribute and improve on original ideas in 
business modelling. Based on this theory, the online resource proposed for the study 
is therefore poised to add to their support tools, incorporating state-of-the-art 
applications and providing a solution for designers, by designers13.  
Through building a database of users, the interactive style of the proposed 
platform invites engagement, co-creation and user innovation, thus benefitting from 
an interactive ecosystem of designers and stakeholders and surpassing geographical 
boundaries.14 This organic evolution of the platform aligns with the premise emerging 
from this thesis that knowledge is gained and shared, and learning is a process of 
determining what questions to ask. Essentially, independent designers do not need 
more prescriptive how-to methods. Rather, they need a method for locating 
information independently. A platform with effective filter mechanisms and intuitive 
capabilities combined with community or human support will ensure that the 
information they access is effective and credible. Unlike an undeviating document, this 
mechanism is a self-perpetuating living record and provides the foundation on which 
communal design thinking for business building can take place. 
My assumption at the outset of this study was that if designers had a definitive 
resource, they could use it to support their business venture and thereby maintain a 
regular, long-term livelihood—in other words, resilience. Insufficient data were 
gathered on the long-term effect of support and represents a limitation of the project, 
but provides opportunity for future investigation. Nonetheless, the designers 
                                               
 
13 See appendix L for further details on the project’s commercialisation. 
14 Although the content is English language-based (with links to English language resources), it 
can be adapted to include other languages. 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
187 
interviewed already see the value of having continuous access to this resource, both 
now and at any time in the business lifecycle.  
Adaptable Fashion Business Support  
Beyond input from expert community members, the professional rigour and 
credibility of content necessitated a guarantee of reliable information. Further, 
according to the designers interviewed, it is important to create tailored content 
specific to genre, needs and location, otherwise that content is considered generic and 
unhelpful and is consequently dismissed. The data collected indicate that local issues 
are important and users require specifics to be signalled—for example, Australian tax 
obligations and legislation. One designer asked: 
So is this going to be like a global thing? I was just reading ‘British Fashion 
Council’ Tax, and I was wondering, how is that going to help me? (Designer 
1 CEA 2017). 
This would require harnessing sophisticated search engine functionality, and 
these concerns led to further research into Web 3.0 technology in this study. 
In the past, Web 2.0 improved the functionality of the static Web 1.0 by offering 
interactivity and e-commerce possibilities. However, the keyword-based search of 
Web 2.0 technology resulted in information overload. Web developers have 
recognised that no real tools exist to manage the superset of data that has accumulated 
on the internet since the early 1990s (Shirky 2010). The development of Web 3.0 has 
introduced the exploration of the semantic web, which attempts to make meaning of 
data by applying filters that aggregate information though Dewey-style contextual 
searches (Shirky 2010); but according to Weinberger (2007), this is not working, as 
the filters are not always relevant. However, the development of AI, made more widely 


---

 
188 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
accessible as recently as 2016, has changed this approach (Vetrov 2017). This 
‘machine learning’ technology15 is currently emerging. Some forms of this advanced 
technology will soon be available ‘out of the box’, overriding the need for the filter 
system of the semantic web. Intended to be self-sufficient, this technology could 
answer some of the concerns posed during the development of the prototype, including 
the requirement for live updates. Thus, algorithm-driven design is an exoskeleton for 
web product designers today, increasing the number and depth of decisions achievable. 
At the moment, using WIX makes it possible to test Advanced Design Intelligence. 
WIX ADI can recognise, for example, that the web designer is interested in fashion 
and therefore finds similar information on the internet. The grid.com has comparable 
software capabilities.  
Applying Web 3.0 technology requires experts in the field and is beyond the 
scope of this study. The prototype was prepared to demonstrate the anticipated 
applications to potential interdisciplinary collaborators. The model can also be adapted 
to large- and small-scale enterprises and other specialisations, an attribute that will be 
expanded in the future development of the platform. Place-specific adaptations could 
benefit designers located in non-fashion centres. The larger vision for this purpose-
built website will encompass state-of-the-art technology, engaging AI for a global user 
base. 
Commercialising the Research 
Funding the technologically advanced version of the website through applying 
AI may be achieved through commercialising the project. Following the design 
thinking process, the prototype constructed within the confines of this study is the

The Fashion Business eMentor 
189 
precursor to its next iteration: a minimal viable product for commercialisation. This 
means that even prior to adding advanced technology, the website will be revised with 
a marketable substructure that can begin to generate income. Monetising the platform 
will ensure its longevity and affect the wider design enterprise community. Although 
not an initial intention, the idea of commercialising the platform did not seem foreign 
to the participants in this study. On the contrary, they often raised the question of 
whether it would be subscriber-based. Therefore, after discussions with the business 
experts, the platform redesign will integrate a semi-subscription model. Based on the 
Business Model Canvas by Osterwalder and Pigneur (2010), Appendix L: Business 
plan (adapted from the Business Model Canvas, Osterwalder and Pigneur 2010) 
presents the platform’s revenue-generating capacities, resources and stakeholders in 
detail. 
Directory-style resources such as this do exist in tourism and human resources, 
but few highly researched and developed products exist in the creative industries. 
Similar websites and close competitors in the fashion sector are listed in Appendix N: 
Pitch deck. These include startupfashion.com (US), successfulfashiondesigner.com 
(US), fashionstudentsonline.com (AUS) and fashionequipped.com.au (AUS). 
However, none of these resources meet all the requirements of the local independent 
designer. The business plan shows that commercialising the research also encompasses 
adapting it to other creative sectors. Additional creative industries that could benefit 
from the resource include entertainment, architecture, landscape design and interior 
design. These parallel sectors have their own complexities and necessitate 
collaboration across communities and connections with specialist suppliers. A plan for 
incremental research funding that evolves into scaling the application of the resource 


---

 
190 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
to other creative industries, as well as internationally, is presented in Appendix M: 
Funding schedule and activities.  
Industry Engagement 
Researching and developing the website represents the study’s practice 
component. As a practical application, the website also represents an artefact that has 
the potential to contribute to industry. Its continued growth and adoption can be 
ensured through a combination of commercialisation, research funding mechanisms, 
government sponsorship and industry engagement. The resource will be pitched to an 
organisation that supports fashion industry development, such as the newly formed 
AFC, the New Garde or the QUT CEA Fashion Accelerator. Organisational adoption 
can initially be achieved by developing the fashion community membership database, 
thus providing evidence of the website’s utility and effectiveness to both designers and 
the other members of the target market listed in Chapter 4, including educators, 
incubators and mentors. The aim of the pitch is to convert this evidence of impact, 
encouraging the organisation to either purchase the concept and business (including 
proprietary algorithmic programming) outright, or incorporate the resource into their 
business model and subsidise its further development. By funding the addition of more 
advanced technology, and my potential role as advisor, the organisation would ensure 
its accessibility to a larger population of design entrepreneurs. I have already begun 
this process by taking part in the pilot program ‘Thinq’, a research commercialisation 
initiative created by the QUT HDR team and CEA.  
Professional Development 
Following Polyani’s (1944) philosophy that we see the universe from the centre 
within ourselves, in the case of this project, I find myself very much at the centre of 
the problem/s described, as they reflect on my own actions and business decisions as 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
191 
well as those of my fellow practitioners. Thus, rather than sociological research, I 
undertook reflective practice for this DCI study.  
Embarking on this project allowed me dedicated time to reflect on research 
questions without interruption from operational procedures, as was the case when I 
was solving my own business problems ‘on the fly’. In a sense, this study not only 
investigated small-scale independent local designers, but my former business 
challenges as well. Effectively, I asked myself in hindsight how I could have had a 
better business. Would everything have run more smoothly and would my returns have 
been better if I had access to easily findable information? To an extent, the answer is 
‘yes, that would have solved this or that problem perfectly’, potentially changing the 
course of my business at times. During the course of the research, I was surprised at 
how much information is readily available to designers, how few designers are aware 
of this, how professional the information is and the fact that it is constantly increasing.  
I also considered my students and mentees and asked myself if they would have 
felt the same enthusiasm about these discoveries. Interestingly, I think not—some are 
not ready to understand what it is to be an independent designer or, more specifically, 
creative entrepreneur, nor what they want and when they need it, an observation also 
made by Raffo et al. (2000). However, a more seasoned colleague who has spent as 
many years in business as I have believes there is always something to learn, 
particularly in the constantly fluctuating creative economy. For mid-career designers 
Alice and Gail, the effect of this research is perhaps less significant. They are currently 
in control of their businesses, have a routine, expect a certain income and hope that 
things keep going the way they are at present. Therefore, the eMentor resource that 
emerged from this study cannot be everything to all people all the time. As Alice and 
Gail continue to progress, they may move through their current calm waters and meet 


---

 
192 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
new challenges—only as the business grows, so too will the problems. At that point, 
they may seek a resource with depth that takes their venture and stage in business 
seriously, offering professional and credible advice that is not only useful, but 
potentially transformational. Does the eMentor have the potential to significantly 
affect a business? Perhaps. Much as designers are constantly seeking ‘insider’ 
information on manufacturing knowledge, their appetite for expert knowledge on 
business structures and systems becomes just as important as their business grows. 
Hopefully, the designer has also developed the personal or meta capabilities to apply 
the knowledge to their particular circumstances and, in this way, improve their 
business resilience. 
Meanwhile, all the secondary stakeholders (listed in the introductory chapter and 
including industry bodies, educators and policy makers) will benefit from a valuable 
reference resource and central knowledge repository. Former fashion organisations, 
clubs and groups such as the fashion mentor group headed by Alex Perry or the AFC 
have come and gone in Australia, for want of a central authority. Many new groups 
and well-meaning consultants are springing up locally, such as Brisbane Fashion 
Month Coaching, the Fashion Business Mentor Program and Modulus Design Fashion 
Consultants, jostling for position in a hierarchy of influence or credibility in the local 
industry. In such a small system, there is little room for competitors, and much more 
need for collaboration. The emergence of ‘human’ coaching initiatives also 
demonstrates that e-mentoring is only part of the solution. Many designers still want a 
real person to guide them and empathise with them. Many more require 
encouragement and a change of mindset and business orientation. Indeed, the human 
exchange of knowledge is arguably far more important than any stone-cold document, 
whether digital or physical. But one-on-one human interaction is almost a temporal 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
193 
luxury. The internet still fills an enormous gap in the need for conveniently accessible 
and abundant information. Thus, all the threads of help that constitute the designer’s 
support environment are more important than any single solution, although a further 
reaching and self-perpetuating attitude is also required on the part of designers.  
Fashion Business Education 
I planned a career transition away from my design practice some years ago. With 
the accumulation of substantial industry knowledge and experience, I felt compelled 
to mentor and teach. Although this project carefully avoided discussions of fashion 
education, I notice that fashion design courses are looking more closely at 
incorporating businesses strategy into the curriculum. Further, an understanding is 
emerging that not all fashion students have the same talents or ambitions (Kingston 
2008; Anderson 2011; Corner 2014, 2015; Hodges and Karpova 2009). Fortunately, a 
diverse array of occupations is available within the fashion system (Granger 2012), 
and expanding as technology has an increasing effect on the industry. A brief survey 
of fashion programs shows that students are expected to learn an entire lifetime of 
fashion business sense in a few short terms. Coupled with their lack of life experiences, 
much of the specialist and jargon-laden information they are given is forgotten (Finn 
and Fraser 2007; Kozar and Connell 2013), and many graduates and their employers 
ask what on earth they learnt at university (Kingston 2008). A by-product that has 
emerged from this research may be that the eMentor closes that gap once students 
graduate. The transition from graduation to starting a small business, in the context of 
shifting economies and advancing technologies, also allows fertile ground for further 
study beyond the scope of this thesis.  
The opportunity to explore support options is one of the reasons why I 
commenced this study. Enlightened by the background research, I now find myself the 


---

 
194 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
custodian of this resource, through which I can collaborate with the larger fashion 
community. I look forward to creating original content and forming a more personal 
relationship with users via the website in its future iteration. The idea of 
commercialising the research has revealed the need to gain new skills and knowledge 
in terms of understanding web design and development and contemporary start-up 
strategies, which I have not previously experienced, and has presented another way 
forward for my research. This new knowledge ventures across disciplines into the 
realms of digital applications, software engineering and modern entrepreneurship. It 
presents a long-term project I can continue to develop. It is also scalable at a specialist, 
international and other creative industry level, offering me opportunities to develop 
products that can be returned to industry and benefit the small business sector. 
Undertaking this research for the DCI program has changed and expanded my career 
direction from business owner to researcher and educator, and closed the loop by 
returning me to the entrepreneur position I held previously, but with new/different 
knowledge, skills and experience. I am now looking forward to developing the 
eMentor project into a platform not yet available to fashion entrepreneurs. This 
research represents a new career trajectory for me within the industry and has set me 
on the path of expertise in fashion business theory and practice.  
Further Research and Recommendations 
The information provided by this research and its practical application may be 
used in the process of knowledge transfer beyond fashion theory and design thinking 
theory and into the diverse fields of small business research and information sciences. 
Designers and their educators can immediately benefit from the resource because it 
can be adapted as a fashion education resource—but the anticipated long-term effects 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
195 
are also to support the creative economy and cultural production as a whole. This 
chapter makes further recommendations as follows:  
• 
build an online community to develop a virtual support network hosted by 
the website 
• 
commercialise the research by building a following (user database) and 
generate income to ensure website self-sufficiency and potential 
profitability  
• 
integrate technologies, formats and a business model for the future 
implementation of the website 
• 
collaborate with interdisciplinary researchers to allow the website to reach 
its potential and become truly innovative, as no other fashion business 
support website is currently known to integrate innovative technology 
• 
apply advanced filters, diagnostics and machine learning 
• 
engage industry for the dissemination of the platform 
• 
facilitate greater engagement of local, state and federal government bodies 
providing specific and informed support and personalised advice in 
conjunction with the eMentor.  
• 
inform government task groups—for example through IBSA or creative 
industries subgroups specific to the industry to help inform policy and 
improve support for small-scale entrepreneurs.  
Such work can include, but is not limited to, articulating and facilitating the 
ongoing development of ways to progressively document, archive and curate data on 
disruptive small-scale fashion business models. This chapter includes the 


---

 
196 
Chapter 6: Analysis 
epistemological value that is emerging from this research, thus adding to small-scale 
business theory and DIY mentoring. Unlike sociologists, who observe designers at 
arm’s length and see them as a disadvantaged group on the whole, affected by outside 
forces, I admit that I am more demanding in my observations of designers. This comes 
from my experience as a design entrepreneur, and our realising what we can expect 
from ourselves. I believe this view represents an important and fresh perspective on 
fashion business conduct, because I can see what can and cannot be potentially 
achieved by designers from an insider standpoint. 
Conclusion 
Alternative and balanced approaches to enterprise arose in this study as an 
essential element in successfully developing small businesses. Although creative 
businesses are considered somewhat simplistic and naive, and may not be taken 
seriously by those in the conventional business world, the main objective for designers 
is that they can achieve a viable and sustained livelihood. Business management and 
ownership is viewed differently within this framework. The study found that rather 
than being presented with readymade answers, designers need to be empowered to find 
their own solutions. However, these solutions must be professionally informed and the 
information readily accessible in a way that makes sense to the designers’ creative 
mindset. By embracing the portfolio of capabilities required to organise and manage 
small business, design thinking and customer-centric rather than purely designer-
centric approaches, professional resources and solutions can underpin resilience by 
building a design community culture in the context of place, but with the advantage of 
the internet’s unrestricted reach.  
This chapter also reviewed the effectiveness of the eMentor support platform 
developed for this study, as well as the requirements for its implementation. It 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
197 
proposed that including funded advanced technology is necessary if the resource is to 
optimise information management, laying out steps for capital acquisition and 
commercialisation of the research. Industry has the potential to benefit from the 
resource, and in turn disseminate and encourage its adoption by designers. 
Aligning with the DCI program, the project has investigated an area of industry 
concern and proposed a potential solution. My own professional development in the 
context of this study shows my circular movement from designer to researcher and 
educator; now, as custodian of the resource, I see further opportunities for research in 
this field. Ultimately, the participants’ acceptance of this project concept shows how 
academic research can discover new ideas that lead to interdisciplinary collaboration 
and industry engagement. The next and concluding chapter summarises the study’s 
contribution to knowledge and presents the final remarks. 
 
 


---

 
198 
Chapter 7: Conclusions 
Chapter 7:  Conclusions 
The overall direction of this research was to find improved ways to support 
independent fashion practice. This chapter assembles the key research findings from 
the study. Gleaned from two projects—collating empirical and textual data, which 
highlighted the need for improved support; a design process that led to the 
development of a website and the testing of that website—the findings of the study 
provide unique contributions to fashion studies.  
This study is the culmination of two research projects and professional practice. 
Project 1 examined the diversity of independent fashion practice in Brisbane through 
environmental scans and interviews with local designers. Findings demonstrate that 
disruptions caused by the digital age have provided both challenges and opportunities. 
Additional results emerged showing a greater diversity of practice than expected, a 
result of both economic challenges and socio-political trends. As Mentor 1 (2017) 
described, ‘this is a super-niche-generation [who] appreciate nostalgia but adapt to 
technology’. This diversity of niches has led to a variety of specialised needs for 
support. Project 2 encompassed textual research to find existing support resources. It 
also encompassed professional practice, which engaged design processes to create, 
develop and test an all-inclusive support offering. By drawing on expertise from the 
local fashion design community, this knowledge was integrated into a draft support 
platform then tested in focus groups with designers. The resultant Fashion Business 
eMentor platform set out to improve access to resources, but assessment of its ability 
to build the long-term resilience of local independent design practice extended beyond 
the scope of the project. This outcome is yet to be confirmed, potentially through a 
longitudinal study once the platform is fully realised.  


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
199 
Although the study culminated in developing a conceptual model for online 
support and its practical outcome, the prototype for the Fashion Business eMentor 
platform, it also revealed that a single, didactic source of information is not the 
designers’ preference. What emerged from the immediate study is that designers want 
and need a combination of resources and support in their environment. Current demand 
suggests a fluid form of professionally organised information, coupled with personal 
delivery and consistent updates. The resource assumes that designers are already 
primed with creative attributes, but are still not reaching their business goals. These 
may include financial viability, marketing reach, operational security and, ultimately, 
business resilience. Just as place does not necessarily guarantee credence or inspiration 
of creative output, as also identified in this study, neither do accessible resources 
provide assurance of business resilience.  
The study also discovered that extrinsic information alone, even if personally 
delivered, is not sufficient. The study recommends that rather than subscribe to a 
linear, business-style approach to enterprise, designers should use their natural design 
thinking and creative problem-solving skills to their venture’s advantage. Their 
personal capabilities, entrepreneurial aptitude and design output ability to meet the 
market at the right time will decide their resilience and success. Applying their innate 
capabilities not only to their creative tasks, but to their business issues, could enhance 
both resilience and business success. In this way, designers may reverse the 
detrimental lack of self-belief in their own business capabilities. This requires a shift 
in mindset away from chronically designer-centred activity and towards balanced 
creative business orientation as well as customer-targeted action. The designer is 
obliged to proactively forge the connections needed in a community of practice to 
further their venture alongside shifting their mindset, embracing the creative and 


---

 
200 
Chapter 7: Conclusions 
commercial challenges of business. This shift can be achieved by actively building 
designers’ positive mindset towards business tasks and informing and influencing the 
intermediaries in the fashion entrepreneurs’ environment, such as their educators, 
mentors, industry bodies, reporters and policy makers. Thus, the eMentor gains a 
secondary purpose as a central and professional repository of knowledge and a change 
agent for stakeholders across the small-scale fashion landscape. Ultimately, the 
designer requires not one but several support mechanisms, with each resource 
functioning as another addition to their cache of business resilience.  
Contribution to Knowledge: Independent Fashion Entrepreneurship 
This study contributes to three concepts underlying creative enterprise and 
expands knowledge on DIY mentorship, coaching and small-scale independent 
fashion design business models. In summary, the contributions to knowledge emerging 
from this study can be categorised as follows: 
Creative Entrepreneurship 
This thesis: 
• 
discovered how the changes in globalisation, technology, laissez-faire 
government politics and new consumer demands taking place in the 
creative economy of the twenty-first century are affecting local 
independent fashion design entrepreneurs 
• 
found that the current creative economy has given rise to innovative and 
nuanced forms of fashion entrepreneurship of varying success 
• 
determined that working in an urban centre not known for its fashion 
credentials minimally affects designers’ creativity, resilience and success 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
201 
• 
mapped and added to data on support systems currently available to 
independent designers, particularly in Australia 
• 
accumulated 10 themes representing the attributes to consider in 
supporting business resilience 
• 
presented a record of small-scale fashion business activity through the 
platform, a living document that also anchors and archives fashion 
business knowledge in Australia. 
DIY Mentorship and Support 
• 
found that DIY mentoring is necessary because designers are isolated from 
peer competitors as well as the global fashion system, which focuses on 
established fashion centres 
• 
recognised that challenges can be met with guided and informed self-
directed learning—as knowledge is gained and shared, learning becomes a 
process of learning what questions to ask 
• 
found that designers want and need both physical and virtual support 
through a new mix of business knowledge, strategy and adapting technical 
innovations 
• 
found that online resources require improved findability, credibility and 
relevance 
• 
proposed, prototyped and tested an online support offering. 
New Small Business Models and Mindset 
• 
recommended that designers engage their innate design thinking and 
creative problem-solving capabilities more fully to their advantage, thus 


---

 
202 
Chapter 7: Conclusions 
dissolving their habitual negative stance and transforming their own and 
their intermediaries’ approach to their business capabilities with a more 
progressive slant 
• 
recognised the role of independent designers in providing cultural richness 
to the local community, which is evolving through changing philosophies 
and approaches to enterprise 
• 
contributed to the scant academic literature by mapping and analysing new 
forms of independent fashion practice in Australia and, more specifically, 
the support mechanisms that surround practice. 
The thesis findings legitimise unconventional but successful business activity, 
placing this trend into an academic context. Similar and emerging DIY business 
mentoring websites demonstrate that the movement towards recognising nuanced 
business model support has already begun (see Chapter 5).  
My professional development across this DCI study shows a circular 
development from designer to researcher, educator and now caretaker of the resource, 
presenting further opportunities for research in this area. This project facilitated ample 
reflexive practice (reflecting and then reiterating an idea), a core epistemological 
element of the DCI program. Importantly, the study’s contribution to knowledge 
proposes a new approach to support methodology for small-scale creative businesses. 
Conducting this study has facilitated a rich ground of reflective practice, and added to 
my recognition as a specialist in the field of small fashion enterprise.  
Conclusion 
This chapter has summarised the study for the DCI program, synthesising the 
findings in the various phases of investigation. It outlined the practical implications, 


---

 
The Fashion Business eMentor 
203 
contributions to knowledge (particularly regarding design thinking applied to small-
scale business practice), technologically driven practical applications, limitations and 
recommendations, a vision for future development and the overall conclusions reached 
through reflective practice. The study contributes to the sparse literature on small-scale 
independent fashion business in non-fashion cities and argues, based on empirical 
evidence, that survival and success is possible even under challenging circumstances. 
It contests the clichéd view maintained by both designers and their intermediaries that 
creatives are not good business owners. On the contrary, it suggests they are very well 
placed to be effective business owners by meeting their challenges with their innate 
creativity. How designers apply these capabilities could be investigated further the 
creative business context. 
At the beginning of my career, I wanted to be a fashion designer. I embarked on 
that journey, not realising how fraught it might be, all the while searching for a guide. 
Through this research, I have reflected how my business would have been affected 
with the support of that strong and knowledgeable imaginary mentor. Because I lacked 
one, I found my own solutions, albeit mostly through uninformed trial and error, just 
as many of the designers I observed in this study are still doing. I agree with the 
wisdom of Master Yoda that ‘the greatest teacher, failure is’, which confirms that the 
best lessons are learnt from experience. However, learning from experience is difficult, 
and a knowledgeable guide could not only smooth the path, but improve the outcome. 
I therefore conclude that designers by their very nature have the ability to solve their 
own problems and maintain resilience, but that the resources to which they have access 
require improvement.  
I now look forward to creating the mentor I wish I had had, even if virtual, to 
help build the business resilience of future independent fashion designers.

---

 
Appendices 
221 
Appendices 
Appendix A 
Studio experiences in Paris and Dresden 
While working at the fashion magazine Madame Figaro in Paris, I developed my 
label in my spare time. This was possible because I had the opportunity of a ready 
supply chain at my doorstep. For example, I had the choice of visiting the African 
quarter around Barbès–Rochechouart to find affordable imports of linen, or the Marche 
St Pierre for factory off cuts or the Rue du Mail (the location of my ‘day job’) to order 
exclusive fabrics from bolts like an old-fashioned mercer, or visit the peak fabric trade 
fair, Premier Vision, which I attended regularly. However, I was not able to conduct 
business or obtain a registration number (and raise invoices) without first attending a 
small business induction course. This was offered at no cost by the local government 
(Mairie de Paris). This course was the beginning of my induction to business. Even 
when I relocated the studio to Dresden, the German system demanded several further 
requirements including compulsory health insurance and payments towards 
unemployment and superannuation, not only for employees, but also for business 
owner/s. Besides highly regulated business systems, I had ready access to specialist 
services for example customised zippers from Spain or Holland, and corsetry 
components from the UK. Networking was easy with a constant stream of likeminded 
entrepreneurs working in collegial competition. There were trade fairs for sourcing as 
well as for self-promotion. With all this prior knowledge I thought I would be very 
successful on my return to Australia, expecting to simply transplant the business. 
Nothing could be further from the reality that faced the business. I did bring knowledge 
of market segment (bridal) trends and imported one Avant-garde label from Belgium. 


---

 
222 
Appendices 
I found that there was no trade fair to market bridal wear to stockists (there are about 
10 within Europe alone each year), instead, a small group of manufacturers and agents 
collectively hire suites in a hotel twice a year, in Sydney and Melbourne for showings 
– behind closed doors. The market was miniscule with several players vying for the 
few viable boutiques that existed in the country. I did not find the stockists I had hoped 
for and instead opened my own boutiques in Sydney, Brisbane and Melbourne. 
Eventually I gained stockists in Western Australia via an agent. The path was difficult 
and shrouded in secrecy. Few designers collaborated and shared their knowledge of 
suppliers and other information (still the case today). Long distances and the small 
market made this a highly competitive exercise, and often led to the frustration I 
experienced as a designer, wishing I could call on a mentor for help or information. 
The business environment was not based in the professionalism I had experienced in 
Europe, not that the European experience was easy, it was simply more proficient. I 
did not have a mentor in Europe ether, but the industry infrastructure provided the 
knowledge and information I needed, when required.  


---

 
Appendices

Appendix B

224 
Appendices 
Appendix C 
Costing calculations 
The price point of what ‘the market will bear’ is based on high street prices of 
mass-produced garments. For example, Alice pitches her prices between those of 
Dangerfield and Gorman at an average of $170, even though her garments should 
average at around $950. Using simple margin calculating formulae, any product 
requiring a single hourly rate calculation of $27 per hour (the average Australian wage) 
(ABS 2016a) plus overheads (not including a surcharge for intellectual 
property/experience/originality) x 3 (commonly accepted mark-up rate for hourly rates 
in small business which allows for cost of sales and overheads) yields $81 per hour. A 
dress similar to Alice Nightingales’ would take about 4-6 hours to cut sew and finish 
in a make-through process (not including design, patternmaking and administration 
time) so the cost should be around $400. Materials and taxes bring the total cost to 
$450. Once presented at market this cost price should be marked up around 210% (an 
average in fashion retailing which covers retail staff, rent, advertising, overheads etc.) 
bringing the true cost to $945. Alice sells dresses for $160 – $180, as this is what she 
believes the market will bear. Below are tables calculating the effect of this pricing 
structure on the overall business, based on average MSE turnover per annum rates 
(ATO 2016).

Appendices 
231 
Appendix E 
Support mechanisms 
I conducted the preliminary desktop review of online sources by looking for 
sections within organisations’ websites that would match the categories listed above. 
This proved difficult because although information was abundant, it was scattered and 
diffuse. Some information was oriented towards large-scale concerns, other 
information was of a generic nature. Some material was at a basic pre-start-up level, 
some was detailed, comprehensible only to ‘insiders’. I encountered theoretical, 
practical and anecdotal text as well as coercive copy to entice purchase of further 
information. As my standpoint for this first search was from a designer/entrepreneur’s 
perspective, I tried a different method, by reversing the process and searching on the 
websites for specific categories. Based on informal discussions with designers and my 
own experience, searching for topics within websites (e.g. for ‘marketing and 
branding’) the search was more likely to align with the way a designer looking for 
specific support, would research a website. I navigated the various tabs and pages and 
then matched them to the areas in the tables, which were based on my 10 themes of 
resilience, providing a short description of each. This was useful as although the 
support entities’ websites appeared sometimes overwhelmingly informative, the 
table/s provided useful categorisation and collation and comparison of data (or lack 
thereof). The tables below provide a summary of the data found. Coloured fields 
identify gaps in information.
