240 
Appendices 
Analysis Table 1 
British Fashion Council 
The BFC has the most comprehensive website. Besides offering physical 
workshops and mentoring events the information accessible online is substantial. 
There are several links that flow to reports and papers including a significant amount 
of information on business development, researched by industry experts. Although the 
resources for designers are broad, local (Brisbane) designers can benefit from the 
online material. This research is only concerned with the online content of the 
organisation’s website. Importantly, all of the ten categories nominated for the data 
collection have been addressed in the BFC website. By colour coding subcategories of 
information into ‘beginner’, ‘established designer’ etc., the BFC website attempts to 
filter the information for the viewer by relevance. However, the information is verbose, 
and requires a significant amount of time reading. A designer may become confused 
having to search too many interfaces to find the information needed. There is no 
interactivity, and there are few instructional videos. There is so much information that 
it would be difficult for a designer to find the right answers in a timely and guaranteed 
manner. Further, although an array of the complexities within the fashion system are 
addressed, they still require adaptation to individual circumstances. For example, 
within one report (‘Commercialising Creativity’) a chapter on finances and cash flow 
is contextualised to fashion, but does not consider the variations in business model 
practice as outlined in Chapters 2 and 3. Hence, the information is findable, but the 
search requires some patience and tenacity. It is credible because of the contributions 
from peak government and industry organisations. It is relevant mostly to London 
designers, but some of the information can be used generically. Nonetheless, the 


---

 
Appendices 
241 
content on this website is very comprehensive and it makes a good starting point or 
model for an interactive and updated support package.  
The Council for Fashion Designers of America (CFDA) 
The focus of the CFDA website is on its charitable activities which raises money 
to support designers, it is clearly aimed at elite, well marketed American designers 
only (and similar to the TFIA in Melbourne, for Melbourne), centred on New York. 
There are several awards available and with its ‘Oscars’ appeal, it rewards US 
designers as a way of fostering their talent and visibility on the market. Information 
that would support small, independent business owners is meagre as the assumed target 
audience of designers is affluent and privileged, and therefore in no need of extra help. 
The information is credible because, similar to the BFC, articles and information is 
contributed from peak bodies or well-known and respected designers. However, 
although the website portrays a glamorous world of fashion design, it is not particularly 
relevant to the small-scale independent designer in Australia.  
The Council for Fashion and Textile Industries Australia (TFIA) 
Uncluttered and easy to navigate, the TFIA website is split into two websites. 
The secondary (older) website contains a sourcing directory and is not updated with 
the same visual appeal or navigation capabilities as the main website. The TFIA has a 
strong focus on small-to-medium enterprises in Australia and an emphasis on 
sustainable practice. It also fosters export opportunities. The organisation is based in 
Melbourne and most of the partners and workshops are in Melbourne. There is a good 
amount of information on product development and supply chains, but none on 
business development (even though this is listed as an aim). There is little information 
on marketing and branding. There is no search bar, except on the blog page. There are 
many headings in the main website but few links. Presumably these are covered in 


---

 
242 
Appendices 
upcoming (but not listed) events. Instead, events and workshops are listed on the TFIA 
Facebook page. Once again, this points to the diffusion of information across platforms 
and the difficulty in finding all the relevant information at any given moment. There 
is no interactivity other than the blog. This organisation is the closest local equivalent 
to the BFC and the CFDA. Although the website has a modern and positive outlook it 
is highly regionalised and focuses on regenerating small to medium manufacture in 
Victoria with sights on export to Asia. The events posted are almost exclusively 
located in Melbourne which does not help the designers outside of the city and should 
be reconsidered if this is a national organisation. In the words of one of the mentor 
interviewed for the study:  
Are the TFIA really helping us in Brisbane? To me it seems a very Melbourne 
based initiative. Very Melbourne oriented. How can we get them to Brisbane? 
How can they help designers nationally? Perhaps bring the workshop to 
Brisbane? (Mentor2 2017) 
This narrows the focus of the organisation. A more comprehensive website, 
including some of the content from the BFC as well as a consciousness of national 
users may increase its value for independent designers. Specific information is not 
easily findable within the website. This is because most of the content consists of 
blogs, announcements and event notifications. It is credible because of the contribution 
from industry experts and current designers and manufacturers. It is relevant to some 
but not all designers nationally because of its Melbourne-centric outlook and because 
it covers several types and sizes of businesses without distinguishing the differences 
for the site visitor. 
Industry.Gov/Small Business Support 


---

 
Appendices 
243 
The Australian Department of Industry has an extensive website of support 
guides, reports, templates and documents. These are of a generic nature as they cover 
small business in all industry sectors. They are based on classic business model theory. 
The user experience is also aimed at the conventional business visitor. Texts are 
effusive and articles neither interactive nor pictorial , although there are some links to 
a YouTube channel about running a small business and some online forms and 
calculators which generate reports. The site is extensive and disorienting as there are 
few back-to or return buttons. There is a search bar but a search for fashion business 
yields several reports on the now discontinued Small Business Grant Scheme, which 
suggests that the site is not regularly updated, or that new updates are simply added 
without removing redundant material. Information relevance to fashion designers is 
negligible. Although the directory does have a filter system it yields a diverse range of 
websites from astronomy to science to manufacture. Of interest on this page may be 
Country of Origin Labelling (this also includes the food industry) or National 
Measurement institute, (again including chemical, legal and biological application). 
Help is available with lodging BAS statements and GST, marketing and branding, 
brand IP and law, but once found requires adaptation to the small creative enterprise. 
Although this platform has an enormous amount of information (arguably more than 
the BFC) it is difficult and time consuming to find relevant information. Altogether, 
the websites of the formal support mechanisms are comprehensive in their content but 
often generalised. They presume a certain, singular clientele, that being the 
conventional/mainstream, albeit small business owner. There is little interactivity or 
ability to find nuanced information for specific sectors.

Appendices 
249 
Analysis Table 2 
Business of Fashion: ’Fashion Foundations’ video series 
Business of Fashion (BoF) is an online ‘insider’ magazine which publishes 
articles on commercial shifts and key appointments within the fashion industry. BoF 
also offers “Fashion Foundations”, including ‘everything an emerging designer needs 
to know about how to start an independent fashion label’ (Imran 2007). The range of 
advice offered is covered with webinar topics such as: ‘Do I really want to start a 
fashion Business: How do you write a fashion business plan: How do you finance your 
fashion business: How do you create brand awareness?’ These modules give general 
descriptions of terms in writing and an accompanying video which is presented in 
lecture format. The information is generalised and assumes that the entrepreneur has 
start-up capital, access to finance and regular cash flow, even though how the designers 
are to accumulate this capital is not explained. A more developed educational/support 
component is planned for the BoF platform but has not yet materialised. 
Startup Fashion 
This website concept is very important to this study because it is the website 
which best covers most of the parameters required by the small business owner. 
Interviews with designers for the study revealed that this was one of the few designers 
they turned to seek business advice. One interviewee even remarked that when he read 
the blogs, it’s as if the writer where leaning over his shoulder watching his every step. 
So, this website provides a degree of findability of information, but once again because 
it is predominantly composed of blogs, it is difficult to find specific information. 
Credibility is provided by the website’s founder, Nicole Giordano who has some 
industry experience. There is some degree of interaction from other designers, and 
Giordano actively encourages the community. However, the credibility of the 


---

 
250 
Appendices 
information seems limited to a peer-to-peer exchange and lacks some of the depth that 
other organisations such as the BFC or industry.gov can provide. For example, some 
of the (pay-walled) templates are rudimentary, as are the business tips. Further, the site 
is published in New York, which again localises the relevance of the information. 
Nonetheless, the site is popular and seems relevant to many young business owners as 
Giordano also presents a subtly rebellious approach, claiming to ‘break the fashion 
rules’ and do it ‘our way’ (startupfashion 2017). 
Creative + Business  
Creative + Business is a consultancy providing paid mentoring, resources and 
assistance for all creative businesses. It is not specific to fashion. Founded in 1996 by 
an individual, film maker, Monica Davidson, the company, then known as Freelance 
Success, has evolved onto a consultancy for a range of self-employed creatives. The 
business offers information, events, workshops and shared spaces. Many of the 
workshops are located in Sydney, although there appear to be an office in Brisbane. 
Information on the website is findable, providing various categories of information. 
The information and resources also appear credible as most of them link back to 
government initiatives for small businesses. However, apart from general small 
business information which can be found directly on government websites, the 
information is not particularly relevant to fashion designers, at least not in key areas 
such as product development and sourcing. It is therefore unlikely that a fashion 
designer will make regular use of this resource.  
Brisbane Fashion Business Meet-Up 
The Brisbane Fashion Business Meet-Up group comprises local fashion 
businesses, entrepreneurs and intermediaries such as PR agencies. It meets at a 
repurposed former restaurant and now shared workspace, Little Tokyo Two. Meet-Up 


---

 
Appendices 
251 
groups are dependent on the activities and initiatives of the organisers. The Brisbane 
Fashion Business Meet-Up group is sporadic in its event organisation. Another such 
meet-up group is the Design to Business group run by Alex Lawton (one of the 
interviewees for this study), and her partner. This pair is very active, regularly hosting 
events of various kinds that may be of interest to fashion business owners. There is no 
theme or agenda to the topics covered by the talks. The organisers are resourceful and 
find interesting individuals to share their stories, thus building a physical community 
around fashion business entrepreneurs. Meet-Up groups provide little online content 
and are aimed at physical social connections rather than delivering information online. 
The websites of these informal means of support are diverse in format and 
function. They appear high on the Google ranking system as they are mostly 
commercially based. That is, their agenda is to provide some free information to 
visitors but also provide more in-depth, ‘insider’, detailed or professional information 
at a price. None of the websites cover all of the aspects listed in Table 7 earlier which 
shows the 10 topics of concern. Instead they are largely blog or journal-article based 
and provide news that is current and topical. Some of this information is useful because 
it is written by fashion experts, but it is difficult to retrieve later.

Appendices 
Analysis Table 3 
QUT CEA Accelerator 
The QUT CEA Accelerator provides a 6-month intensive incubator program for 
a small group of six designers per intake. The website does not provide any 
information or resources for designers. It is merely a marketing tool, advertising the 
benefits of the program. There are some anecdotal testimonials, but it does not have a 
blog or provision for community interaction.  
Fashion incubator programs rely 
on face-to-face interaction with the mentor and mentee in a purpose-built environment. 
One mentor interviewed for this study explains the benefits of incubators: 
I guess confidence building and know-how are the main things. Generally, by 
the time they arrive here, they’ve got the skills and they’re kind of like a little bird in 
a nest, they’re yet to fly. This is a space where I’ve seen them avoid countless 
stumbling blocks or minimise risks and they’ve been pointed just in the right direction 
towards the people that are skilled at helping them.(Mentor1 2017) 
 Some incubator programs are supported by resource rich websites that give 
additional information to designers, either in a bid to entice enrolment in the program, 
or as part of an ongoing service provided by the program.  
Similar to the QUTCEA Accelerator, The Design Residency, The New Garde 
and The Fashion Mentor Program all have little support information on their websites. 
This is because the websites act as marketing avenues and it would not seem in their 
best interests to ‘give away’ information that they have commodified within their 
programs. On the contrary, some other support organisations and providers of online 
courses 
for 
example 
see 
this 
differently.

instance, 
US 
based 
successfulfashiondesigner.com and startupfashion.com provide sometimes very

enticing information that leaves the site visitor curios for more. This may be an 
important attribute in establishing a website’s credibility and relevance for designers.

262 
Appendices 
Analysis Table 4 
Etsy Seller Handbook 
Placed at number 13 on global website rankings (similarweb.com) the Etsy seller 
Handbook provides advice and inspiration for successfully running an Etsy shop. The 
handbook is easily found on the Etsy seller dashboard site and topics are clearly 
defined. Topics covered include: Photography, shipping, getting found, legal, 
productivity, community, branding and marketing, pricing, taxes and finance, growth 
strategies and seasonal tips. The articles although credible, are presented in a semi-
professional manner, assuming that the typical seller is not au fait with sophisticated 
business practice. An article entitled “Are you paying yourself enough?” on the Etsy 
blog, offered friendly advice about setting realistic hourly rates. A template is also 
provided. However, half of the business costs are missing as there are no overheads 
(for example rent, utilities, marketing, bookkeeping, pre-financing production etc.), 
shipping or taxes. This is a very simplified expenses sheet and would not constitute 
best practice for a business. The information is also very generalised and aimed 
towards crafters, which can encompass a number of product types, not just fashion. 
Hence, although the findability of information is good, the credibility and relevance 
for a fashion designer is somewhat generalised. 
Not Just a Label 
NJAL represents a fashion-forward and elite community of designers and their 
highly creative, original and artistic output. Brainchild of founders Stefan and Daniel 
Siegel, the London-based website provides a community of likeminded designers with 
the scope and opportunity to self-present and promote their fashion sensitivities in an 
uncompromising manner. It is elite and select in its fashion tastes. The sense of 
community is strong, but business building information is sparse on the website. Built 


---

 
Appendices 
263 
around images and blogs that discuss fashion theory, marketing trends and aesthetics 
rather than the tin-tacks of business, it lends the participant a sense of belonging to a 
larger ‘in the know’ community, in a sense extending the creative environment 
provided by art school practice into the commercial realm. Information is not filtered, 
blogs appear in date order. They can be searched but the site visitor needs to know 
what to look for. Information is therefore not particularly findable, but it is highly 
credible and relevant to this group of designers.  
The lists above are by no means exhaustive. Many more support services exist 
than are posted here, for example Lynda.com that can help with technical drawing skill 
building, or the online universityoffashion.com which is a subscription service teaches 
basic design skills or the successfulfashiondesigner.com gives in-depth instruction on 
developing garment specification documents. The Kangan Textiles and Fashion Hub 
is a part of TFIA. AFID’s mentoring service: http://www.fashionmentoring.com.au/, 
Melbourne mentoring, The Sample Room: http://sampleroom.com.au/ and The Small 
Business Mentoring Service: https://www.sbms.org.au/ also form part of the landscape 
of support options. The tables above demonstrate the variety, diversity and disparity 
of information available to designers, as well as the need for filtering and organising 
the information in a ‘findable’ manner.  
The investigation of support services on the web show that UK-based resources 
are extensive but pertain to the local environment, providing links and advice on local 
initiatives and government, as is the case with almost all of the websites. Otherwise, 
for instance in the case of BoF, the information is too generic and also assumes a 
certain designer, namely the elite designer with a conventional business model. 
Although plentiful, the information is diverse and fragmented. A nuanced ‘target 
market’ (for example elite, indie or bespoke) is not accounted for except in the case of 


---

 
264 
Appendices 
Etsy. However, the Etsy information was somewhat romanticised and did not present 
best practice.  
The tables provide categorised information on support services available for 
independent designers, including advice and counselling, networking communities, 
industry knowledge and entrepreneurial opportunities. Through examining the 
information available in terms of relevance and accessibility, the findings reveal that 
there are both an oversupply and insufficiency within the various categories. However, 
taking a more semiotic approach to the analysis, and how this affects the user 
experience is also important. For example, government websites are pompous and 
didactic, with mainly written text. BoF is contemporary, glamorous and informative. 
It also has a subscription option, where designers can find more professional insights, 
but the site is mainly geared towards insider information about large 
brands/companies. 
 
 


---

 
Appendices 
265 
The analyses above provide a summary of the information available online, 
conducted from a designer standpoint. A second search was conducted in an advanced 
search for ‘small fashion business support’. However, this search did not provide any 
more professional information than I had already encountered in the initial search and 
points to the questionable reliability of Google searches, even if ‘advanced’. For 
example, the search yielded newspaper articles, opinion editorials and blogs of around 
300-500 words from public audience sites like the Sydney Morning Herald, The 
Guardian and Fashionista.com, with topic headlines such as “5 Tips on Starting a 
Fashion Line”. One article by a blog, fashionista.com claims a list of common designer 
mistakes including: 
• Postage and handling 
• Research 
• Point of difference  
• Marketing  
• and a link to the BFC report “Commercialising Creativity” 
While some of these topics may just happen to be interesting and relevant to a 
designer at the time of a search, results like this merely point to the debatable relevance 
and reliability of some information, or sources of information. These results add to the 
frustration of the time poor designer who already understands the basics and is looking 
for specific support, with more professional/industry current content, rather than either 
academic articles and journalese. 
 
 


---

 
266 
Appendices 
SEARCH 2 
Searching for solutions online 
The aim of this research is to simulate a situation that many designers face, and 
which I often encountered during my business experience. No matter what the stage in 
the business lifecycle, and no matter what previous knowledge and experience I had 
already accumulated, I constantly encountered new problems. These problems arose 
sometimes as the result of my business experiencing a sudden growth (or decline), a 
change in macro-economic conditions or a general shift in consumer sentiment. I was 
unable to consult mentors or peers as this may have compromised perceptions of my 
expertise or my business reputation. There is a degree of embarrassment associated 
with ‘not knowing’ industry-specific information. The internet provides a discreet and 
convenient way of accessing information and acquiring otherwise inaccessible 
knowledge.  
In order to demonstrate this situation, I devised a common ‘designer problem’, 
namely: “how to ensure, maintain or improve cash flow”, and its related matter, “how 
to cost garments for effective cash flow”. As identified in the empirical data collected 
and discussed in the previous chapter, designers have an ad hoc approach to solving 
business issues, through learning by doing and seeking answers for problems as they 
arise. This means that they may ask family or friends first, or reach for information on 
the internet, regarding a specific problem. Some of the main issues of concern for 
designers arising out of the interviews were the costing of garments and cash flow. 
The two matters of costing and cash flow are linked but not inseparable from a 
financial modelling standpoint (NESTA 2016) but for the creative designer, they may 
seem indivisible. Indeed, classic financial modelling is a contentious point in the 
creative industries, with some like McRobbie (2002, 54) describing independent 


---

 
Appendices 
267 
fashion design as ‘experimental semi-entrepreneurial creative practice’ and Mills 
(2011) making the distinction between creative enterprise orientation and business 
orientation. Therefore, the premise of the question is in doubt from the outset. But the 
problem is very real and concerns many designers at any level of experience. Further, 
although the ‘answers’ to this problem are not particularly dependent on trade secrets 
or ‘insider’ information, the results can be complex and require significant 
configuration to be relevant or tailored to individual stakeholders. 
I therefore simulated a designer’s search again and entered the keywords: 
Figure 9: Keyword search testing availability of information on a common designer 
business issue 
This search yielded several articles related to retail clothing16 rather than design 
enterprise, which once again demonstrated the flawed results which are achieved via 
general searches. I then tried an advanced search, removing ‘retail’ and using operators 
(inverted commas, asterisks, wild card). The operators did not change the results but 
without operators, and omitting ‘retail’ I found several useful articles in the advanced 
search. These included the following: 
• 
A two-page PDF article by an accountancy firm, giving general information 
on cash flow in the clothing industry 
• 
A course I can enrol in at RMIT 
                                               
 
16 Again demonstrating the commercial bias of the Google search engine

270 
Appendices 
One link to onlineclothingstudy.com (based in India) lead through to several 
articles to links on garment costing, including samples of costing sheets and a further 
five articles regarding costing. This was a very comprehensive result, however it was 
aimed at large-scale, offshore apparel manufacture. This search demonstrated the 
depth and breadth of information freely accessible to designers, but again showed how 
disperse and unfiltered the information is. Users are left to interpret the findings and 
cobble together a variety of resources to arrive at something that fits their needs. The 
exercise of solving the cash flow problem, not only provided some solutions, but more 
importantly yielded a set of data which demonstrated the diversity of information. 
Finally, as I could see that none of the search results were accessing fashion 
organisations I had listed earlier, I experimented with a DIY search to see how much 
information I could find on “cash flow and costing a garment for small business”. This 
time I conducted the search within the websites listed in the tables of entities above 
because I wanted to ensure credible industry data results. Once again, the search results 
yielded a variety of forms including opinion editorials, blog entries, anecdotal 
recounts, government reports and economic theory papers. The search was more 
onerous than a Google search and took several hours, time which a designer is unlikely 
to spend on finding the solution to a single problem. It also required my industry 
expertise and knowledge of jargon and processes17 to find the information which was 
often deep within the website, or even beyond the website, accessible via links, further 
reading or user generated comments. Some of the information was relevant, detailed 
and credible. I have summarised the information into four tables below, again 
categorised into the four sections listed earlier: formal, informal, consultative,

---

 
278 
Appendices 
The results of this ‘problem’ search demonstrate the depth and diversity of information 
available, with 20 results from within the websites of industry organisations which could 
support or solve the business problems of the design entrepreneur. However, the exercise also 
clearly demonstrates the difficulty of finding this information. Further, doubt has arisen 
regarding the existence of potentially better information which may remain ‘unfindable’. 
Information tailored to the time in lifecycle, size, location and individual situation of the 
designer is almost non-existent, with only the BFC and the department of industry website 
signalling information in colour-coded groups (see Figures 10 and 11 below). The credibility 
and professionalism of information is also not evident and relies on the reader’s intuition or 
experience. Gaps or anomalies in the information arose, including inaccuracies on some 
templates, for example current wage awards, duties, freight costs, taxes etc. and no account for 
local regulations. This points to a danger of false or misunderstood information. The designer’s 
location is therefore a consideration when searching for relevant information and demonstrates 
the need for a location filter. Hence, this second search adds to the argument for the need to 
organise industry-specific information through clearly designed data architecture, allowing 
information to be structured in a findable way through keyword, user based taxonomy, assessed 
for credibility and filtered for relevance and user specific requirements, locations and 
situations.

296 
Appendices 
Appendix J 
Focus group participants and session plan 
Whitelane Textiles / Founder Lorraine White 
Whitelane Textiles is an emerging boutique corsetry brand with focus on classic glamour 
that remains true to traditional construction processes. Catering to burlesque performers, pinup 
beauties and those who seek something special, corsets are individually constructed from the 
finest European coutil and luxurious silks, embodying the glamour of a bygone age. Lorraine 
White is a corsetiere, weaver, knitting dynamo and textile artist based in Toowoomba, 
Queensland. With a passion for preserving textile history and handicraft, she is constantly 
seeking new knowledge and expanding her technical skills to offer her clients something 
special and become an expert in her field. 
Genkstasy / Founder Evie Willsteed 
Genkstasy is the un-gendered alt-luxury clothing label launching in 2017 by Brisbane 
designer Eve Willsteed. Genkstasy (a fusion of /Genki + Ecstasy, translating to something 
along the lines of ‘pure-euphoric-being’, or ‘ultimate-energy-party’) is fashion for trendsetters; 
for those who create and appreciate; for those who lead and for those who walk their own path. 
Reaping an aesthetic born of modern cultural fusion and inclusion with Japanese and hip hop 
fashions at the fore, Genkstasy will aim to change the way we experience and participate in 
fashion, creating a style that is unique but lasting, and clothing that’s ethical and sustainable 
but still fresh. 
SUZII K / Founder Suzanne Knezovic 
Suzanne Knezovic is the founder and designer of namesake label, SUZII K. A fresh, 
unique and contemporary Australian label, creating prêt-à-porter collections. The brand 
focuses on merging visual art and fashion to create a unique point of difference that exudes 


---

 
Appendices 
297 
effortless, chic and on trend ready-to-wear designer pieces. SUZII K is loved and shopped by 
girls in their late teens, travelling women, the fashionista, the everyday girl and the mum. 
Miss Summer / Kathrin Bonfigt 
With beach apparel for the female saltwater enthusiast, Miss Summer aims to prove a 
point against fast fashion. The brand stands for functional, high-quality, sustainable beach wear 
to inspire and support the female that fearlessly follows her own heartbeat and wants to make 
the most of the life she has been given. Through her brand, Kathrin’s ambition is to build a 
platform for events and other projects to help ocean conservation and making the world a better 
place. 
Gloria Dulcie / Alexandra Coleburn 
Gloria Dulcie is a ready-to-wear women’s label focused on representing modern 
femininity. They create quality pieces, hinting at modernist style with delicate traces of 
whimsy. Pieces that are defined by their subtle detail and quality fabric. As supporters of ‘slow 
fashion’, they believe in escaping traditional trend cycles, enabling creative rhythm and 
environmental sustainability. This consciousness allows for the development of exclusive 
collections that are in harmony with what the earth can provide. Now in its second year of 
business, Gloria Dulcie has generated interest from respected creatives, magazines and 
agencies within Australia and internationally. Having secured a stockist, Gloria Dolce has 
grown its online customer base and is a sought-after label at The Village Markets 
Source: QUTCEA. 2017. "The five creative startups at the forefront of fashion at Creative Enterprise Australia." Accessed 
12/011/17. http://qutcea.com/2017/03/02/the-five-creative-startups-at-the-forefront-of-fashion/.

298 
Appendices 
Time 
Activity 
Details 
10 mins 
 
• 
Welcome 
• 
Introductions 
• 
Purpose of 
the session 
• 
Check that 
all release forms 
have been signed 
• 
Ground rules 
We want you to do the talking 
We would like everyone to participate  
I may call on you if I haven’t heard from you in a 
while 
There are no right or wrong answers 
Every person’s experiences and opinions are 
important 
Speak up whether you agree or disagree 
We want to hear a wide range of opinions 
What is said in this room stays here 
We want you to feel comfortable sharing if sensitive 
issues come up 
We will be audio recording the group 
We want to capture everything you have to say 
We don’t identify anyone by name in this report  
you will remain anonymous 
5 mins 
Give the group 
access to URL for 
the prototype 
website.  
 
Time to explore, navigate, take notes if required 
10 mins 
Ask questions 
evaluating website 
1. 
Do you find the website easy to navigate?  
2. 
Do you find the content of the website 
useful to your current business activities?  
3. 
Do you find the content of the website 
relevant?  
4. 
Would you recommend using the website 
to others? 
5. 
Can you describe any challenges that your 
business/practice faces that the website might be 
able to address? 
6. 
Can you suggest any improvements in the 
website’s functionality, useability and relevance?  
7. 
Would you reorder the headings in a 
manner that’s more useful to you? 
8. 
How would you improve on the site’s 
visual appeal? 
10 mins 
Hand out: Pose a 
problem on paper 
and hand to 
individuals 
cash flow projection:

Introduction 
My name is Hilde Heim and I’m currently undertaking research in 
the Doctor of Creative Industries program at Queensland University 
of Technology (QUT) investigating the online support mechanisms 
available to independent fashion designers. 
 
Purpose of the session 
The increase in support available to designers on the internet has 
been prolific, but it has also led to ‘information overwhelm’. For 
this reason, many designers do not access the wealth of 
information available to them, because it is confusing, time 
consuming to search and there is no guarantee of the validity or 
relevance of the information. This is why I have embarked on a 
project to create a comprehensive support website for designers, 
providing them with timely and customised support for their 
business concerns, no matter what the stage is of their business 
lifecycle.  
 
Ground rules 
Thank you for attending this focus group session today. I am 
seeking feedback on how the website I am designing compliments 
the physical support you are already accessing. Feedback from the 
focus group session will be analysed and applied to improve the 
website’s functionality, useability and relevance for designers. The 
session will last for 50 minutes. I will be asking you to view the site 
map (on paper) and the website prototype (online) that is under 
construction, which you can access on your computer at: 
 
http://hildeheim.wixsite.com/mycreativementor 
 
I will ask you questions regarding your interaction with online 
support for independent fashion business as well as your user 
experience in navigating the proposed website. I would like you to 
do the talking, with everyone participating. I would like to hear a 
wide range of opinions. I may call on you if I haven’t heard from 
you in a while. I will pose about 10 questions. There are no right or 
wrong answers. Every person’s experiences and opinions are 
important. Please speak up whether you agree or disagree. I will be 
audio recording the group because I want to capture everything 
you have to say. Please also feel free to write any additional notes 
and comments on this session plan and the site map and return. 
 
It is expected that this project will not benefit you directly, but may 
help you and the broader fashion community to gain a useful 
resource which supports the survival and success of small-scale 
fashion enterprise. It may also provide a convenient extension of 


---

 
302

the physical support you are currently accessing. I hope to pitch the 
idea to a fashion body, like the QUT Fashion Accelerator as a 
compliment to their existing resources. Once complete, I will seek 
funding to continue and develop the website into an interactive 
platform that can constantly update itself in real time, with the 
help of advanced technology (Web 3.0 Artificial Intelligence), 
delivering relevant and timely information to small fashion business 
entrepreneurs. 
 
Please complete the consent forms and the sign-on sheets. The 
sign-on sheets are voluntary and ask for your email address if you 
would like to be contacted in the future for further participation in 
this research. 
 
Thank you in advance for your participation.

Revenue Streams  
1. Banner Advertising space. For example, professional services specialising in fashion law, 
small business set up, accountancy, book keeping, business insurance, or trade events 
like Fashion Exposed, Premier Vision, products like Style Cad, Equipment suppliers e.g. 
Capron Carter etc.  
 
2. Affiliate marketing (recommending others on platform and receiving remuneration) 
 
3. Pay Per Click (Google Ad Sense, and similar programs) 
 
4. Accept sponsored notes and articles, native advertising, advertorials. 
 
5. Generate leads for other companies 
 
6. Directory listings: Featured listing packages for suppliers for example fabric and 
manufacturing suppliers. Currently little or no competition for this, other than 
Ragtrader which caters to larger business  
 
7. Membership 
 
8. Investors (Corporate and Angel) 
 
9. Donations: gofundme.com

can receive preference from the lenders.

Appendices 
333 
Appendix O 
Costing challenge 
The designers were to be asked to find a solution to the problem within a 10-minute 
timeframe. The designers were then to be asked to address a similar problem and use the 
website to help find a solution. The example was to be ‘garment costing and cash flow 
projection’. This was chosen first because it is a fundamental business and entrepreneurial skill, 
often not readily learnt in fashion education programs and can mean success or failure at any 
stage of a business (Malem 2008). Second, it was used as an illustration in Project 1. And 
finally, it was also discussed by the fashion business colleagues. Again, the designers would 
only have 10 minutes to find the ‘answer’. The session would conclude with feedback on their 
user experience and reflection of any increased ability to solve problems with the help of the 
website, considering its functionality from initial enquiry to resolution. Their output would be 
tested by an independent auditor for accuracy and effectiveness (ability to reasonably make a 
profit).
